YOGINĪ TANTRA
ENGLISH WORKING TRANSLATION — FULL-VOLUME DRAFT
First Part: Chapters 1–19. Second Part: Chapters 1–9.
Prepared 1 November 2026.

READ THIS NOTE BEFORE CITING
This is an AI-assisted reading translation with numbered entries for all
2,807 verse units of the main Sanskrit work in the supplied Mishra edition,
plus opening invocations and the translator's closing verses. Chapter
coverage and sequential numbering have been checked. This is not a
critically established, independently reviewed, or publication-ready
translation. A numbered entry does not imply that every word in it is
secure: damaged readings, uncertain mantras, conflicting glosses, and
unresolved clauses are explicitly marked. Do not cite a tentative reading
as a settled interpretation without consulting the Sanskrit.

SCOPE
The Sanskrit main text is translated verse by verse. The accompanying Hindi
commentary is consulted as an aid, not duplicated as a separate full English
translation. The Hindi publisher's essays, advertisements, and blank leaves
are not part of the Sanskrit main work and are excluded.

SOURCE
Yogini Tantra with Translation by Kanhaiyalal Mishra, Lakshmi Venkateswara
Press, 1984, user-supplied 538-page PDF. Printed main-text pages 1–515
correspond to PDF pages 21–533, with pagination defects recorded in place.
Proper names and traditional distances generally retain the edition's
forms; this translation does not silently identify them with modern places.

RESTORED SOURCE GAP
The supplied PDF omits printed pages 400–401, containing Second Part 7.19–28.
These ten units were recovered from an alternate Mishra scan, printed
pages 400–401 (alternate PDF pages 419–420), translated, and individually
labelled as supplements:
https://archive.org/details/YoginiTantraKanhaiyyaLalMishra
The Shastri 1982 Sanskrit edition was consulted for comparison where noted;
its differing verse numbers are not silently substituted for Mishra's.

LIMITS OF LITERAL COVERAGE
First Part6.45 is a non-graphic historical summary of a sexual prescription
involving minors, not a literal translation. First Part6.44 and6.47 are
rendered non-graphically. These changes are explicitly marked. The file is
therefore not advertised as an entirely unabridged literal translation.
Other passages with unresolved readings retain explicit uncertainty rather
than invented text. Seed syllables without lexical meaning are retained
or described in their encoded form, not assigned fictitious English senses.

NAVIGATION
First Part uses headings such as11.18; Second Part usesII.7.19. Search for
a heading to locate a verse. Some sentences continue across numbered units.
The supplied edition's final chapter repeats the colophon numeral8; it is
identified as Second Part9 by its place in the sequence, with a note.

Yoginī Tantra: First Part, Chapter 1 — English working translation

Source and scope

Source: the user-supplied PDF, Yogini Tantra with Translation by Kanhaiyalal Mishra, Lakshmi Venkateswara Press, 1984. It contains Sanskrit verses and a Hindi translation/commentary. This English working translation follows the Sanskrit; the Hindi is consulted as a secondary aid. It is not a translation of every sentence of the Hindi commentary or the publisher's introductory essays.

This chapter file contains the two opening invocatory verses and First Part, Chapter 1, verses 1–56, on printed pages 1–12 (PDF pages 21–32). Other chapters are supplied as separate files and in the full-volume compilation. Chapter numbers and verse numbers refer to this edition. This is a provisional reading translation, not a critical edition or an independently reviewed scholarly translation.

Short notes distinguish uncertain wording, interpretive choices, and apparent printing problems. Square brackets supply an implied word or explain a term. The religious claims and imperatives below belong to the source text and its speakers.

Opening invocation (Maṅgalācaraṇa)

Invocation 1

May Mṛḍānī protect us: she whose radiance is that of a dark-blue rain cloud, who brings auspiciousness, who wears the crescent moon as her crest jewel, whose constant purpose is to quiet her devotees' suffering, and who continually bestows the treasure of spiritual knowledge. Resting upon Śambhu's breast, she arouses great wonder at the sight of the meeting of the heavenly river and the Kālindī.

Note: Mṛḍānī is a name of the Goddess; Śambhu is Śiva. The rivers are the Gaṅgā and Yamunā. Their meeting is a poetic comparison for the juxtaposition of the dark Goddess and pale Śiva.

Invocation 2

May this Yoginī Tantra, translated into pleasing words of our own language by Kanhaiyalal of the Miśra lineage, bring delight to the primordial Mother.

Note: This verse belongs to the translator's framing material: it refers to the Hindi translation, not to the English translation now being made.

First Part — Chapter 1

1.1 — Narrative introduction

Pārvatī questioned Śaṅkara, the Supreme Lord, who had ascended the summit of Kailāsa: the beloved of the mountain's daughter, whose banner bears the bull.

1.2 — The Goddess said

Blessed Lord, knower of every dharma, Lord whose nature is all knowledge, teacher of the world: tell me the Yoginī Tantra that you have mentioned.

1.3

Formerly you proclaimed its greatness at Śrīśaila and Mandara, at Vārāṇasī and Kāmarūpa, in Nepal and on Mount Mandara.

Note: The printed Sanskrit appears to name Mandara twice. This translation retains that repetition rather than silently replacing a place-name.

1.4 — Īśvara said

Listen, Goddess. I shall explain the excellent Yoginī Tantra, supremely purifying and blessed, whose distinctive fruit is liberation.

1.5

Beloved of my life, you and I must guard it carefully. Sweet-speaking one, act so that no other person obtains it.

1.6

Beautiful one, this Tantra is exceedingly difficult for gods and antigods to obtain. All the deities long to hear its unsurpassed truth.

1.7

Supreme Lady, I have not told it even to the Yakṣas and the others. Bound to you by the deepest affection, I tell it out of love for you.

1.8

I bow to that Mother of the universe, whose appearance is like a dark rain cloud, whose row of teeth shines like lightning and a line of white cranes; who is lovely with a succession of garlands of severed heads, whose hair is loose, and whose garment is space itself [who is naked].

Note: The Sanskrit compresses the lightning and crane comparisons into one compound; the division of the images is interpretive.

1.9

Her tongue lolls; her roar is terrible; her three eyes are red at their edges. Her face shines with the radiance of countless millions of moons.

Note: The Sanskrit says that the eyes are red at their edges; the Hindi gloss more broadly describes the eyes as red.

1.10

Her crown shines with the splendour of the amā lunar digit. Two corpses ornament her ears, and many kinds of jewels adorn her.

Note: Amā-kalā is a technical lunar expression associated with the dark or new-moon phase. Its precise symbolic force is retained rather than replaced with a modern astronomical description.

1.11

Her ear ornaments gleam with clusters of sunstones and moonstones. A girdle made from a thousand dead hands encircles her, and her face smiles.

1.12

Her face is striking with streams of blood flowing from the corners of her mouth. Her four arms are adorned with a sword, a severed head, and the gestures of granting boons and freedom from fear.

Note: The printed Sanskrit repeats the number 11 here, but the Hindi gloss identifies the verse as 12 and the following verse is 13. The sequential number 12 is used here.

1.13

She has prominent teeth; she is supreme and eternal; her body is adorned with blood. She mounts Śiva as a corpse and stands above Mahākāla.

1.14

Her left foot rests upon the corpse's heart; with her right she marks the world. She resembles ten million suns and illuminates all the worlds.

Note: The clause about the right foot, dakṣiṇe lokalāñchitām, is awkward in this printing. “With her right she marks the world” is provisional; the Hindi gloss understands the world as adorned by that foot.

1.15

She is resplendent with blazing matted locks, brilliant as a mass of lightning. The Goddess resembles a silver mountain; her form is like a mountain of crystal.

Note: The Sanskrit has feminine forms throughout, although this pale imagery follows the dark Goddess of verse 8. The tension is preserved rather than resolved by silently making Śiva the subject.

1.16

Clothed in space, exceedingly terrible, adorned with the moon and sun, she abounds in many ornaments, and the hair of her body shines like gold.

1.17

She sustains yogic sleep; her lotus-face smiles. She is continually absorbed in sexual union with Mahākāla, in the reversed position [with the Goddess above].

Note: The printed Sanskrit describes the Goddess in feminine forms. The Hindi gloss instead associates yogic sleep and the smiling face with Mahādeva. The English follows the Sanskrit grammar; this passage needs comparison with another edition before a final critical rendering.

1.18

Her great power manifests innumerable universes. She is surrounded by female jackals whose howls are terrible, and she appears at the dissolution of the cosmos.

Note: The first compound is densely printed and its segmentation is uncertain. This is a provisional contextual rendering. Śivā here means a female jackal, rather than the goddess Śivā.

1.19

The radiance of her nails puts countless millions of autumn moons to shame. She is resplendent amid Yoginīs who hold nectar-filled skulls in their hands—

Note: The compressed compound describing the skulls and hands admits uncertainty. The Hindi gloss handles the image differently, referring to nectar at heads and hands. The Sanskrit reading translated here should be checked against another edition.

1.20

—fearsome Yoginīs with reddened faces, intoxicated with rapture; and she is accompanied by Bhairavas of terrifying appearance, mighty roar, and fierce power—

1.21

—who carry corpses and skeletons, constantly cry out in victory, delight in dancing and instrumental music, and are clothed in space.

Note: The word for instrumental music is read from the printed page as vādana; the OCR distorts it.

1.22

She stands in the midst of the cremation ground, attended by Brahmā and the other gods.

Note: Verses 8–22 form an extended description governed by “I bow” in verse 8. They are separated here to preserve the printed numbering.

1.23

Now listen, Queen of the gods, to the exceedingly rare King of Tantras. Out of affection for you I explain it; it must never be disclosed. Beloved, bound by the deepest love and devotion, I am your servant.

1.24

This teaching is rooted in the guru; this universe is rooted in the guru. The guru alone is supreme Brahman; the guru is Śiva himself. The gods bow to the person who has won the guru over.

1.25

If someone drinks, regarding it as nectar, the water used to wash the guru's feet—even feet ulcerated by leprosy—that person will go to the city of the Goddess.

Note: “Ulcerated by leprosy” translates the source's explicit image; the Hindi gloss softens it to serious illness. This is a statement of the text's ritual theology.

1.26

Even if someone drinks liquor that has not been ritually consecrated, under the force of the guru's command, no expiation is required for it. This principle is established even in the Veda.

1.27

Even if the guru states a personal teaching contrary to the Tantra, one should regard that pronouncement as comparable to the Veda and the words of Mahārudra. Everything should be done at the guru's command, without [independent] comprehension of the principle.

Note: The closing expression tattvasyāgamanaṃ vinā is difficult in this printing. The final clause is provisional; the Hindi gloss takes it as the guru's authority applying even without a textual or doctrinal basis.

1.28

The divine majesty is nondual; there is no duality with the guru. [The next clause cannot be translated securely from this printing.] There is no equal [to the guru] on earth.

Textual caution: This is among the least secure verses in the chapter. The printed wording and the Hindi explanation do not yield an entirely consistent statement. The difficult clause reads nādvaitaṃ plavate kāryam in this printing; its intended syntax and meaning remain unresolved here. It is left marked rather than assigned a conjectural meaning. Comparison with Shastri's edition (1982, printed p.4/PDF68, verses31–32) does not resolve the passage: it prints nādvaitaṃ guruṇā saha, “not nonduality with the guru,” in contrast to the apparent na dvaitaṃ of the Mishra reading, “no duality.” The doctrinally opposite readings must not be conflated. This verse remains unresolved and should not be cited as a settled translation.

1.29

Beloved, the guru is the refuge, the guru is the god, and the guru is the Goddess, in heaven, in the mortal world, and in the world of the Nāgas.

1.30

Whether the guru knows little or possesses extensive knowledge, the guru remains the refuge. The guru's sons should be treated like the guru, and so should their children and descendants.

1.31

Great Lady, the guru's wife is herself the guru: there is no doubt. Goddess, the leftovers of the guru's son should be eaten just as the guru's leftovers are. There is no uncertainty about this; acting otherwise leads to a fall.

Note: Ucchiṣṭa means food left after eating, with connotations of contact and ritual impurity that this passage deliberately reverses.

1.32

Great Goddess, the guru's leftovers are exceedingly difficult even for Brahmā and the other gods to obtain. They are declared supremely purifying, higher than the highest.

1.33

Beloved, someone who lives for twenty years eating even a handful of food left by the guru, the guru's wife, or the guru's son becomes long-lived, free from old age and disease, and then becomes Śiva.

Note: The syntax of the printed first line is uneven; the relation to leftover food is clarified by the surrounding verses and Hindi explanation.

1.34

Excellent one, if someone lives near the guru for fifty years, endowed with Bhairava observance and serving the guru's feet—

1.35

—after enjoying excellent pleasures here, that heroic practitioner joins the company of the gods. He sports with the companies of Rudra's daughters, endowed with beauty and youth, for as long as the moon, sun, and stars endure.

1.36

Beautiful one, a mortal who rises in the morning and duly bows to the guru, or to the guru's son or daughter, attains accomplishment. There should be no doubt about this.

1.37

Each morning a person should face the direction in which the guru's dwelling lies and bow with devotion, directing the salutation toward the guru, his wife, his sons, and his daughters. Such a person is accomplished; there is no doubt.

1.38

The guru's dwelling is Kailāsa, and the house there is made of wish-fulfilling jewels. Its rows of trees are wish-fulfilling trees; its creepers are wish-fulfilling creepers. Its water basin is the heavenly Gaṅgā. Auspicious one, everything there is filled with holiness.

1.39

The female servants in the guru's house are proclaimed to be Bhairavīs. A discerning person should always contemplate the male servants as forms of Bhairava.

1.40

Great Goddess, whoever has circumambulated the guru's dwelling has thereby circumambulated the earth with its seven continents.

1.41 — The Goddess said

Great Lord, compassionate one, tell me: who is the guru whom you have described as even greater than the principle [of ultimate reality]?

Note: The Sanskrit reads tattvād adhika, literally “greater than tattva.” The Hindi interprets it as greater than knowledge of that principle.

1.42 — Īśvara said

Great Goddess, the one known as Ādinātha, Mahākāla, is that guru. Queen of the gods, he is now the supreme [guru] in all mantras.

1.43

In the Śaiva, Śākta, Vaiṣṇava, Gāṇapatya, Aindava, Mahāśaiva, and Saura [traditions], he is the guru; there is no doubt. Supreme Lady, he alone is the speaker of the mantra, and no other.

Note: Aindava is retained as the printed sectarian designation, rather than silently replaced by a better-known list of traditions.

1.44

Daughter of the mountain, at the time of imparting a mantra, the human [guru] becomes the seat of that Mahākāla. Śaṅkarī, this state of being guru is therefore beyond the merely human; there is no doubt.

1.45

Goddess, the mantra-giving guru brings about in the disciple's head-lotus that same knowledge which the guru realizes in the lotus of his own head.

1.46

Therefore, Great Lady, the guru is remembered as one alone. Great Goddess, the human [teacher] becomes his seat. Śaṅkarī, his greatness has been proclaimed in all the scriptures.

Note: “His seat” refers back to Mahākāla in verse 44. This distinguishes the divine guru from the human teacher through whom he is present.

1.47

I shall explain further the greatness relating to the guru. In the imparting of a paśu mantra, the prescribed measure extends to ten generations.

1.48

In the imparting of a vīra mantra it extends to twenty-five generations; for all the Mahāvidyās it is understood to extend to fifty generations.

1.49

In the imparting of Brahmayoga, the measure extends to a hundred generations. Great Goddess, Brahmayoga has been proclaimed in the Bheruṇḍā [Tantra].

Note on 47–49: The Sanskrit gives daśapauruṣī, pañcaviṃśatipauruṣī, pañcāśatpauruṣī and śatapauruṣī as measures of maryādā. “Generations” gives the ordinary genealogical sense of pauruṣī, but the precise scope of the rule is not explained here; the Hindi largely repeats the terminology. These verses should not be expanded into a definite rule about family eligibility or the number of authorized initiations without further evidence. Paśu and vīra are technical classes of practitioner; Mahāvidyā names the great Goddess-mantras or their deities.

1.50

The water from the guru's feet is holier than bathing at all the sacred fords. Whatever fruit a person would obtain by bathing at all those pilgrimage places—

1.51

—a mortal obtains that fruit from a single drop of the guru's foot-water. Whoever performs an ablution with it has bathed at all the sacred fords.

1.52

When drunk, it purifies one from every sin. Above all, Mahāmāyā, one immediately attains the state of Śiva.

1.53

A person who bears the dust of the guru's feet upon the head is released from all sins and becomes Śiva. There is no doubt.

1.54

Goddess, whoever makes a forehead mark with that same dust becomes four-armed, the Lord of Vaikuṇṭha. There is no doubt.

Note: The Lord of Vaikuṇṭha is Viṣṇu.

1.55

Whoever has eaten that dust even on a single day obtains the merit arising from ten million sacrifices. There is no doubt.

1.56

Thus, Goddess, I have told you the secret concerning the guru. It must be carefully guarded, like the intimate affairs of one's own lineage.

Note: The final expression svakīyaṃ kulapauruṣam is not completely transparent. The translation gives its likely force as an injunction to protect lineage intimacy or honour; the Hindi retains the Sanskrit expression without resolving it.

Colophon

Thus ends the first chapter in the dialogue between the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among all Tantras, described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses], with the vernacular commentary.

Note: The twenty-four-thousand designation is the edition's traditional colophon, not a count verified for this surviving printed text.

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 2
English working translation

Source: Kanhaiyalal Mishra edition, Lakshmi Venkateswara Press, 1984; printed pages 13–27, PDF pages 33–47. Sanskrit translated with consultation of the accompanying Hindi gloss. The numbering follows this edition. Unclear readings are marked; this draft has not undergone independent scholarly review. Statements about ritual efficacy are the source's claims. Technical terms and ambiguities are retained where the source does not support a secure English equivalent.

2.1 — The Goddess said:
Embodiment of supreme bliss, teacher of the moving and unmoving world, I have heard from you the greatness of the guru, more secret than the secret.

2.2
Now I wish to hear about Kālī, who delivers everyone. In the Yāmala she is called the Great Vidyā and the Vidyā of accomplishment.

2.3
[She is] the exceedingly great knowledge of Brahman, treated in the Cāmuṇḍā Tantra. Śiva, graciously explain the secret of Mahākālī.

2.4 — Īśvara said:
Kālikā is regarded as the supreme and exceedingly great knowledge of Brahman. Having attained her, even the lowest person reaches liberation, nirvāṇa.

2.5
Goddess, the secret is being told. Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva and the others, and likewise all the worlds, are her worshippers—

2.6
—and through Kālikā's grace all partake of liberation and the other blessings. Having repeated the secret [mantra] a thousand times, or by the ten million—

2.7
—the person devoted to Kālī's practice becomes fortunate. All the scriptures establish Kālī as the Mother of the worlds.

Note on 2.5–7: The syntax runs across the verse divisions. “Secret [mantra]” is supplied from the context and Hindi explanation. The exact construction of the repetition phrase needs collation.

2.8
Whoever repeats Kālī's mantra is Kālī's child: there is no doubt.

2.9
You might abandon even this exalted person; at some time you might abandon your own form, all-pervading Goddess. But Kālī's Vidyā, once attained, can never abandon [that person].

Note: The pronoun construction is strained in this edition. This follows the contrast understood by the Hindi gloss, while preserving the claim that Kālī's Vidyā does not abandon its recipient.

2.10
The Śūdra's status as Śūdra departs, and the Brahmin's status as Brahmin: merely by receiving the mantra, all become equal to Śiva.

2.11
Having reached Vārāṇasī and likewise the Gaṅgā, through the bestowal of Kālī's mantra they all become equal to Śiva.

Note: The relationship between visiting the holy places and receiving the mantra is compressed. The Hindi understands initiation at Vārāṇasī or beside the Gaṅgā.

2.12
If a woman becomes like you, or a man like me, blessed is that person's mother, and that person's father is foremost among the gods.

2.13
The ancestors of the person who, through good fortune, has taken refuge in you as Kālī go to heaven, which is exceedingly difficult to attain.

2.14
The ancestors of virtuous people hope: “When a child in our lineage takes refuge in Kālī's mantra, we shall reach the city of liberation and rejoice there forever.”

2.15
Kālī, Tārā, Chinnā, the guru, and the ruler should be understood as one in nature. Through perceiving division [between them], one goes to hell.

Note: Chinnā is Chinnamastā. The inclusion of the ruler is explicit in the printed text.

2.16
A disciple of Tārā should set aside Kālī; a disciple of Kālī should set aside Tāriṇī. Worship of Chinnā and Mahiṣamardinī is prescribed on particular occasions.

Note: “Set aside” concerns the choice of ritual worship here, not an assertion of ultimate difference; the following verses qualify it.

2.17
Or, Goddess, if one worships [another], one should not worship the other deities [as separate]. Auspicious one, one should worship that other [Goddess] contemplating her as Kālī.

Note: The negative construction in the printed second pāda is uncertain in the OCR. This rendering follows the sense of the Hindi gloss and the next verse; the precise wording requires checking.

2.18
The supreme Vidyā who is Kālī is herself Tārā: there is no doubt. From the differentiated conception of these two arise the various mantras. I have explained that to you in the Kālikā Kalpa and the Tārā Kalpa.

2.19 — The Goddess said:
Lord of the gods, sweet-speaking one, explain the various procedures. Above all, Mahādeva, explain the secret of mantra repetition.

2.20 — Īśvara said:
The garland of letters is called auspicious, illuminating all mantras. Goddess, its auspicious representative is the rosary made of mahāśaṅkha.

Note: Varṇamālā means both the alphabet and a “garland of letters.” Mahāśaṅkha literally means “great conch,” but in tantric contexts can denote human cranial bone. It is retained as a technical material here rather than silently reduced to ordinary shell.

2.21
Accomplishment is not far from the person who holds a mahāśaṅkha rosary. If it is unavailable, you who are honoured by the heroes, a crystal rosary grants every accomplishment.

2.22
Great Goddess, I shall tell you the number of beads: with twenty-five comes liberation; twenty-seven bestow flourishing.

2.23
Thirty bring attainment of wealth; fifty are for accomplishment of the mantra. Great Lady, with one hundred and eight comes every accomplishment.

2.24 — The Goddess said:
You have stated the general rule. Tell me the particular rule for those seeking specific results.

2.25 — Śiva said:
A rosary of teeth should be used for repetition; worn at the neck, it is auspicious. Beloved, if the mantra count is to be made with teeth—

2.26
—the rosary bestows every accomplishment when a principal tooth forms its meru bead. Queen of the gods, the same is prescribed concerning the meru in other [rosaries].

Note: Rājadanta is translated provisionally as “principal tooth.” Meru is the rosary's marker or summit bead. The precise dental identification is not established by the passage.

2.27
Great Lady, listen to the counts used in the declaration of intention, in repetition, and in fire-offering. I shall explain them in order.

2.28
One hundred, one thousand, ten thousand, one hundred thousand, and ten million: Great Lady, these are the counts to be reckoned generally.

2.29
Where a special rule applies, Great Lady, follow that special rule. For each specified count beginning with a hundred, repeat eight more.

Note: The Hindi illustrates this as 108 instead of 100. The wording alone does not settle whether every higher count should receive a fixed addition of eight or a proportional increase; no additional rule is invented here.

2.30
Leaving aside the first and last joints, repeat using the eight joints. Auspicious one, at the end of the repetition, then hold the rosary [the last phrase is uncertain].

Note: The OCR gives an unclear expression following mālāṃ. The Hindi gloss interprets it as holding Śiva's rosary. This clause needs comparison with the printed page or another edition.

2.31
With red flowers, an arghya offering, the seed syllable, and the accompaniment of ghaṭa music, the discerning practitioner should offer the fruit of the repetition to the Goddess.

Note: The compound concerning the offering and music is difficult; the Hindi reads ghaṭa as a vessel used in accompaniment. The exact procedure is not reconstructed beyond that evidence.

2.32
Queen of the gods, the secret of repetition, with its principal and subsidiary elements, is explained in the Sarasvatī Tantra. Learn it from there, beloved.

2.33
Great Lady, Supreme Lady, hear about the finger rosary according to the order of Śiva and Śakti, for the accomplishment of all mantras.

2.34
Beginning with the middle joint of the ring finger, and proceeding in the order of the little finger and the others, one should repeat over ten joints up to the base of the neighbouring finger.

Note: The neighbouring finger is understood as the index finger in the Hindi explanation. The passage describes a conventional finger-counting scheme whose exact route is not fully specified by this sentence alone.

2.35
The base of the middle finger is to be treated as meru. Goddess, when repeating the count of one hundred and eight, omit the first and last [joints]. This is called the Śiva rosary. Now hear about the Śakti rosary.

2.36
Beginning with the middle joint of the ring finger, in the order of the little finger and the others, one should repeat over ten joints as far as the base of the index finger.

2.37
Supreme Lady, know the two middle joints [of the index finger] as meru; Queen of the gods, never touch those two.

Note: The relation between madhyamādvitayaṃ parva and tarjanyāḥ is grammatically compressed. The bracketed identification is provisional, and the Hindi gloss is itself ambiguous. The two counting systems should not be treated as reconstructed diagrams on this evidence alone.

2.38
For the repetition of one hundred and eight, omit the first and last joints. Perform daily repetition on the hand, but never repetition for a desired result. Yet, my beloved, when a rosary is unavailable, repetition for a desired result may also be performed on the hand.

2.39
Repetition connected with daily duties is called daily repetition. Bathing, libation, fire-offering, bali offering, and satisfaction [of the recipients] are daily observances.

2.40
With all rosaries, repeat in forward and reverse order. With the finger rosary, repeat only in forward order.

2.41
Goddess, repeat the [male-deity] mantra with a rosary belonging to Śiva; repeat a Śakti mantra with a rosary belonging to Śakti.

Note: The beginning of the first pāda is defective in the OCR. The Hindi reads puṃmantra, “male mantra,” in contrast to śaktimantra in the second half.

2.42
Goddess, repeat the lunar mantra and likewise [the mantra of] the Mother of the Veda. Repeat Sāvitrī on the hand with the Śiva rosary.

2.43
Goddess, repeat the lunar mantra on the hand or with the Śakti rosary. For repeating Sāvitrī, the finger rosary is always approved; crystal, pearl, kuśa-grass, and conch rosaries are also approved.

Note on 2.42–43: These lines are repetitive in the edition and their verse division is uneven. Sāvitrī denotes the Vedic solar formula commonly called the Gāyatrī.

2.44
For Vaiṣṇava [repetition], a tulasī rosary; for Gaṇeśa, elephant tusk. For repeating [the mantra of] Tripurā, rudrākṣa or red sandalwood is approved.

2.45
For repeating [the mantra of] Dhūmāvatī, [use] seeds of datura grown in a cremation ground. A rosary of finger-joints, well strung with sinew—

2.46
—is approved for Bagalāmukhī, Great Lady: truly, truly. Without a declaration of intention, or with a count below or above [the prescribed count], one does not obtain the full result; therefore one should observe the rule.

Note: The unusual materials belong to the text's ritual prescriptions. Karaparva is taken as finger-joint material, and nāḍī as a bodily cord or sinew, in this context.

2.47
[A practitioner should take] a copper vessel containing dūrvā grass and sesame, filled with water—

2.48
—and kuśa grass and fruit; Goddess, having taken it and performed the prescribed sipping of water, one should worship the compassionate guru in the lotus of the head.

2.49
Facing the direction of the Yakṣas [north] or the direction of the lord of the gods [east], [one should state] the month, fortnight, lunar day, and the divine festival or calendrical division—

2.50
—the beginning and ending times, the lineage name and personal name of the person seeking the result, and say: “Today I shall perform these rites.” Then one should pour out water toward the northeast.

2.51
Lunar and solar [reckoning apply] generally; lunar reckoning applies when a lunar day is specified. Lunar reckoning is generally primary, but secondary for fierce rites.

2.52
Beloved, for lending, for giving, and for observances such as Prauṣṭhapadī, a sidereal month is prescribed; for annual observances, a sāvana month.

Note: The opening term is read as ṛṇadāna, lending or advancing a loan. These are distinct traditional calendrical measures; sāvana here does not mean merely the named month Śrāvaṇa.

2.53
Thus it has been declared from age to age; in the Kali age solar [reckoning] is the essential rule generally. Initiation is auspicious in a solar month, not a lunar or sidereal month—

2.54
—nor in a sāvana month, Great Lady, for [otherwise] it would be fruitless. Initiation involving Vedic ritual action is approved even in a lunar month.

Note: The phrase kriyāvatī vedamayī appears to qualify the kind of initiation. The Hindi gloss instead speaks more generally of Vedic ritual action. The Sanskrit construction is followed provisionally.

2.55
Everything auspicious [is performed] in the bright fortnight; the opposite of auspicious [belongs] to the other fortnight. Whoever desires the proper result should perform rites from morning until midday.

2.56
Supreme Lady, fierce rites may also be performed in the remaining [part of the day]. After the first watch has passed, until the third watch—

2.57
—night is prescribed as the time for repetition. Now hear the time for worship. When half a watch of night has passed, and while half a watch remains, the interval for worship is a watch, always bestowing the four aims of life.

Note on 2.56–57: The transition and timing are compressed, and the boundaries of the worship interval are not secure in this edition's wording. The traditional units are preserved; no exact modern clock times are inferred. The four aims are dharma, prosperity, desire, and liberation.

2.58
The two ghaṭikās remaining in the middle watches of the night are designated the Great Night. An act performed then is imperishable.

Note: “The middle watches” and the exact placement of these two ghaṭikās require further checking; the Hindi paraphrase does not settle them.

2.59
Whatever is repeated, whatever is offered into fire, whatever is done as a means to liberation: all of it becomes imperishable and leads to an infinite result.

2.60
Not at night [should one perform] Vaiṣṇava, Saura, Mahāsaura, or ancestral rites. Except at midday, Goddess, except during a lunar eclipse, initiation should be carefully performed, without distinction of the bright fortnight [and the other fortnight].

Textual caution: The arrangement of the exceptions and negatives is uncertain. This is a tentative syntactic rendering, not a settled rule. The Hindi gloss reads the midday and eclipse expressions as exceptions but does not fully remove the ambiguity. Saura and Mahāsaura concern solar worship.

2.61
One desiring liberation [should act] in the dark fortnight; one desiring enjoyment in the bright. One desiring prosperity should begin from the fifth day of the dark fortnight.

Note: The OCR repeats muktikāma in the first two clauses, but the contrast and Hindi gloss support muktikāma/bhuktikāma, liberation/enjoyment.

2.62
One should do an auspicious rite at an auspicious time; one in distress may also act at an inauspicious time. At an eclipse or a great pilgrimage place there is no defect arising from timing.

2.63
In Vārāṇasī especially, one may perform everything at any time. There it is always the Kṛta age, and always the northward course of the sun.

2.64
There is no distinction between daytime, night, twilight, and the great midnight. [The next statement concerning fire and embodiment is unclear.] Auspicious one, when does the conjunction of accomplishment fail to arise in Kāśī?

Note: The rhetoric of the final question means that the auspicious conjunction is always present. The preceding sentence cannot be securely recovered from the OCR and Hindi explanation and is not supplied conjecturally.

2.65
Kāśī [extends] two or three krośas, within the five-krośa circuit. Goddess, in length and breadth it is eternal and ever auspicious.

Note: The dimensional wording is awkward and should not be used as a precise surveyed boundary. A krośa is a traditional measure of distance, not a uniform modern unit.

2.66
Auspicious one, this city of nirvāṇa is made of the supreme light. There [is established] the universe with its summit, things, and human beings.

Textual caution: The verb and the expression sakūṭaṃ vastu mānavam are uncertain. This provisional rendering follows the general cosmological sense of the Hindi; the exact meaning requires collation.

2.67
By wandering there, Goddess, a person may attain nirvāṇa. Even at the cost of all one's possessions, one should satisfy the Brahmins in Vārāṇasī.

2.68
Giving to a Brahmin in Vārāṇasī, delight in Brahmayoga, and engaging in action without desire [for its reward]: all are causes of nirvāṇa.

2.69
In fields of the sages such as the Gaṅgā, [liberation comes] through knowledge and yoga. But Kāśī [makes] the dead pure and liberated through my instruction—

2.70
—for my dwelling is not elsewhere, and without Kāśī [there is] no liberation. Whatever act is done there bestows an infinite result.

Note on 2.69–70: These are the speaker's sectarian praise of Kāśī. The first part of 2.70 is poorly recognized by the OCR; the translation follows the Hindi gloss provisionally.

2.71
Everything becomes imperishable; here one obtains the desired accomplishment. There is the merit arising from that conjunction, and there one is released.

2.72
There I nourish [the practitioner] in the form of that reality, and not otherwise. If the time of the lunar day is short and the rite's duration extends beyond it, begin at the proper time and complete it even outside that time.

Note: The first sentence's object is supplied. The closing rule is much clearer than the opening expression.

2.73
If twilight arrives [during the rite], repeat Gāyatrī ten times; then perform the twilight observance appropriate to the time and complete the rite.

2.74
Thus, daughter of the mountain, I have explained what you asked. Tell me whatever further question is in your mind.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the second chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 74 numbered verse units have English entries. Several readings remain provisional, as flagged above. Unresolved clauses have not been silently invented. This is a draft translation, not a fully collated edition.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 3
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition, 1984, printed pages 27–39, PDF pages 47–59. Sanskrit translated with the accompanying Hindi consulted as a secondary aid. The source's religious claims and commands are rendered as historical text. Some mantric syllables have no lexical English meaning; these are retained or identified rather than assigned invented meanings. Uncertain readings are explicitly marked.

3.1 — The Goddess said:
Blessed Lord, lord of the Pramathas, God of gods, teacher of the world: [tell me about] averting warfare and averting fever and the other afflictions—

3.2
—how may this be accomplished swiftly? Lord, explain with the greatest kindness. Supreme Lord, apart from you the world has no swift protector.

3.3 — Īśvara said:
Daughter of the mountain, out of affection for you I shall explain a great protective armour that swiftly averts warfare, fever, and the other afflictions. I shall tell it in ordinary speech: listen.

Note: Kavaca, “armour,” denotes a protective invocation. Prākṛtena vākyena may mean ordinary or vernacular wording; it does not by itself securely identify a particular Prakrit language here.

3.4 — The protective invocation:
Oṃ, homage to the blessed Goddess Vajraśṛṅkhalā [She of the Thunderbolt Chain]. Strike, devour, consume! Ah, drink blood with the skull-bowl, red-eyed one, red-robed one, ash-eyed one, whose body is smeared with ashes, adorned with masses of thunderbolt weapons! Bind the eastern direction; bind the southern direction; bind the western direction. [The next words concerning the Nāgas, wealth, or the northern direction are uncertain.] Bind the lords of the grahas; bind the lord of the Nāgas. Bind Yakṣas, Rākṣasas, and Piśācas. Protect [me] from Pretas, Bhūtas, Gandharvas, and whatever other [beings] there may be. Protect above; protect below. Bind [svanikā: uncertain]. O you of the great force of water, come, come! [Loṭi-loṣṭi; śatāvali: uncertain mantric expressions.] Thunderbolt flame, mass of thunderbolts! Hūṃ phaṭ; hrīṃ hrīṃ śrīṃ phaṭ; hūṃ hūṃ phūṃ phaṃ. From all grahas, from all harmful disturbances, hrīṃ, from all the rest, protect me!

Textual note: This mantra is not regular metrical Sanskrit and mixes imperative forms. The printed page was checked because the OCR corrupts several phrases. The uncertain expressions above are not securely translated; a reliable liturgical transcription would require comparison with another witness. Graha may denote a seizing spirit or planetary power, not merely a physical planet. Nāgas, Yakṣas, Rākṣasas, Piśācas, Pretas, Bhūtas, and Gandharvas are categories of supernatural beings. No English semantic equivalents are invented for the seed syllables.

3.5
Goddess, this armour is exceedingly difficult for gods and antigods to obtain. It should be employed against grahas, fever, and spirits, and in all undertakings.

3.6
This armour that has issued from my mouth must not be given indiscriminately. If it is so given, accomplishment is lost, and one becomes a sacrificial creature of the Yoginīs.

3.7
It should be given to a peaceful, heroic yogin of good lineage, devoted to good conduct, who has conquered all enemies.

3.8 — The Goddess said:
I have heard the divine armour that came forth from your lotus-mouth. Now I wish to hear the supreme [means] that brings the world under control.

3.9 — Īśvara said:
Listen, Goddess. I shall explain the great [armour] that enchants the world. Formerly Nārada questioned me on the summit of Kailāsa, and I told him the armour that enchants all.

3.10
With that armour Nārada, Brahmā's son, fond of quarrels, penetrated and enchanted the three worlds.

3.11
Seeing that extraordinary deed, Viṣṇu said to the Creator's son: “Sage, tell me how all this has been enchanted; explain its cause.”

3.12
All of it came to Viṣṇu, and Viṣṇu told the daughter of the ocean. Wise Nārada, devoted to the welfare of all worlds, had questioned Mahādeva, the world's teacher, seated on Kailāsa's summit.

Note: The daughter of the ocean is Lakṣmī. The verse moves back to Nārada's earlier question; that narrative return is retained.

3.13 — Nārada said:
Lord, describe the great Mahāvidyā Kālikā. God, what is its fruit? What is this enchantment? Śaṅkara, tell me by what means protection is obtained in battle.

3.14 — Īśvara said:
Goddess, it was hidden during the three ages and revealed in the Kali age. Kālī, the Goddess clothed in space, enchants the universe. Foremost sage, hear this enchantment of the three worlds.

Note: The address “Goddess” occurs within the embedded dialogue with Nārada in this edition; it has not been silently altered.

3.15 — Ritual identification:
For this [invocation], Kālabhairava is the seer; anuṣṭubh is the metre; Śmaśānakālī [Kālī of the cremation ground] is the deity; its application is enchantment everywhere.

3.16 — Invocation:
“[Piṃ …: the opening seed sequence is uncertain], svāhā! May [she] always protect me in dispute. Krīṃ, to the deity Dakṣiṇakālikā, giver of victory in the assembly—

3.17
—hrīṃ hrīṃ, dark-limbed one, kill the enemy, kill! Krīṃ krīṃ, bring the three worlds under control. Hrīṃ hrīṃ krīṃ, protect me, protect me, in disputes and in the ruler's house, supreme twenty-two-syllabled [Vidyā]!

3.18
Always protect me on every side from Brahmarākṣasas and Vetālas. Wherever the armour has left [a part] uncovered, may Kālikā protect me there. Goddess, whose form is that of my mother, protect me everywhere.”

Note on 3.16–18: The opening seed sequence is uncertain even on the printed page; it is not reconstructed from OCR. The readable seed syllables remain untranslated. The imperative māraya is rendered literally as “kill.” The twenty-two-syllable designation is the text's description, not a count verified from the corrupt formula. These entries translate the lexical material but do not constitute a verified liturgical transcription.

3.19
Thus this supreme enchantment has been declared through your good fortune.

3.20
Whoever continually recites it brings the three worlds under control.

3.21
Whoever worships the heroic beloved [Goddess] without knowing this armour is continually afflicted by great disease: there is no doubt. Nārada, I have told you that such a person becomes sick and short-lived.

3.22
This armour is to be worn, especially [written] on birch bark. By wearing the armour together with its mantra, the desired accomplishment arises.

3.23
The mantra practitioner should write it on the eighth day of the bright fortnight and wear it in a gold casing. Great sage, I cannot express the full greatness of this armour.

Note: The verse number is repeated as 22 in the OCR; the gloss and sequence support 23.

3.24
A yogin should wear it at the topknot; one seeking a result, on the right arm. Goddess, this is a wish-fulfilling tree, revealed through affection for you. Great sage, it must be carefully concealed and recited.

Note: The shifting addresses, “Goddess” and “great sage,” are present in the transmitted dialogue.

3.25 — Viṣṇu said:
Mahālakṣmī, accept this perpetual armour. The moving and unmoving three worlds will certainly come under your control.

3.26
Śiva formerly taught it to Nārada, who desired its fruit. By reciting it, Nārada enchanted the moving and unmoving world.

3.27 — The Goddess said:
Ancient sage, honoured by the world, lord of the Pramathas, giver of boons: explain a method in full for the benefit of human beings—

3.28
—by which one swiftly obtains a kingdom, a child, knowledge, wealth, fame, and everything else.

Note: Yoga here denotes a method or ritual combination, rather than specifically bodily postures.

3.29 — Īśvara said:
Goddess, listen: I shall explain what you ask me. It is described at length in the Phetkāriṇī Tantra and the Nīla Tantra.

3.30
Now I shall explain it fully, Goddess of pure smile. The sun might rise in the west, the moon fall to earth, and the ocean dry up, but [this teaching] will never be false.

3.31
Great Lady, this king of methods is always unfailing.

3.32
As Viṣṇu's discus, my trident, and the king of the gods' thunderbolt are unfailing, so is the method I have explained.

3.33
As surely as Brahmā's lotus seat is established, so surely is this method established, Goddess: there is no doubt.

3.34
As the wish-fulfilling tree fulfils desires, Goddess, so is this excellent method declared to fulfil them.

3.35
For a kingdom, for the lineage, or for a child, [use] a gold sheet measuring sixteen finger-breadths in length and breadth—

3.36
—for sacrifice, wealth, or fame, an auspicious silver [sheet]—

3.37
—likewise [of that] measurement, Goddess; on copper for destruction. Supreme Lady, for other purposes [write] on gold or birch bark—

Textual note: The Hindi gloss understands copper as for honour or standing, whereas the extracted Sanskrit reads vināśane, “for destruction.” This translation records the apparent Sanskrit reading provisionally. The opening measurement phrase also needs collation.

3.38
—the mantra of Tāriṇī that bestows every accomplishment. Beautiful one, a seeker of a kingdom, wealth, a child, or fame—

3.39
—or prosperity should write it beautifully with a pen—

3.40
—a gold stylus eight finger-breadths long, of the thickness of the little finger. For knowledge [write] with the root of kuśa grass; for other purposes with dūrvā grass.

3.41
Having performed ritual sipping, Goddess, and bowed to the guru's sandals, face north and worship Tāriṇī.

3.42
[Take] kuṅkuma, rocanā, spikenard, sandalwood, lac, musk, Kashmir saffron, and vermilion, beautiful-faced one.

3.43
Mix them together and first draw a six-pointed diagram. In its centre write the supreme [mantra of] Tārā, consisting of four and a half syllables—

3.44
—or five and a half syllables, absorbed in her. Then write the object sought in the centre of the altar. Beloved of Śambhu, hear what that object is:

3.45
“Bring such-and-such a person's such-and-such speech under control; do, do!”

Note: The Hindi gloss additionally gives an example concerning knowledge, which is not clearly present as a separate Sanskrit line here. It is not inserted into the Sanskrit translation.

3.46
“Produce, produce a good child for such-and-such [women]; give, give such-and-such wealth to such-and-such a person,” beloved.

3.47
Having carefully written the desired object in this order, write the six long letters, excluding the neuter letters, in the six points.

Note: “Neuter letters” is a technical classification of phonemes. The passage does not list them here; a list is not supplied conjecturally.

3.48
[Draw] a circle and a beautiful, clearly delineated eight-petalled lotus. Draw its eight petals with paired filaments.

3.49
Then write on the eight petals the eight letters that will be stated: Vāgbhava, Bhuvaneśānī, Kāma, hūṃ, and praṇava—

3.50
—then the Māyā mantra and svāhā, beginning in the east and proceeding in order. Thus draw the diagram, square and furnished with four gates.

Note: Several items are names of seed syllables; svāhā itself has two syllables. The text's “eight” should not be assumed to mean eight separately named deities. Praṇava is oṃ; the exact expansion of the remaining encoded syllables is not reconstructed here.

3.51
For knowledge, accomplishments, and other purposes, at an auspicious amṛtodaya time, on Thursday, Friday, Monday, Tuesday, or Wednesday, under a favourable star, repeat the mantra with concentrated mind.

Note: Amṛtodaya is a technical timing term. Tārā here is understood as the favourable natal or lunar-star relation, following the Hindi, rather than the Goddess's name.

3.52
Enclose it in yellow cloth, surround it with lac, and let the excellent practitioner bind it in red silk.

Note: The word read as jantunā is glossed as lac in the Hindi; that identification is provisional.

3.53
The skilled practitioner should place it on a gold seat and undertake the prescribed count. Do not let it touch the ground or consecrate it with discarded offerings; never use it when torn or when someone has stepped over it.

3.54
Goddess, carefully make a vessel sixteen finger-breadths in height and breadth, pleasing to everyone's sight.

3.55
A seeker of a kingdom should make the vessel of gold; one seeking a child, of silver; one concerned with battle, of copper; for other purposes, of clay.

3.56
Place pearls, coral, a jewel, silver, gold, and grain inside it, and furnish its mouth with leafy shoots.

Note: The Hindi specifies five leafy shoots; the visible Sanskrit excerpt says pallavaiḥ, “with shoots,” without an explicit five.

3.57
The attentive, discerning practitioner should cover it with a pair of red cloths. On a gold sheet eight finger-breadths square—

3.58
—write the mantra in this manner and carefully place it in the vessel. Worship the supreme auspicious Tārā with sixty-four services.

3.59
At a prepared fire-offering place measuring twenty-four finger-breadths, [form] a lotus flower adorned with sixteen petals—

3.60
—and filaments, Goddess. Taking a bali offering as before, the knower of mantra should present it with great devotion using the bali mantra.

3.61
Having bathed [the object] with the five nectars and the five products of the cow, worship it. The excellent practitioner should make one thousand and eight fire-offerings and place flowers and unbroken rice over that enclosure, Queen of the gods.

Note: Pañcāmṛta and pañcagavya are distinct traditional ritual mixtures. The referent of “enclosure,” puṭa, is not wholly clear in the passage.

3.62
A seeker who desires a kingdom should give land measuring a village. Great Lady, a seeker of battle [should give] as the ritual fee two gold horses.

Note: The relation of the fee and horses is compressed in the Sanskrit; the Hindi understands a pair of horses made of gold. No value or weight is specified.

3.63
For accomplishment in knowledge, give a Śālagrāma stone adorned with gold lines and the like. A seeker of wealth should give cows and gold.

3.64
The steadfast practitioner should feed Brahmins and unmarried girls, tender one. Then a man should wear the diagram on his right arm—

Note: The verb in the extracted Sanskrit resembles sādhayet, while the following verse and Hindi give the sense of wearing the diagram. The rendering is contextual.

3.65
—a woman on her left arm, and a child at the neck.

3.66
Thus this secret has been told. It must not be given away even when one's life is in danger.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the third chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among all Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 66 numbered units have draft English entries. The mantra in 3.4 and the opening syllables in 3.16 are not fully established; the unresolved material is explicitly marked. A complete verified mantra transcription and collation remain necessary before calling this a finished critical translation.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 4
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition, 1984; printed pages 39–53, PDF pages 59–73. The Sanskrit is translated with the Hindi consulted where necessary. This chapter describes historical ritual magic, including destructive rites. The powers claimed below are claims of the text. Technical, corrupt, or encoded expressions are marked instead of expanded into conjectural instructions.

4.1 — The Goddess said:
God of gods, honoured by the world, saluted by gods and antigods: now I wish to hear the heroic practice of the six rites—

4.2
—blessed for the virtuous, for kings enjoying kingdoms and the successive stages of life, for women, for those established in accomplishment, and for all who delight in enjoyment.

Note: The compound describing kings and their enjoyment is poorly recognized in the OCR; the translation of that phrase is provisional.

4.3 — Īśvara said:
Supreme Lady, pacification, subjugation, immobilization, causing discord, driving away, and causing death: these are proclaimed the six rites.

4.4
All-pervading Goddess, concerning Tāriṇī, Kālikā, and Chinnā, out of affection for you I shall explain the supreme [method] that brings swift accomplishment.

4.5
Rati, Vāṇī, Ramā, Jyeṣṭhā, Mātaṅgī, Kulakāminī, Durgā, and Bhadrakālī [are worshipped] at the beginning of rites, for their accomplishment.

4.6
The heroic practitioner should worship with sixteen services, according to his means, in an empty dwelling, a great forest, or a sanctuary of the deity—

4.7
—and perform the five rites there; but the death rite [is performed] upon a corpse, or, if that is unavailable, in a cremation ground. I shall tell you about clothing.

4.8
Being clothed in space is primary; a leopard skin is second. In its absence, red silk; no other garment should be chosen.

Note: Dvīpicarman more specifically suggests leopard skin, although the Hindi gloss calls it tiger skin.

4.9
Gold for the first [pacification], silver for the second [subjugation], stone for immobilization, copper for discord and expulsion, and a skull is auspicious for the death rite.

Note: The object made of these materials is not repeated in this verse. The surrounding discussion concerns ritual equipment.

4.10
A young, dark-complexioned Brahmin or another man is prescribed; a rosary from one who did not die of famine or disease brings auspiciousness.

Note: Human bone is implied by the context and Hindi gloss. The compound concerning famine and disease is uncertain in the OCR; the negative reading follows the gloss.

4.11
If that is unavailable, repeat with a crystal rosary or indrākṣa, beloved Queen of the gods, on earth, a komala [seat], or a viṣṭara [grass seat]—

4.12
—on a skull, on an ayonika [uncertain technical seat], or on a tiger skin, beloved; on a space measuring one, two, or four hand-lengths on each side—

Note: The Hindi reads the seat-term as yoni, while the OCR suggests ayonika. Its precise referent is unresolved; the English does not invent a physical construction.

4.13
—establish a steady seat properly and contemplate fearlessness. If fear arises, Great Lady, repeat the mantra spoken by Bhairava.

4.14
[Form the mantra with] viṣa twice, “vajrajvāle,” then “hana” twice, “sarvabhūta,” and the kūrca mantra at the end: this is Bhairava's mantra.

Note: This is encoded mantric terminology. The exact initial reading, recognized as viṣayugma by the OCR, needs checking. Hana means “strike” or “kill”; sarvabhūta means “all beings.” The complete formula is not reconstructed from uncertain text.

4.15
Then the practitioner should give a spirit offering according to the rule. Great Lady, make the prescribed peg from aśvattha wood.

Note: The repeated term read as dharma may indicate a customary measure or rule here; it is not a securely established specification.

4.16
Perform the five rites in conjunction with Sunday and the other [weekdays]. Goddess honoured by heroes, the death rite is fixed for Saturday.

4.17
All the rites are to be performed at night, pure-smiling one. First the learned practitioner should repeat his own mantra ten thousand times upon a corpse, as a great means of personal protection when undertaking the operation.

4.18
Then, on the day of the intended rite, after one watch of night, the mantra practitioner should worship the lineage-destroying Goddess together with the five deities beginning with Gaṇa [Gaṇeśa].

4.19
Clothed in space, with all the hair loosened, [acting as] the lineage's pivot and united with Śakti, he should repeat the Vidyā, always remembering you mentally.

Note: Kulakīlaka is rendered literally but remains obscure. Śakti in this ritual context can designate a female ritual partner as well as divine power.

4.20
Great Lady, [repeat] one hundred thousand times, preceded by worship of Śakti. Feed Brahmins daily, and Kaulas and the others on intervening days.

Note: The Sanskrit attaches lakṣasaṃkhya to the mantra repetition continued from 4.19; the Hindi appears to attach it to Śakti worship. This difference is not concealed.

4.21
Having offered meat, liquor, and fish into the fire by the hundreds, give the ritual fee to the guru, Śāmbhavī, honouring him as guru.

4.22
If the foremost hero acts according to this prescribed method, Great Lady, he may lead even the gods [there] from the directions.

Note: The exact directional phrase is compressed; the Hindi understands summoning the deities to the site.

4.23
Beloved, there is no further dependence [or waiting]; one certainly shares in the result. Queen of the gods, in a great operation one should offer a black goat as bali.

4.24
After worship, Goddess, honour the fire with its flesh. This method is prescribed throughout according to the sequence of divya, vīra, and paśu.

4.25
By following the sequence one obtains the fruit; by reversing it one becomes sinful.

4.26
Thus I have properly explained the domain of the six rites. Keep it hidden in the presence of the malicious, the wicked, and the ignorant paśus.

Note: Divya, vīra, and paśu are the text's technical classes of practitioner; paśu also carries the literal sense of a bound or animal being.

4.27 — The Goddess said:
God, what kinds of liquors and what kinds of Śakti are auspicious? Ocean of compassion, explain their proper use in the six rites.

Note: The OCR and Hindi gloss sometimes read surā, liquor, as sura, deity. The beverage names in the next verses support “liquors.”

4.28 — Īśvara said:
Mādhvī is proclaimed pacifying; sphāṭikī is auspicious for subjugation; ḍākinī for immobilization; paiṣṭikī is prescribed for discord—

4.29
—gauḍī for expulsion, and bhairavī for the death rite. Goddess, their characteristics have been explained in the Kulamohana [Tantra].

Note: These names concern the liquor categories asked about in 4.27, although the Hindi gloss calls them Śaktis. Their composition is not given here; no recipe is inferred.

4.30
Padminī is proclaimed pacifying; Śaṅkhinī is prescribed for subjugation; Goddess, Nāgavallabhā is praised for immobilization and expulsion.

4.31
For the death rite, Ḍākinī is approved as bringing death to enemies. [A woman who is] fair-bodied, long-haired, and always nectar-sweet in speech—

4.32
—red-eyed and well-conducted is a Padminī, auspicious in practice. She brings accomplishment of the mantra; and Śaṅkhinī also, radiant one—

4.33
—the Śaṅkhinī is tall-bodied and delights the world. [One who is] well-proportioned, of a śūdra-like body, neither short nor excessively tall—

4.34
—long-haired, full at the middle, and gentle in speech is Nāginī. [One who is] dark-bodied, thin-bodied, prominent-toothed, and afflicted by intoxication—

Note: The caste-related bodily phrase is the edition's terminology, not a modern classification endorsed here. Madatāpita is provisionally rendered “afflicted by intoxication.” The portrait continues in the following verses.

4.35
—short-haired, long-nosed, always harsh-speaking, constantly angry, long-bodied, and given to loud shouting—

4.36
—shameless, without laughter, sleepy, and a great eater: she is called Ḍākinī and is praised for death operations.

4.37
Goddess, such Śaktis arise among all social groups. Those with offspring, skilled in sexual union, and who have borne sons and the like are auspicious. Such beloved women should be accepted and worshipped devotedly with the fluids of the Kula.

Note: These are historical ritual classifications of women. “Fluids of the Kula” translates kularasa without silently replacing the sexual-ritual term by an ordinary drink.

4.38 — The Goddess said:
Master, if I am dear to you, tell me the mantras by which a mantra practitioner may accomplish the six rites.

4.39 — Īśvara said:
Kālikā's [mantra] is one syllable; Tārā's has three seeds; the mantra of Vajravairocanī has eleven syllables—

4.40
—and is declared to take away all power. What need is there for much discussion? Listen, beloved of my life.

4.41
Only when united with Śakti should one repeat [the mantra of] the Goddess with concentrated mind. One certainly obtains the result, honoured by heroes, and not otherwise.

4.42
If a malicious person obtains the result while a powerful person is fruitless, Great Lady, then all this would be in vain.

Textual caution: The wording and logic are difficult in this printing. This follows the apparent reading and Hindi gloss; it is not a settled interpretation.

4.43
Auspicious one, I am the creator and protector, engaged in safeguarding [this method]. Daughter of the mountain, it would be astonishing if accomplishment nevertheless failed to arise.

Note: The first line is imperfectly recognized. The translation gives the sense of the gloss, provisionally.

4.44
Now, Goddess, I shall tell you particularly of the death rite: [a letter] ending in sa, joined with fire, and adorned with the left eye—

Note: This is a coded construction of a seed syllable. Its exact opening letter and segmentation are uncertain; a formula is not supplied conjecturally.

4.45
“Hrīṃ hūṃ hūṃ, cause such-and-such a person to die, svāhā.” Then, Goddess, [with] the kūrca syllable twice, “Cause such-and-such a person to die, cause [them] to die.”

Note: The seed spellings are normalized provisionally from the Hindi and OCR; they are not a verified liturgical transcription. Amuka is the placeholder “such-and-such,” not a personal name.

4.46
The fourteen-syllable mantra, ending in svāhā, destroys enemies. Taking khadira charcoal, especially on an eighth lunar day falling on Tuesday—

4.47
—draw an effigy in the enemy's form on an iron vessel. At night, on the head, eyes, forehead, heart, and hands—

4.48
—navel, genitals, waist, back, and the two feet, write the mantra letters in the prescribed order and establish [the effigy] there.

4.49
Having made the gesture of dissolution, contemplate the victory-giving Goddess, tall in form, dark in colour, her breasts and head ever raised—

4.50
—clothed in space, chewing two human heads [held] in her hands. For the enemy's destruction, contemplate the enemy-destroying Goddess in this manner.

4.51
Having contemplated her thus, Śaṅkarī, [use] powdered brick with the left hand [and say], “Oṃ, homage to the destroyer of enemies,” Great Lady—

Note: Iṣṭakācūrṇa literally means brick powder; the Hindi instead says gerū, red ochre. This discrepancy is preserved.

4.52
—and pour a stream mixed with turmeric powder using this [formula]; then [say], “Drink, drink such-and-such a person's blood; eat, eat the flesh; hrīṃ, homage.”

4.53
After worshipping at midday or midnight, repeat one hundred and eight times. On the eleventh day illness will arise: there is no doubt.

4.54
One daṇḍa beyond the twenty-first day, the enemy's death is certain. Or I shall explain another manner of destroying an enemy.

Note: The timing claims are the source's statements of magical efficacy, not verified effects.

4.55
Take a bull's dung and mix it with hot water. Perform repetition, worship, and the other acts in reverse order.

4.56
With “Homage to Mahādeva,” obtain the bull's dung; with the mantra “Homage to Śiva,” form it into a lump.

4.57
Then with “Homage to Paśupati,” establish the vital powers. Great Lady, making the enemy's likeness on an iron vessel with khadira charcoal, establish Śiva there.

4.58
Then contemplate Mahārudra; listen attentively to the meditation: Rudra stands upon the enemy's chest, shining like blazing fire—

4.59
—with the left hand grasping the hair, and with the right drawing out the life-breath; the god wears human skin and is encircled by great serpents and the like.

4.60
Carefully invoke him with “Bearer of Pināka, come here,” and the rest. The excellent practitioner should bathe [him] with “Homage to the trident-bearer.”

4.61
With “Homage to Maheśvara,” worship with water for the feet and the other services. Worship the forms beginning with Īśāna in reverse order—

4.62
—as far as the southeast and the other [directions], following the sun's course, Great Lady. Repeat the root [mantra], “Oṃ, homage to Śiva,” twenty-eight times.

Note: “In reverse order” and “following the sun's course” appear together in the source. Their precise reconciliation within this ritual sequence requires further study.

4.63
Dismiss [him] with the left hand, saying “Hūṃ, forgive [me].” Repeat once the mantra: “Keśava, Ajita, Viṣṇu, Hari, Satya, Janārdana; haṃsa, to Nārāyaṇa, svāhā.”

4.64
Also repeat once [each day] “Hūṃ, homage to the blessed Vāsudeva, svāhā,” and the mantra “Oṃ, homage to Śiva.”

4.65
Goddess, in eleven days the enemy's madness will arise readily and wondrously. Three-eyed one, truly, truly.

4.66
I shall explain an excellent immobilization of enemies. The excellent practitioner should bring from the potter's house one, two, or three good pots—

4.67
—and bring old ash from the kiln. Place it in the pot, [with] a spread of dry nālikā leaves, and place a pot over the ash. Queen of the gods, Great Lady, on Saturday make a hole in the northeast.

Note: The arrangement of the pot and lid is clearer in the Hindi than the extracted Sanskrit. Nālikā's botanical identification is not securely established here.

4.68
Then, radiant one, at midday when the place is deserted, [go] around the four sides of the garden, near the hole, beloved—

4.69
—and with a joyful mind take possession of the ground, [saying], “I protect this much land from thieves,” Goddess.

4.70
Then, radiant one, circle that extent of ground counterclockwise and return to the hole.

4.71
Secretly plant an iron rod in that hole; put above it the pot together with its lid, fill the hole with earth, and go home.

4.72
Goddess, the movement of thieves and the like will be halted. This excellent method is rare upon the earth.

4.73
Piśācas, Bhūtas, Vetālas, Kūṣmāṇḍas, Brahmarākṣasas, Dānavas, and others cannot move there.

4.74
Wealth, children, and prosperity increase day and night; dharma increases day by day. There is no doubt.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the fourth chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among all Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 74 numbered units have draft English entries. Uncertain terms, encoded mantras, and conflicts with the Hindi gloss are marked. This has not been independently reviewed or fully collated.
