YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 5
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition, 1984; printed pages 53–66, PDF pages 73–86. Sanskrit translated with the Hindi gloss consulted as a secondary aid. This chapter concerns ritual practice at a bed, crossroads, a bilva tree, and a skull-seat. Religious claims are those of the source. Encoded syllables and uncertain readings are marked. This draft has not been fully collated or independently reviewed.

5.1 — The Goddess said:
Blessed Lord, knower of every dharma, benefactor of the world: I wish to hear the practice that completes all the constituent parts of every mantra. Great Lord, tell me that.

5.2 — Īśvara said:
First I shall explain the exceedingly wondrous practice of the bed. When one and a half watches of night have passed, the excellent practitioner—

5.3
—clothed in space, having duly performed ritual sipping and sprinkled [the bed or himself] with the root mantra, should lie upon the bed.

Note: The object of sprinkling is not explicit in the extracted Sanskrit; the Hindi supplies the practitioner's body.

5.4
Having worshipped on the left the guru, the higher guru, the guru beyond the higher, the supreme guru, and Gaṇeśa—

5.5
—[worship] on the right, above the eyebrows, with “Homage to the dweller in the cremation ground.” Then, auspicious one, worship with “Homage to Yama's seat.”

Textual caution: The location phrase before “cremation-ground dweller” is uncertain in both the OCR and Hindi quotation. “Above the eyebrows” is a tentative reading of bhruvor ūrdhvam, not a settled reconstruction.

5.6
Goddess, on the surface of the bed write praṇava, Vāgbhava, and phaṭ; then, having carefully sipped water, according to the procedure prescribed for one's own mantra—

5.7
—[form the invocation with] praṇava, “Maṇidhari,” “Vajriṇī,” the great word, and “Pratisīrā, protect me, protect me,” together with hūṃ phaṭ svāhā.

Note: This is a coded or fragmentary statement of the Mahāpratisarā-type protection formula. The segmentation of the divine names is not fully secure. It is retained as printed elements, rather than turned into an invented continuous mantra.

5.8
Goddess, bind the topknot duly with this mantra; then [perform] placement on the limbs, placement on the hands, and placement of the alphabetic Mothers—

5.9
—and purification of the elements and the other [preliminaries]. The excellent practitioner should contemplate the supreme auspicious Goddess in the heart-lotus and devotedly worship her with mental offerings.

Note: Nyāsa is the ritual placement of letters, powers, or deities on the body. Bhūtaśuddhi is purification of the elements, not simply exorcism.

5.10
Beautiful-faced one, having brought her to the bed, upon the mantra [drawn there], Goddess, then [say] praṇava and “Homage to the Baṭukas.”

Note: The first clause is not securely recovered from the OCR. Its object and the relation of the Goddess to the written mantra remain provisional.

5.11
With this mantra, worship Baṭuka mentally with water for the feet and the other services. Then, using the fire seed and a stream of water all around—

5.12
—contemplate a wall of fire; respectfully make the declaration of intention, perform repetition and duly dedicate it, Supreme Lady. Again making the declaration, Queen of the gods, perform the whole sequence, auspicious one—

5.13
—fire-offering, libation, then anointing, and feeding a Brahmin. If [an element] is unavailable, repeat [the mantra] twice as much.

Note: The exact scope of the replacement by doubled repetition is not specified here; it is not expanded into a universal substitution rule.

5.14
Give gold as the ritual fee and perform the declaration that the rite is without defect. Goddess, having [praised or propitiated] Baṭuka, dismiss him with “Forgive [me].”

Note: Acchidrāvadhāraṇa means ascertaining or declaring ritual completeness. The Hindi gloss in this passage appears to confuse it with holding an unbroken vessel; that literalization is not adopted. The verb concerning Baṭuka is uncertain.

5.15
Great Goddess, thus I have explained the excellent practice of the bed, which quickly accomplishes the mantra and bestows union with us.

5.16
Now hear the practice of the three-way and four-way crossroads. Beautiful-faced one, having gone to a place where three roads or four roads meet—

5.17
—bow and worship the guru, then tie the knot with the mantra beginning “Maṇidhari” and ending [with the] vajra [expression]. The excellent practitioner should be fearless.

5.18
“May all the gods, goddesses, and Bhairavas who dwell in the cremation ground show compassion and grant me accomplishment.”

5.19
Bow devotedly with this mantra beginning with praṇava. Then, facing east or north—

5.20
—sit, perform ritual sipping, and pronounce a blessing, Great Lady. Sweep the place clean; there the discerning practitioner should write the preta seed.

Note: Preta is a departed spirit or corpse; the precise seed syllable is encoded, not explicitly spelled out in this verse.

5.21
Great Goddess, spread the prescribed seat over the seed. Queen of the gods, sit there and remember the chosen deity in the heart.

5.22
Having worshipped mentally as desired, give a bali offering in the eight directions. Great Lady, worship Kāla and the others according to the rule.

5.23
Kālī, Kapālinī, Kullā, Kurukullā, Virodhinī, Vipracittā, Nīlā, Balākā, and Munidviṣā—

Note: Vipracittā is supported by the Hindi, while the OCR gives vicitta. Munidviṣā is the name as recognized in this edition and requires comparison with other lists before normalization.

5.24
—their worship is prescribed with bali and the other [offerings], with [their names] beginning in praṇava and ending in namaḥ. Declare the intention, repeat one hundred and eight times, establish the rite's completeness, leave the place, and go home remembering the Goddess.

5.25
Thus this practice, attended by all accomplishments, has been explained. It must not be given indiscriminately; it is supremely beneficial to practitioners.

Note: The printed wording recognized as yasmai karma is uncertain; the restriction on indiscriminate transmission follows the Hindi explanation.

5.26
Next I shall explain practice at the root of a bilva tree. Great Lady, the whole region of the bilva's root is remembered as Śaṅkara.

5.27
Queen of the gods, beautiful one, know its leaves as the form of [my] matted locks. Beautiful-faced one, its three leaflets resemble the Ṛg, Yajur, and Sāma [Vedas].

5.28
Fish-eyed one, know its branches as all the scriptures. The bilva is like the wish-fulfilling tree and is Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva, and the others. Mahālakṣmī was born as a bilva tree on Mount Śrīśaila.

5.29 — The Goddess said:
Compassionate one, how did Viṣṇu's wife become a bilva tree? Tell me this exceedingly wondrous account.

5.30 — Īśvara said:
Goddess, listen: I shall tell the exceedingly wondrous story. In the Kṛta age [the goddesses] worshipped the liṅga named Rāmeśvara—

5.31
—a portion of myself in the form of light, together with Brahmā and the other [gods]. There, through my favour, Vāṇī became dear to everyone.

Note: The subject and one word before Brahmā are poorly recognized. The Hindi supplies the goddesses as worshippers. Vāṇī is Sarasvatī.

5.32
Sarasvatī always became exceedingly dear to Viṣṇu; Keśava did not have the same affection for Lakṣmī.

5.33
Thinking deeply about this, Lakṣmī went to excellent Śrīśaila. Having reached a liṅga of mine in solitude, she performed extremely severe austerities.

5.34
Yet, Supreme Lady, compassion did not arise in my heart. Then she remained before the liṅga in the form of a tree—

5.35
—and continually worshipped with her own leaves, flowers, and fruit. Great Lady, after ten million years my favour arose.

5.36
Through that favour she came to dwell on Viṣṇu's breast. Supreme Lady, she delights there forever.

5.37
For that reason, Goddess, Hari's beloved always worships me in that [tree] form. Auspicious one, she is incomparably devoted to me.

5.38
Therefore I dwell in that tree day and night. Goddess, it always embodies all pilgrimage places and all deities—

5.39
—that bilva tree, Supreme Lady: there is no doubt. Whoever worships [me] with its fruit, its flowers, or its leaves—

5.40
—or with the paste of its wood, is my devotee and is dear to me.

5.41
Whoever bears the paste of its wood upon the forehead, even inadvertently, she [Lakṣmī] joyfully salutes, recognizing that person as Śiva.

Note: The Sanskrit word saṃbhramāt can convey agitation, haste, or confusion; “even inadvertently” follows the Hindi's interpretation of this adverb.

5.42
Therefore, Goddess, one should never wear that paste, nor ever wear its leaves or flowers.

Note: This prohibition is explicit despite the praise in the preceding verses. It is not silently reversed. The text does not state the explanation here; one possible implication is reluctance to receive Lakṣmī's homage, but that is interpretation, not translation.

5.43
Great Lady, a person who gives up life at the foot of a bilva tree becomes Rudra himself, even if burdened with ten million sins: truly.

5.44
Therefore, Goddess, practice there pleases all [the deities]. Having gone to the bilva's root, carefully worship the four gurus as before, and worship the guardian of the field.

5.45
“Guardian of the field, greatly fortunate one, lord of the cremation ground, keeper of excellent vows, creator of the world: give accomplishment, give a place; homage to you.”

5.46
Then bow with this mantra beginning in praṇava. Beautiful-faced one, worship the place and write there—

5.47
—Vāgbhava, the preta seed, and Vāgbhava again. The excellent practitioner should write the root mantra at their end.

5.48
Having worshipped Kāla and the others as before, Supreme Lady, declare the intention, repeat one hundred and eight times, and establish completeness.

5.49
Leave the place and go home remembering the guru. Thus the attainable [secret] has been explained, essence beyond essence, higher than the highest.

5.50
Auspicious one, it must always be concealed, especially in a place crowded with paśus.

Note: Paśu is the text's technical and polemical term for those outside the relevant initiated practice.

5.51
Now, Queen of the gods, hear the excellent practice of skulls. By performing it the practitioner reaches the supreme state of the Great Goddess.

5.52
Beautiful-faced one, [obtain] a human skull, a buffalo skull, and a cat skull; or, Supreme Lady, carefully [obtain] three human skulls.

5.53
Great Lady, [the skulls] of a jackal, snake, dog, and bull, with a human skull in the centre, are called the five-skull [arrangement].

5.54
Or, Supreme Lady, [use] five human skulls; likewise a hundred, a thousand, ten thousand, or one hundred thousand—

5.55
—or a niyuta, or ten million human skulls, Supreme Lady. Having placed and buried the human skull[s] in the ground—

Note: Niyuta is a traditional large-number term whose value varies between systems. It is retained instead of assigned a single unsupported value. These numbers are the text's grandiose prescriptions, not evidence that such arrangements were actually assembled.

5.56
—construct above them an altar one span [high], Goddess, and four hand-lengths in length and breadth.

5.57
For a thousand up to one hundred thousand, [make it] sixteen hand-lengths; beyond that, Great Goddess, make it a hundred hand-lengths.

Note: The Sanskrit reads śatahasta, a hundred hand-lengths; the Hindi appears to print two hundred. The Sanskrit reading is retained.

5.58
On it, worship Bhūtanātha and the others in the four directions.

5.59
The teacher should worship with water for the feet and the other services, using the previously stated mantra “Homage to Bhūtanātha,” and carefully give bali.

5.60
Likewise, Goddess, [worship] the lord of the cremation ground to the south, and excellent Kālabhairava to the west—

5.61
—and [the cremation-ground deity] to the north, respectfully worshipping and offering bali. In the altar's centre [write] the preta seed, followed by phaṭ.

Note: The northern deity is simply read as śmaśāna in the extracted passage. A fuller name is not invented.

5.62
With this [mantra], worship the ritual pits with water for the feet and the other services.

5.63
Then write in the southwest “Hrīṃ, to Caṇḍikā,” and the seed. There worship Bhāratī of white form with devotion—

5.64
—the Goddess of speech together with her attendant powers, with [the formula] beginning with Vāgbhava and ending in namaḥ. Worship with this mantra and offer bali to her.

Note: The juxtaposition of Caṇḍikā and Bhāratī occurs in the edition. It is not silently corrected to make the deity names uniform.

5.65
“Hero, lord of all gods, whose form is the skull, lord of the world, greatly fortunate one: show compassion; be the giver of accomplishment in my repetition.”

5.66
Make śāva joined with fire and pāśa furnished with the lunar digit—

Textual caution: This is encoded seed-syllable formation, not a secure instruction to put a corpse into a fire. The Hindi expands it literally in that manner, but the Sanskrit's mantric context makes that interpretation uncertain. Śāva and pāśa are retained rather than expanded conjecturally.

5.67
—[then] Māyā seed, Kūrca seed, and thereafter phaṭ. With this [mantra], worship the pits with water for the feet and the other services.

5.68
The teacher [worships] in the southwest with “Hrīṃ, homage to Caṇḍikā”; then in the northwest with “Hrīṃ, homage to Bhadrakālī”—

5.69
—in the northeast, Śāmbhavī, with “Hrīṃ, homage to Dayā”; and in the southeast the practitioner [worships] with “Hrīṃ, homage to Caṇḍogrā.”

Note: Dayā and Caṇḍogrā are retained as recognized in the edition; their spellings need comparison with the scan and other witnesses before a final mantra transcription.

5.70
Having worshipped and given bali, stand and face [the altar]: “Whatever gods, goddesses, and Bhairavas dwell in the cremation ground—

5.71
—may all of them show compassion and grant me accomplishment.” With this [mantra] beginning in praṇava, offer three handfuls of flowers.

5.72
Address the whole place: “Come under control, come under control.” Great Lady, spread a grass seat and sit upon it.

5.73
Perform ten thousand and eight repetitions and establish completeness. Bow, set the place in order, and go home contemplating the Goddess.

Note: The Sanskrit number for this unit is misrecognized as 72; the Hindi and sequence identify it as 73.

5.74
Goddess, Śāmbhavī, hear the remaining [rule] according to the preliminary mantra observance: never establish a kīlaka above the three-skull seat.

Note: Kīlaka can mean a ritual peg, a fastening or constraint, or a mantric “pin.” The precise sense should not be assumed without the wider ritual context. Puraścaryā is preliminary mantra discipline.

5.75
In an empty dwelling, beside a river, on a mountain, at a crossroads, at the root of a bilva, in a cremation ground, in solitude, or at an isolated liṅga—

5.76
—in these places bury the skulls for fulfilment of every desire. Or, having duly buried a human skull in this way—

5.77
—all accomplishments arise by this [practice]; what need is there for many [skulls], excellent-vowed one? Thus, Goddess, auspicious one, I have explained practice with skulls, by performing which one becomes lord of all accomplished beings on earth.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the fifth chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 77 numbered units have draft English entries. Encoded mantra constructions, several damaged passages, and disagreements between Sanskrit and Hindi remain flagged. A final collation and independent review remain outstanding.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 6
English working translation, with one non-graphic summary

Source: Mishra edition, 1984; printed pages 67–80, PDF pages 87–100. Sanskrit translated with the Hindi consulted as a secondary aid. The text discusses divya and vīra contemplation, ritual observance, and internal interpretations of the five ritual substances. Statements of doctrine and social hierarchy are those of the historical source. Uncertain text is marked.

Verse 6.45 is represented by a non-graphic historical summary because it includes a sexual prescription involving minors; explicit detail is not reproduced. Its presence and location are recorded rather than silently omitted. Consequently this file is not a completely unabridged translation.

6.1 — The Goddess said:
Blessed Lord, knower of every dharma, expert in all Āgamas, ocean of the nectar of compassion: you are the guru of all mantras.

6.2
Worshipped by the practitioners of all dharmas, sun to the lotus-hearts of yogins, you have shown the greatness of the divine disposition and heroic disposition. Moon-crested one, tell me what is especially secret in them.

6.3 — Īśvara said:
Two yogas are declared, distinguished as divine and heroic. Great Lady, through that yoga a Kaula becomes divine or heroic.

Note: The second half is defective in the OCR and not transparent in the Hindi. The translation is provisional; it should not be cited as a settled grammatical reading.

6.4
Goddess, whoever performs that rite without the corresponding yoga is not a yogin seeking liberation, beloved of the Kula.

6.5
I shall explain it to you; having heard it, make it an ornament of your ears. One should contemplate oneself as supreme Brahman; or, if not—

6.6
—one should always contemplate one's own body as the chosen deity's form, and conceive the whole universe likewise as that form.

6.7
Goddess, carefully conceal this divine yoga. Now hear the heroic yoga, saluted by all gods.

6.8
First contemplate three bindus together with the lunar digit. Then conceive arising from them a female form of sixteen years.

Note: Bindu denotes a point, drop, or concentrated potency; kalā is a digit or aspect. This is a visualization of divine form, not a narrative sexual scene.

6.9
Its radiance, like ten million rising suns, illuminates the horizons. From the upper bindu arises [the form] from the head as far as the breasts.

6.10
The middle body [arises from] the bindu, from the neck to the upper waist, shining with the pair of breasts and adorned with three folds.

Note: The exact anatomical boundaries of the middle segment are imperfectly recognized; the Hindi's reading is followed provisionally.

6.11
Then contemplate with concentration the lovely [form] from the yoni down to the feet.

6.12
Adorned with many ornaments and honoured by Viṣṇu, Brahmā, and Īśvara: in this way contemplate one's own body as the form of Kāmakalā.

Note: Kāmakalā is the technical divine configuration of desire and creative power described by the three bindus.

6.13
Always, Supreme Lady, hear this heroic yoga. I shall briefly describe the excellent observance of the two.

6.14
Liquor, meat, fish, mudrā, and sexual union: this observance, Goddess, belongs to the paśu, not to the divine and heroic [practitioners].

Note: The surprising attribution to paśu is explicit in both the extracted Sanskrit and Hindi; it is not silently emended. The following verses again prescribe liquor and other substances. This doctrinal tension requires interpretation and textual comparison, not a reversal inserted into the translation. Mudrā here is a ritual food, identified below, rather than simply a hand gesture.

6.15
Great Lady, the divine and heroic practitioner should follow divine observance. If, by circumstance, liquor and the other [substances] are unavailable—

6.16
—on that day, Queen of the gods, one should contemplate oneself in that manner. Yet, auspicious one, this [observance/ritual sipping] is not to be abandoned.

Textual caution: The final term appears as ācamanam in the OCR and Hindi but may require comparison with ācaraṇam, observance. The ambiguity is retained rather than resolved without evidence.

6.17
A Kaula does not remain even a moment without the great liquor. Therefore, Goddess, liquor and the others are to be partaken of daily.

6.18
Daughter of the mountain, hear: I shall explain the ritual procedure. Having been initiated by the guru, perform Kaula nyāsa.

Note: Nyāsa, ritual placement, is mistakenly treated as nāśa, destruction, in the OCR or Hindi extraction. “Destroy the Kaula” would be a mistranslation.

6.19
Its procedure [is given] in the Uttara Tantra, and this [procedure] in the Kulārṇava. Following the order I have stated, perform the two consecrations.

6.20
Having also obtained a name from the guru, adopt the excellent yoga, divine or heroic, according to the rite and its essential character.

6.21
Immediately becoming dear [to the deity], one is released from Time. The divine practitioner is truly Viṣṇu: there is no doubt.

Note: Kālataḥ can mean “from Time/death” or “in the course of time.” The Hindi chooses the latter. The former is provisionally followed in the running English, with the alternative recorded here.

6.22
The heroic practitioner becomes Rudra in the end: there is no doubt. In whatever country a divine practitioner or foremost hero lives—

6.23
—that lineage, that country, and the ruler himself [are blessed]. The region for ten yojanas all around is declared a field of accomplishment.

Note: The predicate concerning the lineage and ruler is compressed; the Hindi explains their excellence and holiness.

6.24
All pilgrimage places are there; the foremost river Gaṅgā is there. This [place] is difficult [to approach] for Yoginīs, Ḍākinīs, and creeping creatures—

6.25
—for Brahmarākṣasas, Vetālas, Kūṣmāṇḍas, Bhairavas, auspicious one, Guhyakas, Dānavas, illusion-workers, Yakṣas, and Kinnaras—

6.26
—and it is not afflicted by diseases, dangerous beasts, or famine. Auspiciousness is certain there, and that city prospers.

Note: The lists run across 6.24–26. “Yoginīs” in the first of these lines is uncertain; it may concern yogins and a different syntactic construction. The precise list needs collation.

6.27
Abundance of food, security, health, and the treasury of true dharma [flourish]; all the scriptures continually reveal themselves.

6.28
The excellent practitioner is very dear to Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva, and the others. Even trees thrive there; animals and birds live well.

Note: Sujīvanti means “live well” or “thrive,” rather than necessarily the Hindi's stronger claim that the trees do not die.

6.29
Goddess, wherever a divine practitioner or foremost hero dies, if the mind is devoted to Kula dharma and one's own observance is established, I impart supreme knowledge at the ear. Therefore this Kula dharma must be continually served.

6.30
Goddess, all the inhabitants of heaven, sages, human beings, Nāgas, Siddhas, Gandharvas, and Kinnaras are devoted to Kula dharma—

6.31
—as are Ṛṣis, Vasus, Daityas, and those who are foremost in Kula. Through the grace of Kula dharma they all become leaders of the Kula.

6.32
Indra and the others, mounted in the sky, become long-lived. In whatever manner people take refuge in me, in that manner they partake of the result.

6.33
Brahmins, Kṣatriyas, Vaiśyas, students, householders, forest-dwellers, and renunciants should follow the Kula.

6.34
Leader of the Kula, now hear their rule from me. The Brahmin's liquor [is made] with jaggery and ginger juice.

6.35
Beautiful-faced one, for the Kṣatriya, coconut water in a bronze vessel; beautiful one, for the Vaiśya, honey in bronze is prescribed.

6.36
For everyone, meat and fish are declared [to be represented by] salt and ginger. Whatever roasted grains and other foods are chewed—

6.37
—are called mudrā for all, Goddess, daughter of the mountain. A Brahmin woman [is prescribed] for the Brahmin; a Kṣatriya woman for the Kṣatriya—

6.38
—and a Vaiśya woman for the Vaiśya: this is the rule remembered concerning union. Or, Great Lady, a Vaiśya woman for the two classes, Brahmin and Kṣatriya; also a Kṣatriya woman for a Brahmin, beautiful one—

6.39
—or, in their absence, a Śūdra woman for the three classes beginning with the Brahmin.

Note on 6.34–39: These are historical caste-based ritual prescriptions. The substitute reading of salt and ginger in 6.36 is the likely Sanskrit sense; the Hindi is less explicit about substitution.

6.40
This is remembered for those in the stages of life among Brahmins, Kṣatriyas, and Vaiśyas. Now hear [the rule] for renunciants admitted from those three classes.

6.41
Auspicious one, the meeting of Kuṇḍalinī with the bindu above the thousand-petalled [lotus] is proclaimed the divine union and repose of renunciants.

6.42
Hear the rule I shall explain for one in the avadhūta state. Śāmbhavī, all liquors, including paiṣṭika, [are permitted] for that person.

6.43
Goddess, aquatic, terrestrial, and aerial creatures [provide] that person's fish and meat; the previously described mudrā is to be partaken of respectfully.

6.44
[Non-graphic rendering:] The text excludes the practitioner's mother as a partner and distinguishes prior sexual experience as a condition for the rite; it prohibits violating an inexperienced partner.

Note: This is a non-graphic rendering of the sexual prescription. The force of tāḍay- is more literal and potentially violent than the explanatory English. It should not be taken as a modern consent framework supplied by the source.

6.45
[Non-graphic historical summary, not a literal translation:] The passage claims loss of spiritual accomplishment for violating the preceding restriction, and then gives an age-and-physiology rule for sexual practice that includes minors. Explicit sexual detail involving minors is not reproduced.

6.46
All the observances of an avadhūta are proclaimed for a Śūdra. Hear from me the particular rule concerning that person's union.

6.47
[Non-graphic rendering:] Excluding Brahmin, Kṣatriya, and Vaiśya women, the discerning practitioner should observe ritual union among the other social groups, with the conception of divinity.

6.48
A Śūdra should be a householder, never a member of another stage of life. This observance is proclaimed for other social groups as for the Śūdra.

Note: This exclusionary rule is the text's social prescription, not an assertion about present eligibility or rights.

6.49
[Uncertain passage:] Even in the avadhūta state, union is prescribed [with reference to] the guru, the two or three social classes, and the maternal grandfather's lineage.

Textual caution: The exact prohibition or permission in this verse cannot be established from the extracted Sanskrit and Hindi. The Hindi largely repeats the unclear wording. This is a record of its recoverable terms, not a settled translation of the relationship between them.

6.50
Kālī, Tārā, Chinnamastā, Sundarī, Bhairavī, Mātaṅgī, Vidyā, and the Vidyā Dhūmāvatī—

Note: Vidyā is repeated in the source. It may be an epithet or reflect a damaged list; a missing Goddess is not supplied conjecturally.

6.51
—the observance of their practitioners is remembered as like that of an avadhūta, in every stage of life, every social class, and every yoga, auspicious one.

6.52
Everywhere, in all places, there is no particular difference. Bliss is the form of Brahman, and it is established in the body.

6.53
Thus a Brahmin should offer the blood of his own body to the deity. In offering one's own blood there is loss of power and disturbance.

Textual caution: The affirmative dadet, “should give,” is visible in both Mishra scans and Shastri's edition (1982, printed p.43/PDF107, verse53). The following warning conflicts with it. The English preserves the affirmative as printed; it does not silently insert a conjectured negative to remove the tension.

6.54
Kula yogins offer a substance that manifests that [bliss]. Goddess, substances and the rest [are expressions of it; the final construction is unclear].

Note: The key term abhivyañjana refers to manifestation or expression. The Hindi links it specifically to the preceding blood offering, but the wider argument concerns the manifestation of bliss. The final phrase cannot be securely resolved here.

6.55
By the first yoga alone one becomes a practitioner of Kālī and the others; by the second, [one reaches] Bhairava; by the third, the state of Śiva—

6.56
—and by the fourth, lordship over all accomplishments. Daughter of the mountain, this is wondrous: progressing ever higher, one becomes equal to me, without doubt.

Note: This passage refers to four yogas although the chapter opened with two. Their identification is not supplied in these verses; the difference is preserved.

6.57
By serving the Kula principle, those who see that principle become Bhairavas, sharing their appearance and their equal vision.

6.58
As a house enveloped in darkness is seen with a lamp, so the mind covered by Māyā is seen with the lamp of knowledge.

6.59
Distinctions between things deemed pure and impure are dispelled. One is liberated while still embodied, and at the body's end attains peace.

6.60
Having drunk the Kula fluid, the hero should resort to contemplation of Brahman. Great Lady, contemplation of Brahman causes liberation in Brahman.

6.61
Great Lady, hear that essence beyond essence, higher than the highest: one's own individual life, body, and the limitless universe—

6.62
—all of it, Goddess, whatever there is, from the body to the great ocean, should not be contemplated [as independently real]. Conceive that all of it does not exist [as separate being].

Note: The qualifications in brackets express the nondual context. Literally the final instruction is “conceive that it is not.”

6.63
[Opening qualifier uncertain:] The great radiance is all-pervading consciousness. I alone have the form of a network [jālarūpa] and am without a support or body.

Textual caution: Both Mishra and Shastri (1982, printed p.44/PDF108, verse64) print an obscure opening qualifier, approximately mātsyīyakaṃ, and jālarūpa. Jāla with long ā means a net or network, whereas jala means water; the Hindi's water-like interpretation is not adopted as a secure reading. The final compound ādhāradehavarjita is rendered “without a support or body.” These literal elements do not resolve the verse's overall obscurity.

6.64
Queen of the gods, contemplate oneself also as non-different from that [reality]. This is called contemplation of Brahman, for making [the contemplation] completely steady.

Textual note: The Mishra text layer gives tadbhedena, “with that distinction.” Shastri's edition (1982, printed p.45/PDF109, verse65), checked visually, clearly reads tadabhedena, “as non-different from that.” This translation explicitly adopts that attested variant; it is not merely a contextual conjecture.

6.65
Yogins partake of the substance [in this contemplative manner], never otherwise. Whoever for a moment contemplates the self as “I am Brahman”—

6.66
—Goddess, [the practice] gives that person its fruit, whose limit cannot be reached by me, by Brahmā, or by Viṣṇu in any way.

Note: The Hindi reads the second half differently, as inability to know the practitioner in the absence of the stated contemplation. The Sanskrit's tasyāntaṃ … naiva gamyate supports “its limit cannot be reached,” but the text requires checking.

6.67
Therefore, Great Lady, do not neglect the daily observance. If the substance is unavailable, perform it even with water.

6.68
Or practise Kula yoga mentally every day. Auspicious one, lotus-faced one, I shall explain the [nectar] procedure: listen.

Note: The extracted word resembles ayuta, ten thousand, but amṛta, nectar, is supported by the following verse and may be the intended reading. This is explicitly a contextual emendation, not a verified letter-by-letter reading.

6.69
Great Lady, the supreme nectar that flows from the moon when Kuṇḍalinī unites [with it] is what the yogin drinks: truly, truly, beautiful-faced one.

6.70
Great Goddess, this great drinking is remembered as Kula yoga. Śāmbhavī, having slain the beast of sin and merit with the sword of knowledge—

6.71
—one should lead the mind into the supreme Self: this is called “meat.” Mentally restrain all one's senses and unite them with the Self—

6.72
—and that yogin is an “eater of fish,” released from bondage, beloved. Lead the entire vessel of the universe into supreme Brahman.

Note: The Sanskrit word for the cosmic vessel is poorly recognized as brāhmaṇa/brahmāṇḍa in the text layer; the Hindi supports brahmāṇḍa, universe. These verses deliberately reinterpret ritual consumption as inward discipline.

6.73
The union of the self with supreme Śakti—not [the emission of] semen—is called sexual union. Thus, Goddess, I have explained the essence beyond essence, higher than the highest. Conceal it, conceal it: this is the practice that is my all.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the sixth chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
There is an English entry for every numbered unit, 1–73. Verse 6.45 is a non-graphic summary, not a literal translation; 6.44 and 6.47 are also rendered non-graphically. Several corrupt or ambiguous passages remain explicitly provisional. This is not an unabridged, fully verified translation.

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 7
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition, 1984, printed pages 80–91; PDF pages 100–111. Sanskrit translated with consultation of the Hindi gloss. Claims about dreams, revival, and magical influence belong to the source. Seed syllables are retained without invented lexical meanings. Unclear readings are marked.

7.1 — The Goddess said:
Homage to you, Mahādeva, deliverer across the ocean of worldly existence. Victory to you, lord of the entire universe, lover of devotees, sovereign Lord!

7.2
Mass of supreme bliss, cause of causes: I have heard the two yogas distinguished as divine and heroic.

7.3
Now I wish to hear the auspicious Svapnavatī Vidyā, the Mṛtasañjīvanī Vidyā, and Madhumatī. Supreme Lord, tell me their practice and the swift result.

7.4 — Īśvara said:
Listen, Goddess: I shall explain what you ask me.

7.5
“Oṃ hrīṃ, Svapurāvāhi Kāli, tell [me] in a dream; give such-and-such [a thing] to such-and-such [a person]; krīṃ svāhā.”

Note: The vocative expression Svapurāvāhi is uncertain in this printing; its spelling is provisional, not a verified mantra transcription.

7.6
First utter praṇava, then the Māyā seed; after that the two vocatives, “Svapurāvāhi” and “Kāli.”

7.7
Then “tell in a dream,” followed by “such-and-such [a thing] for such-and-such [a person], give”; then the Kālī seed, and at the end the wife of fire [svāhā].

7.8
This is the Svapnavatī Vidyā, exceedingly rare in the three worlds, producing great marvels, contemplated by Mahākāla.

7.9
Repeat one hundred and eight times daily for four years. Then the Vidyā becomes accomplished and is present in dreams every day.

7.10
Whatever is conceived in the mind is seen in a dream. Daughter of the mountain, now hear the Mṛtasañjīvanī Vidyā.

7.11
Utter the nectar seed, then “Mṛtasañjīvani”; after one's own mantra, [say], “Raise this dead person.”

Note: The Hindi identifies the nectar seed as vaṃ; the Sanskrit encodes it rather than explicitly spelling it here.

7.12
At the end [utter] the wife of the great fire [svāhā]. This Great Vidyā is renowned in the three worlds; it lies asleep and is remembered as essence beyond essence.

7.13
Śāmbhavī, merely through one hundred and eight daily repetitions, that Mṛtasañjīvanī Vidyā becomes a giver of accomplishment.

7.14
If someone has gone to the abode of Time, or even entered the smoke of the funeral pyre, touching the corpse and repeating the mantra makes that person long-lived: truly, beautiful-faced Goddess, there is no doubt.

Note: This is the text's supernatural claim, not a verified account of revival.

7.15
I shall explain Madhumatī Vidyā, who delights everyone.

7.16
Having said “Śrī Madhumati,” [say] “the directions, the unmoving and moving, the oceans, cities, and jewels; attractress of all—”

7.17
“—ṭhaṃ ṭhaṃ svāhā.” This supreme Great Vidyā of eighteen syllables is proclaimed the attractress of the three worlds, difficult even for gods to obtain.

Note: The syllable count is the source's designation; it is not verified from the extracted formula, whose grammar and segmentation are uneven.

7.18
A person should repeat one hundred and eight times daily for one year. Then the Great Vidyā becomes accomplished, revealing all knowledge.

7.19
It may attract Sumeru, the directions, the ocean, rivers, jewels, cities, women, mountains, trees, and inaccessible substances, even those situated in the underworld and elsewhere.

7.20
[One may know] the residences and affairs of rulers and enemies. Repeating a hundred times at night upon the bed, the practitioner attains accomplishment.

7.21 — The Goddess said:
God of gods, lord of the universe, be gracious! Lord, I have heard what I asked; now I wish to hear the Great Vidyā Padmāvatī, who delights in every Vidyā.

7.22 — Īśvara said:
Beautiful one, I shall explain auspicious Padmāvatī Vidyā. First utter praṇava, then the Māyā seed, then the Goddess's name “Padmāvati” in the vocative.

7.23
After that [say] “news of the three worlds” and “tell” twice. This Great Vidyā ends in svāhā; it is declared a wish-fulfilling creeper.

7.24
Beloved, repeat one hundred and eight times daily for two years. Then the Great Vidyā becomes accomplished and tells the practitioner everything.

7.25
The devoted yogin, lying on the bed, should repeat the mantra one hundred and eight times. Day by day the practitioner knows the affairs concerning the world's welfare.

7.26
Śaṅkarī, auspicious Padmāvatī Vidyā tells in dreams the affairs of Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and the others, and of the three worlds.

7.27 — The Goddess said:
I have heard the holy practice spoken by Mahākāla. Now, Mahādeva, I wish to hear an excellent and great subjugation requiring little effort and producing swift accomplishment.

7.28 — Īśvara said:
At your request, Queen of the gods, I shall explain it: listen. Formerly, Goddess, concerning the arising of yogic knowledge, I told you—

7.29
—briefly; now, Goddess, I shall explain it fully. It must always be carefully concealed in a gathering of paśus.

Note: Verses 28–29 share one combined span in the printing. This division preserves the sequence of the sentence rather than asserting a new metrical reconstruction.

7.30
At night on a new-moon day falling on Tuesday, a person should write the enemy's name and place it beneath the left foot.

7.31
Over that foot, Queen of the gods, the discerning practitioner should repeat Vāgbhava one hundred and eight times. Then the adversary comes under control and becomes mute in dispute or litigation. As a serpent becomes motionless on seeing Garuḍa, so the opponent becomes motionless upon seeing [the practitioner]: there is no doubt.

7.32
I shall explain another excellent subjugation, through whose power one brings the world under control.

7.33
First utter praṇava, then “Sundari,” “Bhairavi,” and “Yogini,” Goddess; [then] “rulers, subjects, great warriors—”

7.34
“—subjugatress,” then aṃ, iṃ, uṃ, ṛ [the final seed spelling is uncertain]. The mantra is declared twenty-six-syllabled, a wish-fulfilling tree.

7.35
Goddess, with this mantra [consecrate] oil or sandalwood by one hundred and eight repetitions. Beautiful-faced one, apply that oil to the face.

7.36
Daughter of the mountain, make a forehead mark with that sandalwood. Having performed this rite of subjugating the world, the excellent practitioner—

7.37
—brings under control whomever he looks at, Goddess, without doubt. By this method one may enchant even the king of the gods; how much more human beings and universal monarchs!

7.38
Great Goddess, now another excellent subjugation is explained, the exceedingly wondrous enchantment of all worlds.

7.39
First I shall explain the mantra hidden in all Tantras.

7.40
First utter praṇava, then “Rājamukhi”; again “Rājamukhi,” then the Māyā seed twice—

7.41
—then the Kāma seed; then “Devi” twice, then “Mahādevi,” then “Devādhidevi”—

7.42
—these four vocative expressions, Queen of the gods. [Say] “Bring everyone's favourable regard under my control; do, do,” ending with svāhā. This great mantra grants subjugation of all.

Note on 7.40–42: These numbers are grouped in the source. Their subdivision here follows the formula's order. Sarvajanasya … abhimukham is provisionally understood as favourable regard or attention; a different segmentation is possible.

7.43
Great Lady, while on the bed in the morning, the skilled practitioner should wipe the face three times with the right hand using this mantra.

7.44
Beloved, do this daily for subjugating the world. This moving and unmoving universe certainly comes under control.

7.45 — The Goddess said:
Mahādeva, through your grace I have heard this ancient [teaching]. I have understood the Svapnavatī Vidyā that you explained.

7.46
Now I wish to hear whatever particular procedure applies. Mahādeva, if you favour me, explain it.

7.47 — Īśvara said:
Wise one, listen: I shall explain supreme Svapnavatī Vidyā. First utter praṇava and then extract the bride seed—

7.48
—after “Svapnāvati,” utter “tell in a dream”; then the Māyā seed and svāhā. Daughter of the mountain, this is the mantra. Having eaten sacrificial food by day, repeat it one thousand times at night.

Note: “Bride seed” is the encoded vadhūbīja; its exact expansion is not supplied conjecturally.

7.49
Then on a pure bed one sees in dreams, Supreme Lady, everything conceived in the mind.

7.50
Now I shall explain another excellent awakening through dreams. Merely through knowing it, one certainly knows everything.

7.51
Lotus-eyed one, first pronounce praṇava, then “hili … śūlapāṇaye” [the intervening syllable is uncertain], and svāhā at the end. This great mantra has been explained to you.

7.52
The entire procedure is as before. Hear the prayer at the end of repetition.

7.53
“Oṃ, homage to the three-eyed one, the tawny, the great-souled one, whose form is Vāmadeva, lord of dreams. Tell me in a dream the truth of every matter, auspicious or inauspicious.”

Note: The opening epithet is imperfectly recognized in the OCR, but “three-eyed” is supported by the Hindi gloss.

7.54
Having prayed with this mantra, one knows everything in truth. Goddess, thus I have explained unsurpassed understanding through dreams—

7.55
—the supreme and delightful secret, excellent subjugation: Great Goddess, all this bestows all knowledge.

7.56
[It is] continually served by the foremost accomplished beings. Through Madhumatī's grace [all that concerns] all births has been stated.

Textual caution: The last clause is poorly recovered and its referent is unclear. This is only a provisional rendering of the words visible in the text layer and gloss.

7.57
[They] seek [it], Supreme Lady; therefore one should resort to you. Beloved, the repetition prescribed for Svapnavatī and the other Vidyās—

Textual caution: The first sentence is uncertain; the Hindi instead refers to the Goddess having asked. Neither reading is presented as settled.

7.58
—[brings attainment] according to the stated number of years for one seeking accomplishment, Śāmbhavī. Without that repetition there is no attainment of the fruit, Goddess. Through the power of accomplished Vidyā the gods and antigods attain accomplishment.

Note: The negative is supported by the Hindi and context; the OCR's final construction remains uneven.

7.59
Thus I have properly explained the exceedingly wondrous secret. Conceal it in the presence of the malicious, the wicked, paśus, and the ignorant.

7.60
Whoever acts otherwise becomes food for the companies of Ḍākinīs. Therefore it must be concealed with every effort, especially [from them].

7.61
Give it to a peaceful, self-controlled yogin of good lineage, a devoted, sinless, great-souled practitioner.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the seventh chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 61 numbered units have draft entries. Some mantra syllables and several final clauses remain provisional, as marked. This is not an independently reviewed or fully collated translation.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 8
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition, 1984; printed pages 92–105, PDF pages 112–125. Sanskrit translated with consultation of the Hindi gloss. This chapter narrates the Goddess's conflict with Ghora and the emergence of the Yoginīs. Difficult readings and numbering defects are marked. The dialogue's social claims and supernatural events belong to the text.

8.1 — The Goddess said:
I have heard all the practices issuing from your lotus-mouth, attended by gods, Dānavas, Gandharvas, Siddhas, and Cāraṇas—

8.2
—abounding in supreme bliss, bestowing intense delight and powers, supreme, transcendent, exceedingly meritorious, purifying, and great.

8.3
Mahādeva, tell the origin of the Yoginīs, rare even in the three worlds, increasing unalloyed bliss.

8.4 — Īśvara said:
Auspicious one, have you forgotten all that formerly happened between us? [It is] exceedingly secret, supreme, terrifying to gods and antigods—

8.5
—ancient and to be concealed, essence beyond essence, higher than the highest. Lovely-limbed one, beloved of Śiva, listen: I shall tell it briefly.

8.6
Auspicious one, conceal it as one conceals one's intimate body from another man.

8.7
At the end of the universe's lifespan, when it was devoid of all beings, and only the five principles beginning with earth remained dissolved [in their source], auspicious one—

8.8
—there was nothing in the three worlds apart from you and me, Great Lady. At that moment I smiled and questioned you:

8.9
“Is the greater capacity mine, or yours, Great Lady? Now, Supreme Lady, the sphere of the universe—

8.10
—contains nowhere to stand. Where shall I stay, beautiful one? Everything I had made has passed away.

8.11
“Goddess, I am always detached from the business of worldly existence. Great Lady, now conceive a place for us to stay.”

Note on 8.9: The Hindi interprets the first question as conceding the Goddess's greater capacity. The Sanskrit comparison is rendered as a question rather than adopting that paraphrase as certain.

8.12
Hearing this, Goddess, your eyes reddened with anger, and you spoke harshly to me, terrible [in response to] misconduct:

8.13 — The Goddess's remembered speech:
“God, whatever you have done has always depended upon me. Mahādeva, without me you are certainly a corpse.

Note: The printed or OCR verse number repeats 12, but the sequence and Hindi support 13.

8.14
“In conjunction with you, Great Lord, I have extended all this, conceiving [you] in the form of my child. What capacity do you possess?

Note: The reading vatsarūpeṇa, “in the form of a child,” is supported by the Hindi but the syntax remains awkward.

8.15
“In the causal state I am always the form of the creator. Nothing is beyond my making; I am the imperishable Supreme.

8.16
“Entering the state of effect, I am always the form of Prakṛti. Then Brahmā and all the others appear.

8.17
“God, this moving and unmoving universe consists of my Māyā. Supreme Lord, [its activity proceeds through] projection and concealment.”

Note: Vikṣepa and āvaraṇa are the two powers recognized by the Hindi explanation; the Sanskrit extraction of the final phrase is defective.

8.18 — Īśvara continued:
Having heard your terrible, thunderbolt-like words, I said nothing, Goddess, and remained still.

8.19
Goddess, filled with inward pain, I settled in my heart upon a means of checking you.

8.20
I went to the western region of the universe, beautiful-faced one, and there, Great Goddess, in solitude, [created] a terrible [being]—

8.21
—from the ash of my own body: the king of Dānavas named Ghora, exceedingly dreadful, wondrous, never previously seen or heard of.

Note: The Sanskrit paragraph around 20–21 is unevenly numbered. The English assigns the narrative clauses in order, guided by the Hindi's combined reference to verses 20 and 21.

8.22
He extended ten million yojanas, with a breadth of thirty-two hundred thousand; exceedingly fearsome, he had ten million hands and ten million shining eyes—

8.23
—and fifty hundred thousand mouths, crowded with flames. Great Lady, having given him the great accomplishments beginning with aṇimā—

8.24
—and made that terrible being equal to me in every respect, I returned to you, Goddess, with delighted mind.

Note: Aṇimā is the power of becoming minute, conventionally the first in a list of extraordinary yogic powers. The cosmic dimensions are the narrative's measurements, not historical geography.

8.25
That Dānava king remained, having swallowed [an ocean: the extracted word is uncertain] and made the Suvela and Vela mountains into two mouthfuls [or rinses].

Textual caution: The names and the swallowing imagery are not securely recovered. The Hindi itself retains an unclear janārṇava and uses gaṇḍūṣa, a mouthful used for rinsing. This verse remains provisional.

8.26
Knowing my thought, you then said to me, “Now the vessel of the universe has become devoid of living beings.

8.27
“Mahādeva, auspicious one, give permission for me to see everything.” Then, smiling inwardly and increasing your eagerness—

8.28
—I said, “Auspicious one, come toward the western direction.” Queen of the gods, having seen everywhere else, you then followed me.

8.29
“Such is women's nature: wherever something lies below [or is restricted], they feel a great desire to see it and go there.”

Textual caution: The literal directional phrase and its intended implication are unclear. The sentence records a stereotyped claim of the speaker in the text; neither an endorsement nor an established generalization is intended.

8.30
Knowing this, auspicious one, I spoke words forbidding it. Then, from lack of a purpose and from your own disposition, auspicious one—

8.31
—you went nowhere else, Queen of the gods, remaining near me. [Then you went] to that great forest place where Kedārakeśvara is.

8.32
Having gone there, Great Goddess, enchantress of the world, you pierced the Daitya king with thousands of arrows of desire.

8.33
Then the Daitya king rose, pierced by the arrows of desire. Spreading out all his hands, he spoke many flattering words.

8.34 — Ghora said:
“Come into my embrace and become my sovereign lady. I am sunk in the ocean of desire: rescue me by giving yourself.

8.35
“Beautiful one, I cannot live even a little while without you. Embrace me as your husband and preserve my life.”

8.36 — Īśvara continued:
He repeatedly addressed you with these and other flattering words. Then, smiling and casting a sidelong glance, you said:

8.37 — The Goddess said:
“You are lord of all Daityas, enjoyer of everything; you are mighty among the hosts of gods. You are powerful and destroy all. I will choose you if you accomplish this [condition].

8.38
“Hear my story. Because of [my vow], I can settle nowhere. If you wish to take me, fulfil the vow I made before.

8.39
“My resolve is this, king of Daityas: whoever defeats me in battle will be my husband, and no other. Therefore first undertake battle with me.”

8.40 — Īśvara continued:
When you spoke thus, Goddess, he became greatly enraged and threatened you loudly, with a roar like the ocean at dissolution.

8.41
Then that Daitya rose, outdoing Kālarudra [in terror], and rushed forth to destroy the whole world.

Note: The middle compound is poorly recognized; the Hindi instead says he reviled Mahākāla Rudra. “Outdoing” is provisional and the alternative is recorded.

8.42
Yet he could not seize you in any way. Then that Dānava lord rushed with great speed—

8.43
—and by the touch of his hands mountains were reduced to powder; struck by his feet, they fell and sank into the ocean.

8.44
The wind produced by his body threw up the circle of waters in exceedingly dreadful waves, as far as the upper and lower limits of the cosmic shell.

8.45
Pervading the universe, he roamed continually. Mahāmāyā, though wishing to seize you, he could not catch you.

8.46
The foremost Daitya saw you only ahead of him. Whatever battles he fought cannot be described.

8.47
Auspicious one, whatever he hurled at you became ashes through your radiance. Great Lady, even then he was overcome by rage—

8.48
—and Ghora, the Daitya of terrible prowess, continued. Queen of the gods, hear this wondrous and splendid account of battle.

8.49
Much dust arose from the water-born cosmic shell. Thus, beginning with a single day, the battle lasted ten million years.

Note: The image of dust rising from the water-born shell is retained rather than normalized into a physical explanation.

8.50
Great Lady, during that battle I was troubled by fear. Resorting to yoga, I made my body exceedingly subtle, Supreme Lady, and remained sheltered in you.

8.51
Unable to seize you by any means, the Daitya king considered different ways of killing you.

8.52
“Enlarging my body and pressing [her] with my arm, I shall kill you within the shell, wicked one.”

Note: The enemy's threatening words are reproduced as narrative speech. The precise verb for pressure or crushing is uncertain in the OCR.

8.53
Thinking thus, he expanded his body until the universe was filled by him, and Ghora rejoiced.

8.54
Then the Daitya said to you, “Where will you go now, doomed one?” Seeing the Daitya filling the universe—

8.55
—you said, “Ghora Daitya, stand, stand, foolish one! Why are you agitated? I shall kill you effortlessly.

8.56
“Greatly wicked one, even now you do not know me. Creation arises from me and dissolves into me.

8.57
“By me all this is sustained; the world consists of my Māyā. Nothing exists apart from me: I am eternal Brahman.

8.58
“Foremost fool, hear my supreme, great auspiciousness. Whether with hostility, Daitya, or with virtuous devotion—

8.59
“—in whatever manner people serve me, I grant their corresponding desire. In the end I bestow the unsurpassed great fruit.

8.60
“Daitya, you have served me for a long time: there is no doubt. With concentrated mind you have striven to reach me.

8.61
“You are Śiva, without doubt, because of the effort you have made for me. Now behold my form, the supreme state of the bliss of Brahman.

8.62
“[The vision of] that supreme form, contemplated as Śiva, [is exceedingly rare; the remainder of the sentence is uncertain].

Textual caution: The Hindi and extracted Sanskrit do not yield a secure common reading. This is not treated as a settled complete translation.

8.63
“Hear: that supreme abode and form is the form of Kālī. Nowhere is there a form of Brahman higher than this.”

8.64 — Īśvara continued:
Having spoken thus, Goddess, Bhavānī who liberates all contemplated that supreme form, saying “I am Kālī”—

8.65
—and then, Supreme Lady, you became Kālikā: dark in colour, exceedingly terrible, standing upon Mahākāla—

8.66
—lovely with garlands of severed heads, loose-haired, smiling, with lolling tongue, adorned with three terrifying red eyes—

8.67
—radiant with a crown gleaming with the amā lunar digit; [surrounded by] countless thousands of female jackals born from the circle of radiance—

8.68
—roaring mightily in every direction, of terrible prowess. From clusters of rays arose countless millions of Yoginīs—

8.69
—terrible in form all around, eager for the great battle. In every hair-pore countless millions of universes shone continually, Goddess, all filled with solar light.

Note on 8.67–69: The syntax runs across the numbers. The immediate referent of “all filled with solar light” is not entirely secure; the Hindi relates it to the Yoginīs, while the Sanskrit also permits attachment to the universes. The cosmic image is retained without claiming one reading as definitive.

8.70
Seeing Kālikā thus, the Dānava lord fainted. Beholding Mahākālī's radiant face, he became delighted.

Note: Pratīta, “pleased/delighted,” is recovered from the alternate Mishra scan, printed page104; the supplied OCR obscures the initial consonant.

8.71
Immediately the Dānava lord attained knowledge of Brahman. Then Kālī, with her lolling tongue, drew that pure, knowledgeable devotee, the Dānava lord, into the midst of battle.

8.72
The Mother chewed him together with the universe and in a moment made him dead. Then Kālī sported in a great festival of terrible instrumental music, with many great instruments and banners spread abroad.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the eighth chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 72 numbered units have English entries. Verses 8.25, 8.29–31, 8.41, 8.62, 8.67–69 and several smaller expressions remain provisional. This is a reading draft, not a fully collated scholarly edition.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 9
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition, 1984, printed pages105–118 (PDF125–138). Sanskrit translated with the Hindi gloss consulted. Verse numbering follows the sequence where OCR misreads numerals. Several difficult clauses remain marked; this is a draft, not an independently reviewed critical translation.

9.1 — Īśvara said:
Seeing that great marvel, my mind overwhelmed with fear, I suddenly went into that excellent wilderness.

9.2
Goddess, having gone there by the path of Suṣumṇā, I cannot describe everything that was revealed and heard.

9.3
Goddess, all was wondrous, never seen or heard anywhere: innumerable exceedingly vast universes—

9.4
—move there continually; who could count them? Goddess, with innumerable millions of faces and arms—

9.5
—Brahmās, Viṣṇus, Śivas, and the others, dwelling in each universe, possess great sovereignty and take many different forms.

9.6
Goddess, seeing this universal marvel my mind was bewildered; everything was forgotten, and I wondered, “Who am I?”

9.7
“Who am I? Where have I come? Whom should I ask, and where?” Thus, Goddess, I remained forgetful of the world in many ways.

9.8
Wandering through many places, I remembered nothing. Then, after ten million years, I reached the lotus of your heart.

9.9
Supreme Lady, I cannot describe all the excellent marvels I saw upon going there.

9.10
[There I saw] the scripture from which understanding of existence arises, the cause of happiness and liberation; the supreme Self, Āgama, the Vedas, the individual self, philosophical vision, and the senses—

9.11
—the body, Purāṇa, its limbs, the Smṛtis and rules; all the scriptures were there, beautiful-faced one, [as] hairs and the other [parts].

Note: The relation of the listed subjects to the Goddess's bodily or lotus imagery is compressed in these verses; brackets mark supplied connections.

9.12
As there are distinctions of individual selves, so also among the limbs of the Veda: at the tips, centres, and ends of the petals in the heart-lotus—

9.13
—I saw an auspicious succession of letters, made of intense radiance: phonetics, ritual procedure, grammar, etymology, and metre—

9.14
—and the other scriptures, whatever lesser ones there are. But through seeing them completely I came to know [them]; I tell you this.

Note: The Hindi includes astronomy/astrology as the sixth Vedāṅga, though it is not securely recognized in this Sanskrit extraction. The list is not silently supplemented in the running translation.

9.15
Then, Goddess, I perceived the exceedingly brilliant centre of the lotus, shining like countless millions of suns and moons—

9.16
—adorned with circles of the radiance of innumerable great fires. Within it I saw an exceedingly brilliant mass of letters—

9.17
—luminous as ten million suns, cool as ten million moons, blazing as ten million fires, constant and consisting of supreme Brahman—

9.18
—Goddess, consisting of all knowledge, always of every marvel, every sacrifice, and every pilgrimage place—

9.19
—consisting of all merit and every dharma, knowledge of Brahman and the bliss of Brahman—

9.20
—the authority of all scriptures beginning with the Vedas, Great Lady, the authority of all principles: that supreme radiance of Brahman—

9.21
—outside all Māyā, cutting away all Māyā, consisting of every bliss and always the bliss of Brahman—

9.22
—full of perfect bliss, excellent liberation in Brahman; Goddess, yet consisting of all Māyā and all Vidyās—

9.23
—consisting of all austerities, all accomplishments, all liberation, and all Vedas—

9.24
—consisting of all worlds, all enjoyments, all scriptures, and every yoga, Goddess.

9.25
Seeing that Āgama there, the night in the dark ocean of my ignorance passed away, and I saw [knowledge] bright as sunrise.

9.26
Through Mahākālī's grace I studied everything. Having seen it all, I mastered it at that instant: there is no doubt.

9.27
Then, having gone among the clusters of lotus filaments, Goddess, I saw masses of letters shining like sunlight—

9.28
—Nyāya, Mīmāṃsā, Sāṃkhya, the Pātañjala [system], and Vaiśeṣika. I knew them immediately as before.

9.29
Then at the edge of the lotus's centre I saw a succession of letters, brilliant as a hundred suns and delighting everyone.

9.30
I studied Āyurveda and the physician's Veda then. Within [that region], Great Goddess, I saw another auspicious succession of letters—

Note: The apparent distinction between Āyurveda and bhiṣagveda is retained; it is not assumed to name two independently established canonical Vedas.

9.31
—pure and exceedingly radiant, resembling a thousand suns. Queen of the gods, [I studied] Smṛti, Itihāsa, and the Purāṇas—

9.32
—I mastered all of them instantly, without doubt. Yet the bewilderment of my embodied condition was not cleared.

9.33
Then, Goddess, I saw knowledge of Brahman, bright as ten million suns, surrounded by the radiance of Brahman—

9.34
—in a supremely radiant mass of letters, renowned as Vedānta. I studied it immediately [and encountered that great state beyond delusion: final clause uncertain].

9.35
Within it I saw a brilliant mass of letters, bright as ten million suns and cool as ten million moons—

9.36
—consisting of all knowledge, all pilgrimage places, all sacrifices, and every dharma, Goddess—

9.37
—the authority of all beings and scriptures, Great Lady: the excellent four Vedas, Sāma, Atharva, Ṛg, and Yajur.

9.38
I mastered all of them immediately: there is no doubt. Yet even then satisfaction did not arise in me.

9.39
I [became] composed of all knowledge, all beings, and every accomplishment.

9.40
Then [I saw] you, eternal Kālī, honoured by those [scriptures], dancing with female jackals and Yoginīs, your form Brahman itself.

9.41
Pausing repeatedly before me, you showed your radiant face. Then, having risen and flown, I reached the two-petalled [lotus]—

9.42
—the Ājñā wheel between Mahākālī's eyebrows, Great Lady. There I remembered Brahmā and Viṣṇu.

9.43
Great Lady, while Kālī was dancing, two drops of sweat fell from her chin. From those two arose the two endowed with qualities—

9.44
—Brahmā and Viṣṇu. Seeing [her], their bodies trembled with fear, and they quickly went to the two nostril openings.

Note: The Hindi reads movement out through the nostrils; the Sanskrit phrase allows going to them. The subsequent verse describes the two channels, so the exact direction is not supplied as certain.

9.45
Then, Great Lady, Brahmā [went] into Kālī's Piṅgalā, and Viṣṇu into Iḍā. Having gone there, the two auspicious ones—

9.46
—became greatly bewildered by seeing many marvels. Continually weeping, Goddess, [they forgot what would happen: final expression uncertain].

9.47
Thus those two lords, distressed by great sorrow, wept and ran here and there.

9.48
Knowing this, Great Lady, I first went to Viṣṇu's abode. I gave him knowledge and the supremely auspicious mantra.

9.49
Immediately he became equal to me, [remaining] at my left side. I gave him all the scriptures, apart from Āgama, merely through speech.

9.50
Mahāviṣṇu, mounted on Garuḍa, became joyful and flourishing. Taking him with me, Supreme Lady, I went into the universe [of Brahmā]—

9.51
—and there gave [Brahmā] the supremely wondrous mantra. Great Goddess, immediately the Grandfather became greatly knowledgeable.

9.52
He became equal to me, [remaining] at my right side. Supreme Lady, at my command Viṣṇu then gave him all the scriptures and the Vedic teachings.

Note: “Right side” is supplied from the Hindi where the OCR of the Sanskrit is defective.

9.53
Freed from distress, Brahmā became joyful and flourishing. Thus you are always my primordial guru.

9.54
Having accepted that knowledge, Great Lady, I went with the two of them, following Kālī by those paths.

9.55
Absorbed in her great dance with the female jackals and Yoginīs, she, whose nature is supreme, danced for a hundred koṭis of divine years.

9.56
Delighted by many kinds of instruments and singing, adorned in many ways, [she sported] with marvellous flowers like the moon, sun, and fire—

9.57
—with flowers rising and falling, with divine fragrances; Goddess, beyond the range of sight, she danced continually in solitude.

Note: The relation of “beyond the range of sight” to the Goddess or flowers is uncertain. The imagery and sequence are retained without asserting a definitive attachment.

9.58
She was adorned with many marvellous and beautiful banners resembling hundreds of universes.

9.59
Then we undertook to praise dreadful Kālī: eyes filled with tears, voices faltering, heads bowed, and hands joined.

9.60
First Brahmā praised her devotedly with every scripture. After ten million years, Great Lady, the Supreme said to him:

9.61 — The Goddess said to Brahmā:
“Creator, you possess qualities and know the meaning of all scriptures. You know how to investigate [or connect their principles]; therefore be the creator for me.”

9.62 — Īśvara continued:
Thus commanded, the Creator was fulfilled. Then Mahāviṣṇu praised her with every Veda, Śāmbhavī.

9.63
After ten koṭis of years, Śivā said to him: “Mahāviṣṇu, you know the Vedas; you are my devotee, abode of virtues, and knower of dharma. Be the world's preserver and increase creation.”

Note: The Sanskrit time expression is imperfectly recognized; the Hindi gives ten koṭis of years, followed here provisionally. A koṭi is ten million.

9.64
Receiving that command upon his head, the benefactor of the world was fulfilled. Then I [praised] supreme, eternal Kālī, eternal Brahman—

9.65
—with highest devotion through Āgama, Great Lady. After twenty koṭis of years she spoke to me.

9.66 — Kālī said:
“Sadāśiva, you know Āgama; you are greatly wise, beyond Māyā, endowed with qualities, and a great yogin. Become the destroyer of creation.”

9.67 — Īśvara continued:
Receiving the command upon my head, I praised her again. After five koṭis of divine years, she said to me:

9.68 — Kālī said:
“Sadāśiva, you have praised me through Āgama; I am pleased with you. Mahādeva, what do you ask? I shall grant it, without doubt.”

9.69 — Īśvara said:
“Mother, may I always remain at your lotus feet.”

9.70 — Kālī said:
“Whoever will fight me with even a fraction of a fraction of the battle I fought with the Dānava named Ghora—

9.71
“—will be born from a buffalo cow's womb, arising from your seed, Lord of the gods, and bear the name Mahiṣāsura. Having assumed an Asura disposition, you will wage a great battle.

Note: The switch between the third-person demon and second-person Śiva is explicit in the passage; the deity-demon identification is not silently removed.

9.72
“Then, having destroyed him in the form of Bhadrakālī, I shall place the big toe of my left lotus-foot upon your heart.

9.73
“Now, Mahādeva, always remain beneath my feet in the form of a corpse: become my seat.”

9.74 — Īśvara continued:
Thus commanded by the Great Goddess, I fell near her feet. A hundred thousand years passed in prostration, stretched upon the ground.

9.75
There Kālī disappeared, her nature consciousness, indivisible Brahman. Thus I have explained to you in detail the origin of the Yoginīs. Carefully conceal this excellent means of practice for all.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the ninth chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses]. The numeral in this colophon is misrecognized as 5 by the OCR; the Sanskrit chapter-name and sequence identify it as ninth.

EDITORIAL STATUS
All 75 numbered units have draft entries. Uncertain clauses are explicitly marked. This is not an independently reviewed or fully collated translation.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 10
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition,1984; printed pages118–128 (PDF138–148). Sanskrit translated with the Hindi gloss as an aid. Apparent textual problems are marked. This is a reading draft, not a critical edition.

10.1 — Īśvara said:
Rising again, Goddess, I no longer saw her. Greatly distressed, sunk in the ocean of sorrow, [we lamented]:

10.2
“Alas, Mother! Your lotus-face puts ten million moons and suns to shame. What splendour belongs to your two feet, an ocean of compassion!

10.3
“How vast your supreme form, enchanting even Mahāmāyā! What supremely auspicious radiance shines from the moons of your nails!

10.4
“Your face shines with countless millions of moons. What has happened, Mother? What will happen? Where has that wonder gone?

10.5
“Alas, Mother, that form is existence, consciousness, and bliss, imperishable. We no longer see her as our mother.”

10.6
Like children deprived of mother and father, we all wept and wept.

10.7
Goddess, Supreme Lady, lotus-eyed one, we passed five hundred thousand years in these and many other lamentations.

10.8
Weeping, falling and rising again, we cried loudly: “Mother, Mother, where have you gone? What will become of us?

10.9
“Why have you created us and then, unconcerned, left us in this difficult ocean? Mother, we are your helpless children. Alas, have you no compassion?

10.10
“If you do not protect us, who else will? Apart from you we have neither mother nor father.

10.11
“Without seeing you we shall die: this is certainly true. How can a child without mother and father live?

10.12
“Mother, consider it yourself.” Seeing our hearts despondent, she became compassionate. That Yoginī spoke words pouring forth great nectar.

10.13 — Kālī said:
“Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Maheśvara, do not be troubled by fear. Gods, I always remain: they call me eternal and imperishable.

10.14
“I am existence, consciousness, and bliss; I am Brahman, shining radiance. I am never destroyed. Remain free from doubt.

10.15
“Contemplate that pure and supreme form of mine which you saw, and repeat my mantra.

10.16
“Then great auspiciousness and radiance will arise for you. Viṣṇu, now enter Brahmā's body and remain steady.

10.17
“Maheśa, Lord of gods, enter Brahmā's body too. [Remain until he brings forth] this creation made of knowledge and activity.”

Note: The syntax of the command and the duration clause is uneven; the bracketed connection follows the Hindi gloss provisionally.

10.18 — Īśvara continued:
Śāmbhavī, having spoken thus, Mahākālī gave us the powers of will, knowledge, and action, accomplishing every purpose.

10.19
Will was given to Viṣṇu, action to Brahmā, and knowledge to me; she herself is the form of all powers.

10.20
Then Mahākālī said—listen, Supreme Lady—“I enter all of you, and in Śaṅkara [I enter] in my complete form.

10.21
“This Śiva is the divine guru, the Supreme Lord. He is the speaker of the scriptures; neither the Creator nor Hari nor any other is [their speaker].

10.22
“I am the śrotriyā of all of you, without doubt. I shall enchant Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Maheśvara.”

Note: Śrotriyā ordinarily denotes a woman learned in Vedic revelation; its exact intended force here may depend on a variant reading. The Hindi gives “female Veda-reciter.” It is retained rather than expanded into a new doctrine.

10.23 — Īśvara continued:
Having said this, Mahākālī entered us. Then I too entered the bodies of Mādhava and Brahmā.

10.24
She then deluded Brahmā, whose form is Brahman. Then Brahmā, knowing himself, offered [to] the imperishable [Goddess] himself—

Textual caution: The sacrificial construction and the first two pronouns are difficult in the extracted Sanskrit. This is provisional, not a securely reconstructed ritual statement.

10.25
—and became renowned as “Self-born”: thus it is declared, without doubt. Troubled by thoughts of “What shall I do? Where shall I go?”—

10.26
—the Creator remained thus for a year. At the beginning he created water, all-pervading, supreme, and vast.

10.27
That water, associated with identification with the qualities, was the mighty causal ocean. Remaining there, he created an accumulation of golden seed.

10.28
That seed arose as a multitude of bubbles nearby; each is called a universe, floating on the causal ocean.

10.29
Goddess, I continually carry out the protection and separation of those universes, myself bearing the form of Rudra.

10.30
Great Goddess, auspicious one, I always stand beside each universe as a distinct Rudra, trident in hand.

10.31
Remaining on that causal ocean, I continually act so that two universes do not come into conjunction.

10.32
Thus all of us, Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Maheśvara, are established within each universe: there is no doubt.

10.33
Then Brahmā, creator of the world, entering each universe in a distinct form, [creates] the other four elements—

10.34
—earth, fire, air, and space. With these five principles he makes the whole creation.

Note: Water, created earlier, completes the five. “Elements” renders tattvas in this context without assuming modern chemical elements.

10.35
Then the blessed Viṣṇu, by his own will, emerges from Brahmā's body and continually preserves that [creation].

10.36
Goddess, by the Creator's command, in each universe [I am established] separately in Viṣṇu's mighty-armed form—

10.37
—and within each universe I destroy again and again. Supreme Lady, I myself take refuge in Brahmā's body.

Note: The syntax of 36–37 is imperfectly recognized. The Hindi supplies preservation as Viṣṇu and destruction as Rudra; that wider sense is compatible with the narrative, but the unclear Sanskrit is not silently rewritten.

10.38
Great Lady, do you remember none of this? [The world's source] knows that all this activity is mine.

Note: The final expression jagannidhiḥ is provisionally rendered “the world's source”; the Hindi instead says that the world rests in the speaker. The exact clause needs collation.

10.39
All the principles beginning with water, the entire expansion of the universes, all gods, the directions and intermediate directions, and the moving and unmoving—

10.40
—effect and cause, Goddess, and Viṣṇu's arising: Brahmā knows all this, made through my Māyā.

10.41
Yet all of it is unreal, Goddess: it is only the Mother's Māyā. Those who worship that Māyā go beyond it.

10.42
Pleased, that supreme Māyā grants liberation; displeased, that supreme Māyā grants connection with the earthly world [renewed birth].

Note: The extracted bhūmiyoga is interpreted as womb-birth in the Hindi. The English gives the literal earthly connection and records the interpretive sense.

10.43
Goddess, liberation rests obediently at her lotus feet. Great Lady, for whomever the Great Goddess, my mother, is pleased—

10.44
—to that person Mahāmāyā grants liberation, Śaṅkarī.

10.45 — The Goddess said:
Lord, through your grace I have heard everything: the unheard supreme principle beyond even Brahmā and the others.

10.46
Now I wish to hear about the causal ocean you described. Mahādeva, on what does it rest, and what is the support of that support?

10.47
Lord of gods, if you have affection for me, tell its boundary.

10.48 — Īśvara said:
Goddess, listen: I shall explain the auspicious [truth], more secret than the secret, exceedingly concealed, beyond Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and the others.

10.49
The great ocean is Mahākāla, Maheśvara, Goddess. For play, [the Mother] conceived a husband in the form of emptiness.

10.50
She herself is Kālī, Mother of the universe; becoming equal to Mahākāla, she sustains Mahākāla as a form of half the radiance.

Note: Mahākālatulā and ardhatejasī are uncertain in this printing. The expression “half the radiance” follows the Hindi, but the precise cosmological relation remains provisional.

10.51
She is conceived as empty in form, dark in colour, with [upward/half] radiance. Goddess, the boundary you asked about is she herself, Brahman alone.

Note: The adjective recognized as ūrdhvatejasī conflicts with the preceding possible ardhatejasī. Both possibilities are recorded rather than harmonized without evidence.

10.52
The radiance of Brahman is light itself, the form of illumination. Great Goddess, its shining is free from the distinction of the pervaded and the pervader.

10.53
It is neither the supported nor the support, continuously without a second. Goddess, all this [manifest world] is made of Māyā.

10.54
Everything is unreal, Goddess; Brahman alone is true. Thus I have told you the essence beyond essence, higher than the highest—

10.55
—the secret beyond the secret, exceedingly hidden, Great Lady. This is supreme knowledge, cutting away all Māyā.

10.56
[This discernment], consisting of all knowledge, has the form of the great sun. [Through] countless millions of great gifts and great austerities—

10.57
—countless millions of great knowledges, great vows, and immersions at great pilgrimage places, sovereign Lady—

10.58
—after many births have passed, if someone at last hears it, that person is liberated, Goddess, and is not born in worldly existence again.

Note: The construction spanning 56–58 is damaged. The Hindi additionally interprets the knowledge as superior to the listed gifts and disciplines. The temporal connection is retained provisionally, with that alternative noted.

10.59
Thus it has been explained to you. Conceal it as your own intimate body. Carefully act so that no other obtains it.

10.60
Conceal it, conceal it, conceal it carefully. My excellent all is to be carefully concealed.

10.61
Give it to the peaceful, steadfast yogin, Kula yogin, and knower, Queen of gods. Otherwise one goes to hell.

10.62
Goddess, whose eyes dart like wagtails, I have told you the essential knowledge of Brahman. What further do you wish to hear?

10.63
Nothing higher than this exists in my mind. Auspicious one, always conceal it in the presence of paśus and the ignorant.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the tenth chapter, with the vernacular commentary, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All63 numbered units have draft entries. Textual uncertainties are marked rather than concealed; no independent scholarly review is claimed.

YOGINĪ TANTRA — FIRST PART, CHAPTER 11
English working translation

Source: Mishra edition,1984; printed pages129–141 (PDF149–161). Sanskrit translated with the Hindi gloss consulted.
