That Bhairava [obtained] the Great Vidyā from the radiant mouth of the true guru Mahākāla Śrībhairava, Ādinātha, auspicious one.
19.11
Merely receiving that mantra, Karāla Bhairava purified himself and became higher than all, released from worldly existence and honoured by gods beginning with Brahmā.
19.12
First he performed exceedingly difficult austerity on Udayācala, Goddess, standing with feet upward for two niyutas of years.
19.13
A hundred thousand [years] in the Himalaya, a hundred thousand on Mandara, a hundred thousand in Kanaka's hermitage, and two hundred thousand in Uḍḍīyāna—
19.14
—and twenty-five hundred thousand in Jālandhara, auspicious one, and likewise twenty-five hundred thousand on the holy mountain—
19.15
—and twenty-five hundred thousand in Kāmākhyā's yoni-region: thus he performed terrible, exceedingly difficult austerity for ten million years.
19.16
Despite Karāla Bhairava's great hardship, Goddess, skull-bearing Kālī was not pleased with him.
19.17
Then Bhairava went to the guru and related the full account of his austerity, auspicious one.
19.18
Hearing it, glorious Ādinātha, Mahākāla Maheśvara, my own Lord, Supreme Lady, fell into contemplation:
19.19
“Without the supreme observance accomplishment does not arise. How shall I briefly explain that supreme observance to you?
19.20
“It belongs to Kālī's heart, secret beyond the secret, inaccessible to Brahmā and the others—
19.21
“—directly giving supreme liberation and granting every accomplishment. This manifest seed of Kālī is my entire treasure.
19.22
“Merely through its being spoken and heard, one becomes equal to Śiva, a peaceful great yogin. Therefore it has been carefully concealed.
19.23
“For observance a portion was entrusted to Kālarudra. This is my whole treasure; [having obtained or concealed it] one becomes free from agitation. Perform that supreme observance in utmost secrecy.
Note: The grammar of the middle expression is unclear; the Hindi takes it as receipt of the teaching bringing composure.
19.24
“Through obtaining the true observance and knowledge of reality, the greatly intelligent person becomes lord of all, honoured by the world, Sadāśiva, free from ageing and death, greatly peaceful, supreme Lord.
19.25
“Seeing someone constantly peaceful and devoted to true observance, I give that person my own station and guruhood—
19.26
“—and always dwell joyfully in that person's body. I dwell in the supreme thousand-petalled lotus of everyone.
19.27
“I always remain in my own Śivahood. Śiva, Śiva am I, Śiva: no distinction exists anywhere, ever.
19.28
“Where the guru of Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva and the others [abides], there is Śiva. That peaceful great yogin is capable of the observance.
19.29
“No one else is ever capable of that true observance. Only through Śiva's grace is a practitioner capable.
19.30
“Girijā the Goddess is capable, and no one else. Karāla Bhairava is always fierce in disposition and terrible in deeds.
19.31
“How am I able to preserve that supreme observance [in secrecy with him]? As Kālikā is my object of worship, so is this observance.
19.32
“I may be fit to give the worship-worthy queen of Vidyās; I am not fit to give the supreme observance dear to Kālī's heart.”
19.33
Thinking thus, Ādinātha commanded Karāla Bhairava: “Child, go to the Three-eyed one.”
19.34
There the compassionate Goddess stood mounted upon Śiva. Bhairava himself then approached Mother Kālikā.
19.35
Abandoning all austerity, he reflected inwardly: “Whatever I repeated or practised in austerity has all become fruitless.
19.36
“Through my misfortune even the guru's command has failed. Therefore I shall abandon this body; I will not bear it any longer.”
19.37
Having made this resolve, he prepared to relinquish the body. Then a lovely voice arose from the sky:
19.38
“Greatly fortunate, great-souled Bhairava, listen. I shall state the command: hear and make it an ornament for your ears.
19.39
“Foremost Bhairava, your guru's command has seemed fruitless because you performed austerity continually according to your own understanding, without the observance.
19.40
“How shall I give you accomplishment or a vision? Go again to Mahākāla Bhairava, destroyer of worldly existence—
19.41
“—your guru Ādinātha, and relate this account. Then he will certainly give you the great observance.
19.42
“God, worship me through that observance. I shall soon bestow everything in your mind.
19.43
“Whoever repeats the great mantras of Kālī and Tārā [or a beneficial mantra: additional phrase uncertain] without Kula observance certainly goes to hell.
19.44
“How could accomplishment arise for that person? Liberation remains far away.”
19.45
Receiving this command of Mahākālī, excellent Bhairava went again to the glorious Lord, mighty master Ādinātha—
19.46
—Mahākāla, Mahādeva, whose form is emptiness, guru of the world, and related all the words that had been spoken.
19.47
“Mother Mahākālī has now shown grace to Karāla Bhairava.” Thinking thus, the guru whose nature is emptiness rejoiced.
19.48
Then, according to the prescribed rule, [he explained the observance to] Karāla Bhairava.
Note: The extracted Sanskrit's verb and subject are uneven; the Hindi and the following verses support the guru's instruction.
19.49
Mahākāla, lord of the world, consecrated him in the supremely hidden Kula observance taught in the Kālī Tantra—
19.50
—with the Śākta consecration and full consecration, and gave Karāla the renowned name Krodhavaktra.
19.51
Teaching the divine disposition and then the heroic disposition, Mahākāla, the guru himself whose form is emptiness, gave him liquor, fish, mudrā food and a vessel filled with kāraṇa.
Note: Kāraṇa is a ritual designation for the consecrated substance, often liquor in this context; its technical force is retained.
19.52
Then this entire moving and unmoving universe became beautiful. Krodhavaktra bowed to Mahākālī's lotus-feet—
19.53
—and, Goddess, [remembering] Mahākāla and the guru's sandals, accepted the kāraṇa with the appropriate disposition and at the guru's command.
Note: The name Mahākāla in the Sanskrit extraction may be a vocative or a subject; the Hindi gives Mahākāla himself as accepting the substance. The running rendering leaves the connection explicit but provisional.
19.54
Intelligent, radiant, filled with supreme bliss, the lord of wrath plunged into an ocean of delight, Goddess—
19.55
—and repeatedly bowed to the feet of Śakti and the guru, Great Lady, then praised with highest devotion while still in the ocean of worldly existence.
19.56 — Krodhavaktra Bhairava said:
“I bow to the Lord, wish-fulfilling tree of the gods, great descent of consciousness and bliss, eternal knowledge, infinite in form, higher than the highest, whose nature is Brahman and Śiva—
19.57
“—dwelling of the world, primordial root of the world, unknown, one, united with the supreme Self, luminous, partless reality, free of action, supreme and stainless—
19.58
“—without manifestation, wholly complete, without beginning or end, beyond Prakṛti, formless form, evident truth, embodiment of compassion, whose nature is emptiness—
19.59
“—seed that destroys beginningless worldly existence, supreme, pure, beyond [ordinary] perception: I always bow to the guru called Śiva, whose manifestation is the mantra-name alone.
19.60
“All knowers of the primordial behold you as smaller than the small and greater than the great. The eye born of knowledge is true; Lord, [its possessor] has truly entered you.
19.61
“I honour the primordial, ancient Person, boat upon the ocean of insubstantial worldly existence. You alone are Kālī's ultimate seed. I bow to your lotus-feet.
19.62
“Whoever, devoted practitioner, praises you with this hymn in morning, midday and evening: become the giver of liberation to that person.”
19.63 — Mahākāla Bhairava said:
“Child, I am always pleased by this hymn of yours. Quickly go to the yoni-seat, taking refuge upon the Goddess's summit.
19.64
“Worship Kālī, devoted to the disposition of Kula observance and courtesans. Soon your desired accomplishment will arise, without doubt.
19.65
“‘When shall I see the heroic practitioner among courtesans?’ Thus Kālī speaks. Therefore be devoted to courtesans.
19.66
“In mantra observance there is never a defect [on that account]. Therefore abandon delusion and take refuge in Kula dharma.
19.67
“Do not harbour delusion there, because it causes loss of accomplishment. If the mind becomes pure, accomplishment stands nearby.”
19.68 — Īśvara continued:
Then, joyfully bowing in many ways to Mahākālī and the guru, he reached the yoni-seat and took refuge upon the Goddess's summit.
19.69
Devoted to the five courtesans Urvaśī, Menakā, Rambhā, Pañcacūḍā and Tilottamā, following Kula observance, the lord of wrath repeated the Great Vidyā with purified disposition—
19.70
—the queen of Vidyās, Ghorakālī Aniruddhasarasvatī. Through one hundred and eight [repetitions] she became directly manifest to him.
19.71
Eternal, terrible-faced Kālī, whose form is radiance, continually enveloped the universe with her light.
19.72
Seeing that immense radiance, Bhairava became overwhelmed by fear and helpless; fainting, he fell from the mountain to the earth.
19.73
Then Kālī, Mother of the world, an ocean of compassion, reassured that wrathful one with nectar-like speech.
19.74
Bhairava rose, hair standing on end, and praised the Goddess with supreme devotion through many procedures. Bowing repeatedly, he then spoke to Kālī:
19.75 — Krodhavaktra said:
“Supreme Brahman, supreme abode, supreme Self, eternal one, Goddess of my mind: always grant everything I desire.”
19.76 — Kālī said:
“Excellent Bhairava, become the perpetual governor of Brahmā, Viṣṇu and the others, and of the inhabitants of the universes.
19.77
“Child, whoever worships me through Kula observance becomes my child: truly, truly, without doubt.
19.78
“Receive the face I shall give you, bringing fear to the universe. That face will be Time's form, supremely subduing all.”
19.79 — Īśvara continued:
Giving him that face, Śivā Mahākālī became tranquil for a moment. Bhairava also became a thunderbolt-bearer.
19.80
He obtained the form of Karāla Bhairava as Krodhavaktra. Through Mahākālī's grace the thunderbolt-bearer was called Lord.
19.81
Thus I have related the First Part of the illustrious Yoginī Tantra. Auspicious one, always conceal it as one's intimate body is concealed from another man.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Chapter 19 of the First Part of the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara, with the Hindi commentary by Kanhaiyalal Mishra of Moradabad.
The First Part of the Yoginī Tantra is concluded.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 1
Kāmarūpa and the sacred seats: English working translation
Source: Mishra edition (1984), printed pages 239–249; PDF pages 259–269. Sanskrit primary, with the Hindi gloss consulted. Place-names and traditional distances are retained; uncertain OCR and conflicting readings are flagged. This is a provisional translation, not a surveyed map or critical edition.

II.1.1 — Invocation
Homage to Kāmākhyā. May that supreme light, Māyā, forever protect the worlds: she manifests the three worlds through knowledge, through the inherent modes of primordial substance, support, and differentiated existence.
Note: The opening compound is philosophically compressed; its syntactic division remains provisional.

II.1.2 — Kālī said:
Beloved of my life, I have heard of the seats beginning with Uḍḍīyāna. Now I wish to hear the determination of Kāmarūpa.

II.1.3
Lord, you described the seat named Kāmākhyā, renowned in the Kali age, whose proclamation destroys terrible sin.

II.1.4
God, sovereign Lord, tell me that supreme remedy which breaks the manifold fangs of the serpent Kali.

II.1.5 — The Blessed Lord said:
Queen of the gods, Uḍḍīyāna appeared in the Kṛta age; Pūrṇaśaila arose at the beginning of the Tretā age—

II.1.6
—Jālaśaila in Dvāpara, and Kāmākhyā in the Kali age, Great Lady, for the destruction of the terrible sins of Kali.

II.1.7
In each region there are two seats and subsidiary seats, three great fields, and three holy forests.
Note: The extracted pratīvarṣa may be “in each varṣa [region]” rather than “each year”; the geographical list supports region.

II.1.8
At every seat are Mahādeva and the four-armed Lord; at every seat the Gaṅgā is established, and Pārvatī at every seat.

II.1.9
At each seat, each field, and each forest [is pilgrimage holiness]. In Kali, people recognize a pilgrimage place when it is far from home.

II.1.10
Yet all pilgrimage places exist through their own nature. At every seat the dharma is distinct, and the observance distinct.

II.1.11
In each country the Kula observances must be understood according to their circumstances. On earth the pilgrimage seats have distinct worship and distinct mantras.

II.1.12
Pārvatī, Bhadrāpīṭha lies to the south of the middle country; Jālandhara to the west, and Pūrṇapīṭha to the east.

II.1.13
Conceive Kāmarūpa in the eastern portion of the northeast; Jālandhara in the northwest, and Kolāpura to the north.

II.1.14
Vihāra is in the northeast, Mahendra somewhat farther north, and Oḍra to the east. Now hear the subsidiary seats.
Note: Oḍra is the apparent Sanskrit reading; the Hindi gloss appears to read Śrīhaṭṭa. The discrepancy is not silently resolved.

II.1.15
Queen of the gods, Oḍrapīṭha extends sixty-eight yojanas by boat, and its length is three times its breadth.

II.1.16
The seat is cart-shaped, four-cornered, furnished with four gates, and marked by the emblem of wind.

II.1.17
Sindhubhadrakapīṭha contains twenty million pilgrimage places; there are the Someśvara liṅga and the primordial seat.

II.1.18
There are Kāmadhenu and Hara as Cakreśvara; then the field named Viraja and, thereafter, Ekāmra.

II.1.19
[There is] the Sun's great field, where Mātaṅgaśaṅkara dwells; holy Kuśasthalī, and the Daṇḍaka forest.

II.1.20
[There is] Sumanta forest, Śivayūpa to the east, Dhenuka forest to the west, and Gayāśiras to the north.

II.1.21
To the south is Candrabhāgā; beautiful-faced one, Oḍrapīṭha extends thirty yojanas across and a hundred in length.
Note: The change from verse 15 is retained as printed, not harmonized.

II.1.22
There the Goddess Kāmeśvarī is established in the form of the yoni seal.

II.1.23
At Bhūgolapīṭha there is Golokeśvara; at Dharmapīṭha, the great seat, Hara as Kāmeśvara.
Note: Bhūgola is provisionally read from the gloss; the text layer is defective.

II.1.24
[There are] the great field Avimukta and Haṃsaprapatana, where Brahmayūpa and the white banyan stand.

II.1.25
There also is Kurukṣetra, where the Māyāsvanā river flows; holy Ayodhyā forest, and then Dharma forest.

II.1.26
[There is] the great Kacātmaka forest, where Pātālaśaṅkara dwells; the Gaṇḍakī river to the east, and Viṣṇuyūpa to the west—

II.1.27
—the Vṛṣabha liṅga to the south, and the banana forest to the north. Between them is the charming bow-shaped seat.

II.1.28
There the lotus shines red. [The following seat] extends eleven hundred and nine yojanas in length—

II.1.29
—and eighty-eight across: the excellent triangular seat. There are Pravarapīṭha, Aśokapīṭha, Sītā's great field, and Agastya's hermitage.
Note: The extraordinary length and the segmentation of verse 28 are uncertain but supported by the Hindi; no modern measurements are substituted.

II.1.30
[There is] Hara's supreme field: these are the three fields, beloved. [Then come] Mādhava forest and Hara's forest—

II.1.31
—and Bharga's forest: thus three forests.

II.1.32
To the north is Brahmakṣetra, to the south the ocean; to the east Udayakūṭa, and to the west Śrīparvata, beloved.

II.1.33
The seat between these is named Puṇyākhya. From one step to another, with a distance of two hand-lengths between—

II.1.34
—[one proceeds] at Śivarātri, reckoning the month by the solar month. Know Kāmarūpa as six-cornered and with a prominent inner enclosure.
Note: The transition from distances and timing to Kāmarūpa is obscure in both Sanskrit and gloss. These clauses are tentative, not a reliable travel instruction.

II.1.35
Know it as holy and comparable [to that seat], with nine arrays and three circles; its altar's centre is declared to contain ten mountains.

II.1.36
[There is] Madhyapīṭha, the great seat, where Kāmeśvara dwells. Queen of the gods, in that seat flows the Campāvatī river.

II.1.37
[There is] Kanyāśrama, the great field where Rudra's two footprints are; and the supreme field Ekāśrama, where Śaṅkara named Nāga dwells.

II.1.38
[There is] the Mānasa field, where Hara as Viśveśvara dwells; then Nāṭaka forest and Campaka forest.

II.1.39
To the south is Picchilā, Gautama's great-fruited [river]; eastward as far as the Svarṇa river, and the Karatoyā to the west—

II.1.40
—Mandaśaila to the south and Vihagācala to the north. [The seat is] half a yojana across and five yojanas long.

II.1.41
[There are] thirty thousand tri-streams and ten [myriads] of step-originated [waters: uncertain]. Saumāra is eight-cornered, where Dikkaravāsinī—

II.1.42
—the Goddess who grants liberation through knowledge and meditation, always dwells. There one obtains one's abode through the Goddess's grace, not otherwise.
Note on 39–42: The list's internal transitions are not clear enough to assign every boundary to a modern region. The hydrographic numbers in 41 are corrupt; their terms are recorded provisionally.

II.1.43
Now, in the following [description], the nine seats as far as Saumāra are told. Dikkaravāsinī always visibly dwells there.
Note: Uttare can mean “to the north” or “in what follows”; neither the Sanskrit extraction nor gloss securely settles this clause.

II.1.44
Northwest of Dikkara is exceedingly rare Nīlapīṭha, where the Goddess Kāmeśvarī has the form of the yoni seal.

II.1.45
[There is] Pārijāta, the great field where Ādityaśaṅkara dwells; the field of Koṣeya's city, and Amarakaṇṭaka.

II.1.46
[There are] Ādvina forest, auspicious Gautama forest, and Śivanātha's forest. Beloved, hear [their limits] beginning with the seat.
Note: Ādvina is uncertain; the Hindi appears to read Āśvina. The name is not securely identified.

II.1.47
To the east is Sauraśilā forest, to the west the auspicious Svarṇā river; to the south Brahmayūpa, and to the north Lake Mānasa.

II.1.48
The seat in their midst bestows enjoyment and liberation: the great seat named Saumāra, six-cornered and with three circles.

II.1.49
Hayatāmra, the fifth [seat], extends a thousand yojanas in length. Kolāpīṭha is declared diminished in its breadth [the measure is uncertain].

II.1.50
[There are] the great seat [named] Saumāra and the seat Śivatalpa; the Vakradeveśvara liṅga, where the Kamalā stone is.
Note: Several names in this verse are poorly recognized; the Hindi itself does not yield a consistent correction.

II.1.51
First [comes] Kedārakṣetra, where Śaṅkara as Kedāra is; [then] Piṇḍakara field, where Aruṇa stands.

II.1.52
[There are] Durgā forest, Soma forest, and Bhadra forest; the great field named Cauhāra, eighty yojanas across and thirty-six in length, where one goes and grieves no more.
Note: The Sanskrit reads ṣaṭtriṃśat, thirty-six; the Hindi appears to print sixty-three. The Sanskrit order is followed.

II.1.53
[There are] Brahmakṣetra, Kalākṣetra, Raghukṣetra, Nandana, Pārijāta, and then Śiva forest.

II.1.54
Then Deśa forest is proclaimed: this is the seventh seat, beloved. To the east is the exceedingly holy river Hīrikā—

II.1.55
—to the west the Nāthaka liṅga, to the north Kili mountain, and to the south the Nātha tree: thus the seat is described.

II.1.56
By ox-cart [the circuit takes] three months and three days; [by boat] one month less than that previously stated period.
Note: The transport contrast is supplied from the Hindi; the extracted Sanskrit's last clause is defective.

II.1.57
Vārāhī is the first seat, Kolapīṭha the second; Kumāra the first field, Nandana the second—

II.1.58
—and Śāśvatī the third field. Mātaṅga is the first forest, Siddha forest the second, and Vipula forest the third.

II.1.59
[It is] surrounded by millions of liṅgas and countless millions of attendants. To the east is Pañcatīrtha, and to the west the Dhanahā river—

II.1.60
—to the south Pāryā, and to the north Kuruvaka forest. Goddess, the place amid these is named Śrīpīṭha.
Note: Pāryā is recognized in the Hindi as a forest-name, Patrākhyā. The name remains uncertain.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Chapter 1 of the Second Part, on the sacred seats of Kāmarūpa, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally designated twenty-four thousand [verses].

Coverage: 60 numbered units. Corrupt names, dimensions, and transitions remain marked. This geographical tradition should not be silently harmonized with other sources or modern borders.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 2
Pilgrimage preliminaries: English working translation
Source: Mishra edition (1984), printed pages 249–261, PDF pages 269–281. The historical astrological, caste, and ritual rules below are the source's prescriptions. Sanskrit primary; Hindi consulted. Uncertain or contradictory readings are marked.

II.2.1 — The Blessed Lord said:
After completing daily observances at home and devotedly worshipping the ancestors beginning with the Nāndīmukhas, undertake the journey according to rule.

II.2.2
When Venus has gone northward and the auspicious planets are favourable, obtain permission from the guru, parents, and especially Brahmins; feed seven Brahmins and then travel.

II.2.3
The discerning person should not travel east with Leo, Sagittarius, or Aries rising, nor west with Libra, Aquarius, or Pisces rising.
Note: The Hindi lists Gemini rather than Pisces in the western series. The apparent Sanskrit reading is retained provisionally.

II.2.4
Do not go south in Taurus, Virgo, or Capricorn; nor north in Cancer, Scorpio, or Pisces.

II.2.5
Do not go southeast in Sagittarius, Taurus, or Virgo; avoid the southwest in Capricorn, Cancer, or Pisces.

II.2.6
Avoid the northwest in Aquarius, Aries, or Sagittarius; do not contemplate the northeast in Leo, Pisces, or Cancer.

II.2.7
Know this as the general rule. Śaṅkarī, hear [the rule of] the Yoginī. My beloved, do not travel east on Saturday—

II.2.8
—west on Sunday, south on Wednesday, or north on Tuesday. The discerning person should avoid those directions.

II.2.9
[Do not go] southwest on Thursday, northwest on Friday, or northeast on Saturday and especially Monday.

II.2.10
Great Lady, the first and ninth lunar days [belong] to the east. Do not travel there, because the Yoginī faces one there.

II.2.11
Avoid the west on the fourteenth and sixth days; do not go south on the thirteenth and fifth.

II.2.12
Avoid the Rākṣasa direction [southwest] on the tenth and second days. The northwest is wholly inauspicious at full moon and on the seventh.

II.2.13
Do not go northeast on new moon or the eighth day. Viṣkambha, Prīti, and Āyuṣmān are excluded on the first lunar day.

II.2.14
Saubhāgya, Śobhana, and Atigaṇḍa [are excluded] on the second; Sukarman and Vaidhṛti on the third.

II.2.15
Avoid Gaṇḍa, Vṛddhi, Dhruva, and Vyāghāta on the fourth day, Goddess; Harṣaṇa on the fifth.

II.2.16
Śaṅkarī, know Vajra, Siddhi, and Vyatīpāta [as excluded] on the sixth; avoid Varīyān and Parigha on the seventh.

II.2.17
Avoid Śiva, Siddhi, and Sādhya on the eighth; Śubha and Śukra on the ninth; Brahma on the tenth.
Note: The calendrical names appear in this form in the extraction; Śukra may require collation with Śukla. No correction is silently made.

II.2.18
Avoid Aindra on the eleventh, Vaidhṛti on the twelfth; the three Uttarā stars on the thirteenth, and Viśākhā on the fourteenth—

II.2.19
—Maghā, Ārdrā, and Bharaṇī at full moon. Kṛttikā is censured on the first day, and Jyeṣṭhā on the second. Never depart under a star unfavourable for travel.

II.2.20
For travel one need not consider the defective karaṇa. Always avoid travelling in one's birth month, on one's birthday, under one's birth star, and on the eighth and ninth days.

II.2.21
In an emergency, dawn is approved for westward travel, and cow-dust time [dusk] for eastward travel.

II.2.22
Midday [is approved] for the south, afternoon for the north, night for the northeast, and the two twilights for the southwest.

II.2.23
Midday [is approved] for the southeast, early morning for the northwest. In Vāruṇa and the other conjunctions act according to the appropriate time.

II.2.24
Excluding the birthday and the eighth and ninth days, to journey east one should initially face north—

II.2.25
—to go west face east, to go south face west, and to go north face south: thus travel for successful accomplishment.
Note: The gloss explains an initial movement in the prescribed facing direction before turning toward the actual destination.

II.2.26
Flowers, yāvaka, a conch, kettle-drum music, a ruler, a hundred cows, a chariot, and a conveyance are auspicious on the traveller's right.

II.2.27
Cooked food, a lump of meat, a vessel of rice, a wondrous [object], a piece of fish, and excellent food are all auspicious on the right.

II.2.28
A maiden, a couple, a courtesan, full vessels, women, and moving animals are auspicious on the left.

II.2.29
Curd and fruit ahead are declared auspicious for travel. If cranes and peacocks go in pairs while one travels, know success; otherwise predict obstruction.

II.2.30
When vultures, hawks, and cillas [a bird-name] move beside one, the traveller should not depart; [nor when] a crow situated in the wood—

II.2.31
—on a fallen [tree] cries harshly, or on hearing a house-lizard's call, weeping, or quarrelling: never go then.

II.2.32
When Jupiter and the Sun [are conjoined], when Jupiter is in Leo, or Venus has set, [avoid] seeing deities, journeys, and consecrations—

II.2.33
—beginning vows, marriage, entry into a house, and the like—

II.2.34
—tanks, wells, reservoirs, beginning [the making of] a yantra, planting a sacrificial tree, and making a garden—

II.2.35
—divine vows, releasing a bull, beginning a sacrifice, ear-piercing, increase-rites, and bringing a bride home.

II.2.36
Never perform a first viewing of the gods [then], the first beginning of vows, or a journey [the expression concerning a year is unclear].

II.2.37
For the exceedingly holy Vyāla vow, a daily vow, and the Rohiṇī vow, there is no restriction of time—

II.2.38
—nor for Śivarātri, tonsure at Prayāga, or a country's conflagration should one consider planetary inauspiciousness.
Note: Deśadāha is literally a burning of the country; whether this is an emergency exception or a rite-name is not established here.

II.2.39
At Ujjayinī [consider] the Sun's purity; in the middle country the Moon's; at Janasthāna Mars's, and in the forest Mercury's.

II.2.40
In Gauḍa and Āndhra [consider] Jupiter's purity; in Kāmarūpa Venus's; at Mathurā Saturn's; in Aṅga and Vaṅga Rāhu's.

II.2.41
Oḍrapīṭha is established [with] bow and arrow; at Jālandhara [the measure] is four hands, and at Ūrmika a full hand—

II.2.42
—at Kāmarūpa ten hands; at Saumāra three hands; at Kolapīṭha four hands; at Cauhāra twice that, auspicious one.
Note: The referent of these measures is not made explicit in the immediate text. These are not translated into modern distances or a reconstructed diagram.

II.2.43
At Mahendra [the measure is] kalā-hasta, at Śrīhaṭṭa three hands. At subsidiary seats and Pātāla know it as one hand.
Note: Kalā-hasta remains an uncertain technical measure; vahni encodes three.

II.2.44
At Ratnapīṭha, north of the Lohitya, at Baladeva's hermitage and Kanyāśrama, [it is] six hands. Facing the Yoginī, abandon bathing, meditation, and movement.

II.2.45
Know half the result [for travelling] by conveyance, and likewise with a parasol and sandals. Physical exercise and meat-eating destroy pilgrimage's fruit.

II.2.46
The fruit of a pilgrimage place arises even when reached incidentally. One obtains it through knowledge; without knowledge it is fruitless.

II.2.47
At a pilgrimage place avoid ritual sipping, washing the feet, bodily evacuation and cleansing, and praise of another pilgrimage place.
Note: The Sanskrit repeats sipping; the list is preserved in sense, not harmonized with other ritual manuals.

II.2.48
Attachment to another pilgrimage place is always a fault at a pilgrimage place. Do not discharge waste there or cut the hair there.

II.2.49
Do not reside on a pilgrimage bank, especially on its south side; never bathe south of the pilgrimage place.

II.2.50
On the northern part of a pilgrimage place dwell eighty million thousands of pilgrimage places and holy shrines; [also] south of a sacred pool and on a river's left, beloved.
Note: The enormous number and the change of orientation for a pool and river are the text's, not corrections to verse 49.

II.2.51
Never test a Brahmin at a pilgrimage place. Whatever deities and Brahmins dwell at a particular place must be saluted and worshipped; through their words comes purification.

II.2.52
There is no time restriction for ancestral offerings; omit honey from the rice-balls. Having reached a pilgrimage place, the discerning person should not stay far away [from its bank].

II.2.53
At an eclipse, at a pilgrimage place, on the ancestor's day, at the beginning and end of a sacrifice, and especially at an earthquake—

II.2.54
—[consider] a Brahmin's eligibility for gifts and ancestral offerings. What is given without examination is declared fruitless.
Note: This seems to conflict with the instruction against testing in verse 51. The contradiction is retained; the Hindi also understands examination here.

II.2.55
[Do not perform] invocation, arghya, fire-offering, sprinkling with the pavitra, or the declaration of imperishability—

II.2.56
—nor the presentation of clothing-thread at a pilgrimage ancestral rite. At the time of rice-ball offerings, place seventeen rice-balls there.

II.2.57
After completing the ancestral rite, never give [further] rice-balls. Immerse oneself in every pool and every pilgrimage place—

II.2.58
—ten, eight, five, seven, or three [times] in a day. Then perform anointing and worship each pilgrimage place.

II.2.59
At Lohitya, Śoṇa, Gayā, Viraja, Kanyāśrama, Agastya, and Pāriyātra—

II.2.60
—at Mukurāṅga and Ekāmra, avoid tonsure. A mass of sin is firmly established at the tips of the hair.

II.2.61
Therefore, leaving the topknot, [keep/remove hair] at the roots of the ears [uncertain]. At a fortnight's end, month's end, six months, or a year—

II.2.62
—or at the end of each eight years, perform tonsure again.
Note: The sense of the ear-root clause and its relation to the tonsure prohibition require collation. The Hindi repeats rather than resolves the difficulty.

II.2.63
Then make a kuśa-grass figure and place it in the pilgrimage water. Facing north, the discerning person should bathe the relatives [by representation].

II.2.64
With the mantra beginning “kuśo …,” immerse the kuśa three times. [The represented person] obtains a quarter of the pilgrimage place's fruit, without doubt.

II.2.65
Having taken [a Brahmin or a double kuśa figure: uncertain] and pronounced the name at immersion, half the pilgrimage's fruit is prescribed for the Brahmin.
Note: Dvijakoḍa in the extracted text is corrupt; the gloss understands bodily contact with a Brahmin. No secure physical procedure is reconstructed.

II.2.66
Better to sell one's mother, better to sell one's father, than to bathe someone of a different gotra [as proxy] or accept gifts at pilgrimage places.
Note: This is the source's emphatic rhetorical condemnation, not approval of selling persons.

II.2.67
At fields of accomplishment and pilgrimage places, do not bestow a blessing on another. Go from there elsewhere [rather than accept] sesame or cows from a Śūdra in particular—

II.2.68
—do not accept [from him], nor reside with him. One is stained by the sin of the sacrifice whose recipient one becomes. Therefore avoid another's sacrifice with every effort.
Note: The final two verses are exclusionary social prescriptions of this historical text. Some connecting syntax is uncertain and follows the Hindi provisionally.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Chapter 2 of the Second Part, concerning Kāmarūpa, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, traditionally designated twenty-four thousand [verses].
Coverage: all 68 numbered units; difficult text and contradictions marked.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 3
Vāyukūṭa, Candrakuṇḍa, and Aśvakrānta: English working translation
Source: Mishra edition (1984), printed pages 261–274; PDF pages 281–294. Sanskrit primary, Hindi consulted. Traditional directions, measures, calendrical rules, and promised rewards are retained as the source's claims. Corrupt words and formulae remain marked.

II.3.1 — The Blessed Lord said:
Kāmarūpa is the great seat, supreme and more secret than the secret. Śaṅkara always dwells there with Pārvatī.

II.3.2
Worshipped in that seat, the god soon becomes gracious. Beloved, now hear: I shall explain that seat.

II.3.3
On the journey, at Vāyukūṭa's extremity, [flows] Śatanadī, surrounded by ten million liṅgas, measuring a bow and a hand [the connection is uncertain].

II.3.4
There [the deity] stands in wind-form, and wind issues from him. Worshipping that Wind, one attains the world of Vāyu.

II.3.5
East of Vāyugiri is the mountain Candrakūṭa; Godanta in the middle, and auspicious Aśvakrānta to the south.

II.3.6
Mādhava [dwells] at Candrakūṭa; Jaṭādhara, Ṣaṇmukha, and Jayanta at Godanta; Janārdana at Aśvakrānta.

II.3.7
Worship each with the corresponding seed and mantra, honey and milk-rice. Whoever resides at Vāyukūṭa becomes lord of riches.

II.3.8
From residence at Candraśaila one becomes consumptive; at Godanta sinful; at Aśvakrānta, liberated.

II.3.9
East of Candraśaila, somewhat toward the southeast, amid the Lohitya, Queen of the gods, measuring thirty bows—

II.3.10
—is [the mountain] known as Indraśaila. Residence there brings great fruit.

II.3.11
Somewhat south of Indraśaila and north of Candraśaila, the discerning person should leave sixteen bows [of intervening space].

II.3.12
The lake named Candrakuṇḍa is three bows across and a hundred and twenty-five long. Bathing and drinking there, a person attains absolute liberation.

II.3.13
“Candra's ford is a great ford, equal to Piṇḍāraka.” Bathing with this mantra dispels the sin of killing a Brahmin.

II.3.14
“Born from the lineage of the wise Lord [or Moon], giver of delight to Mādhava, remove sin through the delight of hearing [uncertain]; homage to you.”

II.3.15
[Form the mantra with] Bhṛgu joined to sarga, together with the primordial series; above it bhṛgu and daṇḍī, ending in ja and accompanied by Śakti and sura [encoded terms uncertain]—

II.3.16
—joined to nāda and bindu; again [the ja-ending syllable]. Having uttered that eight-syllabled mantra, complete [the arghya offering].
Note: Verses 14–16 contain corrupt wording and encoded phonemes not securely recoverable from text and gloss; no continuous mantra is invented.

II.3.17
In Aries or Pisces, at full moon, repeating the [prescribed] mantra at night and bathing, one attains Brahmanhood and does not fall into an evil state.

II.3.18
Perform the bath by day, destroying great sins. Whoever bathes with this mantra in Candra's waters—

II.3.19
—dwells continuously in glorious Candra's mansion while the earth endures, and gradually reaches the supreme state.
Note: The daytime rule is preserved alongside the preceding night's repetition, rather than collapsed into a single timing instruction.

II.3.20
Four bows to its south is the pilgrimage place named Mānasa, destroying every sin.

II.3.21
Supreme Lady, bathe there with the mantra in Kārttika's bright fortnight and attain Viṣṇu's world.

II.3.22
That ford certainly pleases Viṣṇu. For attaining yogic knowledge and satisfaction, give arghya to the lord of rivers.

II.3.23
Twenty-eight bows to its south is the lake named Ayuta; bathing there, one reaches Acyuta's station.

II.3.24
Śaṅkarī, whoever bathes there at midday through the four rainy-season months goes to Brahmā's dwelling.

II.3.25
Having bathed with the mantra, the practitioner should give arghya. Offering a great rice-ball at the [lake] named Amṛta brings accomplishment; residence at Amṛta removes beings' sins.
Note: Ayuta/Amṛta may reflect different names or textual corruption; neither is silently substituted throughout.

II.3.26
Ten bows south is the lake Ṛṇamocana. Going there, released from the three debts, one does not return to worldly bondage.

II.3.27
Bathing there on Lalitāsaptamī in Bhādra, one goes to heaven; bathing on Māgha's fourteenth day, one obtains liberation.

II.3.28
Ṛṇamocana lake releases all debt-burdened people from the various bonds of action and the three debts.

II.3.29
South of Aśvakrānta, somewhat toward the southeast, is the lake named Aśvakrānta, measuring twelve bows.

II.3.30
Janārdana in Kalki's form rose from the Nāga world, bathed there in Viraja, and established the Horse Ford.

II.3.31
North of the path, carefully present arghya with scented water and milk, using this mantra:

II.3.32
“Holy one served by Brahmins, surpassing Vārāṇasī by a digit, accept this arghya with milk. I seek liberation from you.

II.3.33
“Born from the horse's step, remover of sin, source of countless [blessings], homage to you, Aśvakrānta.”

II.3.34
Bathing with this mantra, one obtains sacrifice's reward; merely bathing at Mausala [one gains] the merit of horse sacrifices and the rest.

II.3.35
Merely bathing according to rule yields the rājasūya's reward; giving and libations for ancestors become imperishable.

II.3.36
When the Sun stands in Viśākhā and full moon in Kṛttikā, that conjunction is named Padmaka, exceedingly rare at Aśvakrānta.

II.3.37
[There are] Aśvakrānta's Devatīrtha and then auspicious Paitāmaha. Those who bathe there obtain worlds of great exaltation.

II.3.38
They have no longing for merit already performed or yet unperformed [uncertain]. Great Lady, this statement is true.

II.3.39
It is the supreme pilgrimage ford in the three worlds; Kārttikī especially is supreme, removing merit and sin.

II.3.40
Whatever [fruit] Brahmins obtain through all gifts and austerities [is obtained] merely by bathing at Ayuta, contemplated auspiciously.
Note: The place-name and construction are uncertain in the extraction; the Hindi explains a general equivalence of ritual rewards.

II.3.41
Beloved, even great sinners are freed from sin; those tainted by subsidiary sins are themselves released.

II.3.42
Śaṅkarī, one fasting there swiftly and effortlessly obtains the fruit of the Puṇḍarīka sacrifice.
Note: Yajñasya, “of the sacrifice,” is the likely Sanskrit reading; the Hindi appears to understand a Yakṣa named Puṇḍarīka.

II.3.43
Whoever bathes there in Māgha and devotedly gives sesame to a worthy Brahmin according to ability dwells in the Lord's mansion.

II.3.44
Whoever fasts, bathes there, and eats cow-products goes to heaven after death.

II.3.45
The human beings who live nearby also go to heaven through its power: there is no doubt.

II.3.46
Those who give wealth to a Brahmin and worship for Brahman's peace, mounted upon a lotus-seat and four-armed, attain union with Brahmā without rebirth.
Note: The opening ritual expression is imperfectly recovered; the final reward follows the Sanskrit and gloss.

II.3.47
The foremost people who undertake prāyopaveśa there go fearlessly to heaven in a swan-carriage.
Note: Prāyopaveśa is the historical religious practice of fasting unto death. The verse's promised outcome is the text's claim.

II.3.48
Their ancestors dance and their grandfathers are satisfied; through libation and rice-ball offering there they attain heaven.

II.3.49
Sinners who touch [the place] are released from worldly bondage. By giving there one becomes Brahmā's attendant, without doubt.

II.3.50
Having reached Aśvakrānta, [one's mind becomes stainless through bathing], whether after eating or otherwise, by day or night—
Note: The negative construction is corrupt; bracketed wording follows the Hindi's stated sense, provisionally.

II.3.51
—that ford is regarded as foremost of all pilgrimage places, destroying sin and producing merit for beings.

II.3.52
Those of disciplined selves who bathe there and worship Janārdana according to their ability go to heaven.

II.3.53
Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Rudra are always present, Great Lady. No ford on earth is holier than the Horse Ford.

II.3.54
Viraja, the ford of stainless water, is renowned in the three worlds. Fortunate people behold that pilgrimage place, the station of Brahmā's world.

II.3.55
A hundred years of performing agnihotra [produce the same fruit] as a single Kārttikī [full moon] at that ford, Great Lady.

II.3.56
It equals the fruit of all sacrifices and gives the fruit of all pilgrimage places; through it [the equivalent of] completing all the Vedas is accomplished.

II.3.57
Having gone to Aśvakrānta and performed twilight worship, carefully [bring] Aśvakrānta's water with a hand-staff [or carrying pole: uncertain]—

II.3.58
—in a bhṛṅgāra, karaṅga, or earthen vessel, Śaṅkarī. Discerning people bring that holy water and perform twilight worship—

II.3.59
—with concentration, preceded by breath-control. Beautiful-faced one, hear the merit gained by that observance.

II.3.60
It equals twelve years of properly observed twilight worship. Bathing gives a horse sacrifice's fruit; drinking ten times that; fasting gives immense, infinite fruit.

II.3.61
Whoever stays one day at the ford obtains all the fruit of duly giving ten million cows at another pilgrimage place.

II.3.62
Whoever gives gold measuring half a pāda receives [the fruit of] the [full] gold-measure; therefore give [there].
Note: The precise metrology and final clause are uncertain; the Hindi understands a doubling of merit.

II.3.63
The stream reaching Candraśaila is proclaimed Jāhnavī; the stream touching the Horse Ford is prescribed for bathing.

II.3.64
The stream touching Candraśaila should be known as Sarasvatī.
Note: The repetition of Candraśaila in 63–64 is retained as extracted; it may require comparison with another witness rather than geographical harmonization.

II.3.65
The confluence at Aśvakrānta appears in the rains, and especially in Kārttika. Know that as Prayāga [the extracted word is uncertain].

II.3.66
Whoever performs tonsure there obtains a fruit equal to Prayāga's. Goddess, even today the two-stream [confluence] is seen at Gayākuṇḍa.

II.3.67
A person who is poor in this world or has lost a kingdom should enter Aśvakrānta's water, repeat a Vaiṣṇava mantra, make worship and offerings, and libations to gods and ancestors—

II.3.68
—and give rice-balls. Before long, free from birth, [that person] becomes a universal king: this is true, without doubt.
Note: The combination of release from birth with kingship is present in the verse; it is not silently rationalized.

II.3.69
For one who makes the journey in Kārttika, good fortune, wealth, grain, and excellent women arise in this birth amid manifold afflictions.

II.3.70
Whoever performs, causes to be performed, or hears this pilgrimage procedure is released from every sin.

II.3.71
A person who undertakes Agastya's [seasonal] journey [obtains the fruit of] many hundreds of years of Brahmanical rites; making the Caitra journey, one obtains divine consecration.
Note: Agastyāgamana may denote a calendrical appearance of Agastya; its precise observance is not described here.

II.3.72
What need is there for much more speech, beautiful one? Nothing attainable fails to be attained thereby; one does not behold [worldly bondage].

II.3.73
It equals the fruits of all sacrifices, bestows the fruits of all pilgrimage places, and constitutes worship of all gods together.

II.3.74
Those who reach the Horse Ford and duly bathe once are enriched in their lineage with sons, daughters, and wealth—

II.3.75
—[become] foremost givers, and delight with young women, all their limbs fulfilled: there is no doubt.
Note: The first compound is corrupt; the sense follows the Hindi provisionally.

II.3.76
Eight ages' residence in Kāśī and one day in Puruṣottama [yield a reward]; seeing Viraja's mouth yields ten million times that. At the Horse Ford know that fruit at every instant.

II.3.77
Seeing it brings the rājasūya's reward; bathing in its water ten times the vājapeya's; drinking a mere mouthful, a horse sacrifice's. Beyond all sacrifices, it ends worldly existence.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Chapter 3 of the Second Part, concerning Kāmarūpa, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, traditionally designated twenty-four thousand [verses].
Coverage: all 77 numbered units; uncertain formulae and readings marked.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 4
Divine names and the Aśvakrānta–Gayā pilgrimage: English working translation
Source: Mishra edition (1984), printed pages 275–299; PDF pages 295–319. Sanskrit primary, Hindi consulted. Place-name spellings and some formulae are uncertain. Directions and rewards are translated as historical sacred geography, not modern geography. All numbered units retained; textual difficulties are identified.

II.4.1 — The Goddess said:
Lord, if you favour me, if I am worthy of a boon, sovereign one, tell me where each of your names is established.

II.4.2
In which places do Brahmins always behold you? By what name does each place shine upon the earth?

II.4.3 — The Blessed Lord said:
At Puṣkara I am Sureśāna; at Gayā, Suśarmada; at Kānyakubja, Vedagarbha; at Bhṛgukaccha, Pitāmaha.

II.4.4
At Kauberyā, Sṛṣṭikartṛ; at Nandipurī, Bṛhaspati; at Prabhāsa, Padmajanman; at Svarṇanadī, Surapriya.

II.4.5
At Dvārāvatī, Vāgdeva; at Nāṭaka, Nāṭakeśvara; on Nīlācala, Kāmeśa; on Hastiparvata, Piṅgala.

II.4.6
At Kuśāvarta, Vijaya; on Puṣkarācala, Jayanta; on Bhasmācala, Bhayānanda; on Candrakūṭa, Mādhava.

II.4.7
In the inner sanctuary, Padmahasta; at Maṅgalā, Tryambaka; at Bhadrapīṭha, Divyeśa; at Aśvakrānta, Janārdana.

II.4.8
At Ahicchatra, Tulānanda; at Śrīśaila, Jaganmaya; at Kuśahasta, Padmapāṇi; on Mānaśaila, Munīśvara.

II.4.9
Hear, beloved of my life: at Śrīkaṇṭha, Śrīnivāsa; at Kanyāśrama, Rudra; on Maināka, Viśvanāyaka.

II.4.10
At Ekāmra, Nāgeśa; at Viraja, Maheśvara; at Mūlikārya, Viṣṇu; on Mahendra, Bhārgava.

II.4.11
At Kauśikī, Bodhi; at Ayodhyā, Bhārgava; on Maṇikūṭa, Hayagrīva; on Bindu mountain, Varāha.

II.4.12
At Godanta, Jaṭādhara; at Gomanta, Jāṅgaleśvara; at Brahmaputra, Parameṣṭhin; on Viśvaśaila, Graha [uncertain name].

II.4.13
On Citraśaila, Citreśa; at Devikā, Caturbhuja; at Vṛndāvana, Padmapāṇi; at Naimiṣa, Kuśahasta.

II.4.14
At Mandara, Mahābodhi; on Hanu mountain, Gopīndra; at Bhāgīrathī, Padmagarbha; at Kāmpilla, Kanakapriya.

II.4.15
At Karaṇa, Kāmāri; at Kapota, Havyavāhana; at Arbuda, Vasiṣṭha; at Śvetanadī, Manobhava.

II.4.16
At Dhavalā, Pinākin; at Picchilā, Trivikrama; at Agastya, Yajñagarbha; at Urvaśī, Madhusūdana.

II.4.17
At Rukmiṇīśa, Dārvi [the gloss reads Hari]; at Paitṛka, Ruci; at Gomanta, Vāmana; at Kāśī, named Viśveśvara.

II.4.18
At Prayāga, Prajāpati; at Vidarbhā, Dvijapriya; at Bhadrapīṭha, Gaṅgādhara; at Mātaṅga, Tryambaka.

II.4.19
On Nandaśaila, Tripurāri; on Pāṇḍuśaila, Trilocana; at Gaṅgāhrada, Trilokeśa; at Bhittipurī, Divākara.

II.4.20
At Vamaṭa, Miṅgilanātha; at Dāruśṛṅga, Kalānidhi; in Dāru forest, Mahāliṅga; at Aśoka, Vināśaka.

II.4.21
At Cullakā, Harisena; at Parṇāṭa, Ananta; at Vaṭa, Mārkaṇḍeya; at Ikṣudvāra, Divākara.

II.4.22
At Gokarṇa, named Vikarṇa; at Mandāra, Madhusūdana. Thus I have proclaimed to you a hundred and eight places.
Note: The list's traditional total is retained; it has not been forced into 108 by adding unprinted names. Several obscure names in verses 3–22 need collation.

II.4.23
Excellent-vowed one, my presence and yours are perpetual there. A devoted person who beholds even one of them and obtains stainless bathing rejoices for eternal years.

II.4.24
All wrongdoing committed by mind, speech, or body is pacified: there is no doubt.

II.4.25
Wherever a person goes and beholds me at those places, I remain there and grant liberation.

II.4.26
Through flowers, offerings, incense, satisfying Brahmins, and steady meditation, one quickly attains [me], Supreme Lady.

II.4.27
North of Aśvakrānta and west of Ṛṇamocana is an exceedingly rare field measuring twenty-two bows—

II.4.28
—named Dvipañcaka, saluted by all gods. Worshipping Rudra there, one gains the jyotiṣṭoma's reward.

II.4.29
Dwelling there six months, with regulated food, celibate and concentrated, one attains the supreme state, Queen of the gods.

II.4.30
Puṣkara is approved in the Kṛta age, Naimiṣa in Tretā, Kurukṣetra in Dvāpara, and the Horse Ford in Kali.

II.4.31
Therefore accomplish the heart's desire on its northern bank. In Dvāpara, at Kurukṣetra, giving yields imperishable fruit.

II.4.32
Giving at Pañcaka is difficult; everything there is difficult. Sin done elsewhere vanishes at a pilgrimage place, but sin committed there is not removed anywhere else.

II.4.33
After dwelling ten days, twelve days, or [a month and ten days: uncertain] on Meru's back, the illustrious Goddess, seated upon Rudra's seat, bowed and asked Mahādeva:

II.4.34 — The Goddess said:
Śaṅkara, explain how the sins accumulated through thousands of earlier births are destroyed.

II.4.35 — The Blessed Lord said:
Listen, Goddess: I shall explain the unsurpassed secret of secrets. Beyond all pilgrimage places is renowned Aśvakrānta.

II.4.36
The field north of it I have called Avimukta. This is supreme knowledge; this is supreme austerity.

II.4.37
This is supreme Brahman and the supreme state. As Puruṣottama Nārāyaṇa is foremost among gods and Giriśa among lords, so this is foremost among baths.

II.4.38
Giving, repetition, fire-offering, austerity, meditation, study, knowledge, and whatever is performed there all become imperishable.

II.4.39
At Aśvakrānta are supreme yoga, supreme attainment, and supreme liberation. No ford is like it.

II.4.40
A heap of sin equal to Meru and Mandara entirely perishes upon reaching Aśvakrānta.

II.4.41
Even those of evil deeds, touched by wind-driven dust from Aśvakrānta, go to the supreme state.

II.4.42
The state prescribed for Aśvakrānta's residents is not [obtained] at Kurukṣetra, Gayādvāra, or Puṣkara.

II.4.43
The excellent state unattainable through giving, austerity, sacrifice, or learning is obtained at the Horse Ford.

II.4.44
Liberation comes through contact [with it], and [purity] through accepting it; it is greater even than Agastya and the other [fords].
Note: The middle phrase is uncertain; the gloss supplies the sense of a pure state.

II.4.45
Even a Brahmin-killer who reaches Aśvakrānta is relieved of that killing through the Horse Field's greatness. No return [to birth] is ever seen for that person.

II.4.46
Contemplate a northern, southern, or any other entrance.

II.4.47
Every time is auspicious at Aśvakrānta, beautiful-faced one. The fruit a person obtains elsewhere through a great gift—

II.4.48
—is obtained imperishably by giving a mere kākiṇī at the Horse Ford. My beloved, whoever fasts here one day, devoted to me, obtains a thousand years' fruit.

II.4.49
Duly giving ten million cows at another pilgrimage place and staying one day here yield equal fruit.

II.4.50
The fruit of properly bathing at Prayāga in Māgha is multiplied ten million times at the Horse Ford every instant.

II.4.51
Śaṅkarī, attending [the ford] at midday and afternoon [yields] sixty million thousands and sixty million hundreds [of the usual fruit].
Note: The huge numerical expressions are retained; the supplied text does not explain a uniform unit for the comparison.

II.4.52
All the heap of sin, even as great as Meru and Mandara, is destroyed upon reaching Aśvakrānta.

II.4.53
Insects, moths, mosquitoes, trees, beings moving in water or on land, frogs and fish, leaving their bodies at Aśvakrānta, attain Śiva.

II.4.54
Whoever dwells continually at Pañcaka goes to the supreme state.

II.4.55
Whoever wishes to reach that station above all worlds, even above Brahmā's world, should dwell here, difficult though it is.

II.4.56
As Madhusūdana is the beginning of all gods, so Pañcaka is called the beginning of all fields.

II.4.57
Beautiful-faced one, bathe at Pañcaka and the Horse Ford in forward and reverse order, separately and together.

II.4.58
The northern forest yields the same fruit. Merely seeing Pañcaka gives the fruit of all the duly described pilgrimage places.

II.4.59
Ten million thousands of pilgrimage places exist on earth; all are present at the Horse Ford's doorway of liberation.

II.4.60
As long as mountains and oceans endure, those dying at Pañcaka are held to dwell in Brahmā's world.

II.4.61
Śaṅkarī, one bath burns all sins accumulated across a thousand births, from birth to death.

II.4.62
A person who dwells there practising yoga properly for three years obtains liberation in one birth through yoga.
Note: The extracted Sanskrit repeats 63 here; the gloss and sequence give 62.

II.4.63
Queen of the gods, Pañcaka lies all around Ṛṇamukti; auspicious one, Brahmā's dwelling [extends] everywhere around it.

II.4.64
Bathing, repetition, fire-offering, ancestral rites, gifts, and the other acts there each purify the whole lineage, Great Lady.

II.4.65
Near the Horse Ford, slightly to the west, is Siddhakuṇḍa, measuring eight bows.

II.4.66
Bathing and drinking its water releases one from all sins. For twice-born people, attending these pilgrimage places is prescribed after a three-night or one-night fast.

II.4.67
Auspicious one, whoever controls the vital wind, restrains hands and feet, is ritually anointed and celibate, obtains pilgrimage's fruit.

II.4.68
“Siddhakuṇḍa of great enjoyment, duly consecrated by the gods, purify me from every sin. Foremost ford, homage to you.”

II.4.69
Bathing with this mantra on the thirteenth day of Vaiśākha's dark fortnight purifies both family lines.

II.4.70
West of that ford, somewhat northwest, is the Brahma lake, sixty-four bows in measure.

II.4.71
Having bathed there and duly satisfied the ancestors with devotion, one delivers even sinful ancestors, without doubt.

II.4.72
“Having bathed, the twice-born person goes properly [to purity/consecration]. Brahmā himself [established this bathing place to please the Lord: uncertain]—”

II.4.73
“—Brahmā himself bathed here; therefore it purifies.” Having bathed according to rule with this mantra—
Note: The mantra in 72–73 is badly corrupted in both OCR and gloss; the bracketed clause is provisional, not a verified liturgical wording.

II.4.74
—especially on the fourteenth day of Māgha's bright fortnight, and duly given a gift, one is honoured in Brahmā's world.

II.4.75
Eighty bows north of Indrakūṭa know Rāmakṣetra. Its pool is [near the bank: uncertain], beloved—

II.4.76
—measuring a bilva/kunda [uncertain expression]. Bathe and worship the ancestors. Rāmatīrtha is the supreme ford among fords, beautiful-faced one.

II.4.77
Worshipping Brahmins, one is honoured in Brahmā's world.

II.4.78
“Greatly fortunate ford, on whose bank all the ancestors were satisfied by great-souled Śrī Rāma: foremost ford, homage to you.”

II.4.79
Merely bathing with this mantra on the eleventh of Vaiśākha's dark fortnight purifies both family lines.
Note: The Hindi says bright fortnight, conflicting with Sanskrit kṛṣṇapakṣa. The Sanskrit is followed.

II.4.80
Nine bows to its east is Sītātīrtha, beautiful-faced one. Bathing there, twice-born people go bodily to heaven.

II.4.81
“[At Krauñca] a great ancestral rite is said to be imperishable. This excellent ford was made by Sītā and Rāmabhadra. Therefore purify me from sin and grant liberation, worshipped by gods.”
Note: The first place-name is uncertain in the extraction.

II.4.82
Keeping silence and bathing there on the thirteenth brings great fruit. After bathing with this mantra and offering arghya with a jewel, one is freed of all sins and dwells in Brahmā's world after death.

II.4.83
Ten bows south of Sītā [ford], anointing [at that ford] makes one always victorious.

II.4.84
The splendid ford named Vijaya is supreme among fords; there Maheśa's liṅga is renowned as Vijaya.

II.4.85
Dwelling there six months with regulated food, celibate and concentrated, one is freed from every sin, Queen of the gods.

II.4.86
The supreme ford named Yogatīrtha removes every sin; [it is] the abode of Śambhu and Parameṣṭhin.

II.4.87
There the god's liṅga is renowned as Yogīśa. One obtains desired blessings and becomes dear to Rudra.

II.4.88
Know Muktitīrtha as measuring twenty-two bows. Offer [arghya] with the mantra to be stated:

II.4.89
“You are continually saluted and worshipped by those seeking liberation. Greatly fortunate Yogakuṇḍa, worshipped by immortals, purify me.”

II.4.90
Far from it is the unsurpassed Lokavṛtta pool. Having bathed there according to rule and satisfied ancestors and deities—

II.4.91
—and given various gifts to Brahmins, bathe with the Kola seed and offer arghya with the [prescribed] principle.

II.4.92
Then behold Koleśvara and be released from worldly bondage.
Note: This number is repeated as 91 in the extracted Sanskrit; the gloss identifies 92.

II.4.93
[A pool] fifteen hands in measure, attended by gods and Gandharvas: [bathing] there on the bright third day yields a village, grain, and wealth.

II.4.94
Twelve bows south of Indraśaila is renowned Sūryatīrtha, a supreme ford among fords.

II.4.95
The Blessed Lord, seven-horsed, devoted to his chariot, is there invisibly for the world's welfare, all-pervading, himself of yogic form.

II.4.96
Mahāhara has made his perpetual abode here; all the gods likewise come to attend that [place].

II.4.97
Bathe with the Sun seed, and give arghya with Tāra [praṇava or Tārā mantra: uncertain]. Then the practitioner, intent on accomplishment, should go to Rāmakṣetra.

II.4.98
There are two Durgā wells and Brahmayūpa. Drinking Durgā-well water on the fourteenth of Māgha—

II.4.99
—devotedly, [one becomes pregnant: apparent Sanskrit reading]. Repeat the mantra ten thousand times, circumambulate the post, and then arrange the ancestral rite—
Note: Garbhadharā appears in the Sanskrit extraction but is omitted from the Hindi paraphrase; the reading needs collation rather than silent deletion.

II.4.100
—thereby deliver the ancestors and be honoured in Brahmā's world.

II.4.101
Place kākiṇīs, gold, and silver in the well. [One] repays whatever wealth was owed to a friend in a previous birth.

II.4.102
Then go toward Indraśaila facing south; behold Maṇīśvara and be released from sin.

II.4.103
Goddess, even one involved in killing, bondage, or subsidiary sins is freed instantly upon beholding Maṇinātha at Indrakūṭa, [together with the wind: uncertain].

II.4.104
At Lomatīrtha's end, measuring five bows, is Nāgatīrtha, renowned upon earth.

II.4.105
[With the formula] “At Nāgakuṇḍa is Vaikuṇṭha,” bathe and worship the Nāgas. It gives merit among all fords and destroys serpents' poison.

II.4.106
Those mortals who bathe devotedly on Śrāvaṇa's fifth day never have their lineage afflicted by snakes.

II.4.107
To those who perform ancestors' rites there, satisfied Brahmā grants the supreme station, without doubt.

II.4.108
North of Candraśaila, measuring sixty-four [hands], Gayākuṇḍa is in the water and the field is on its bank.

II.4.109
East of Gayāśiras, twenty-two bows, as far as Lohitya, and to the north as far as Brahmayoni—

II.4.110
—is exceedingly secret Gayātīrtha, most dear to ancestors. Having given rice-balls, a person is not born again.

II.4.111
At Agastya, Gayā, and Nīlācala give rice-balls according to the particular journey; at Gayā [give] once, beloved.

II.4.112
The ancestors grieve [at unnecessary repetition], and the effort is vain. The ancestors sing a song which the great seers proclaim:

II.4.113
“Whoever goes to Gayā will deliver us. Many sons, well-conducted and virtuous, should be desired—”

II.4.114
“—for among them, if even one goes to Gayā [we are delivered].” Therefore, with every effort, and especially the Brahmin—

II.4.115
—should go to Gayā, concentrated, and give rice-balls according to rule. Blessed are the mortals who offer rice-balls at Gayā: delivering seven generations on both sides, they attain heaven.

II.4.116
Give rice-balls for others by name, with milk-rice. The seers have prescribed [offering] with piṇyāka, not at home [uncertain syntax].

II.4.117
Devoted people should always give sesame-cake. Perform the ancestral rite there without arghya and invocation.
Note: Piṇyāka is pressed oilseed-cake; the Hindi's identification as a date fruit is not adopted.

II.4.118
Guard [the offering] from rats, vultures, and crows. The ancestral rite at that ford is declared supremely satisfying to the ancestors.

II.4.119
It must be performed there carefully; devotion alone is its cause.

II.4.120
Ancestors are pleased by devotion; pleased, they grant desires: life, sons, wealth, grain, and other desired things.

II.4.121
When Rudra is devotedly worshipped, people's fathers and grandfathers obtain a good state through the Gayā rite, whether at the proper time or otherwise.

II.4.122
Upon reaching it, always bathe and satisfy the ancestors. The rice-ball offering there is exceedingly dear to them.

II.4.123
The ancestors arrive in the sky and look on with expectation. Fulfil their hope with supreme devotion.

II.4.124
Do not delay or cause obstruction. Those people's succession of descendants will always be unbroken.

II.4.125
The ancestors give sons and desire the ancestral rite of increase.

II.4.126
Therefore give them that [offering] in the prescribed manner. The Self-born himself formerly proclaimed the rite; twice-born people devoted to ancestors should perform it swiftly.

II.4.127
At a ford, at an akṣata [uncertain term], at home, at a solar transition, eclipse, equinox, or affliction of the birth-star: these are occasions for ancestral rites, said the Self-born long ago.

II.4.128
When the rite is performed, bodily affliction does not arise; all wrongdoing here and hereafter is abandoned.

II.4.129
Affliction from Yama, planets, thieves, rulers, and the like does not arise; all wrongdoing perishes, and in the next world an auspicious state—

II.4.130
—is obtained, without doubt, like Prajāpati's boon. [Visit] Kāmeśvarī [at “saptaveda”: uncertain], and Aśvakrānta in Kārttika.

II.4.131
The Gayā ancestral rite gives priority to the mother; elsewhere to the father. The discerning person should give sixteen rice-balls, then arrange a larger number.

II.4.132
Ascending Soma mountain, pour streams of milk: “Let Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Maheśvara be my witnesses. Having reached Gayā, I have discharged the three debts.”

II.4.133
Then, amid kettle-drum music and the other sounds, a person should mount a palanquin.

II.4.134
Going home, duly worship the household Goddess; then feed Brahmins and declare the offering imperishable.

II.4.135
Eat together with the Brahmins and provide the ritual fee.

II.4.136
By rice-ball offering here, at Agastya, Viraja, Daśāśvamedhika, and Viṣṇupada—

II.4.137
—one who has offered at one [such place] should avoid repeating the rite. Recalling [the ancestors] again causes a curse to fall upon the head.

II.4.138
Offer rice-balls for three days, especially at Gayā; then for one day at Mātṛgayā. At Agastya and Viraja offer for three days.
Note: The relation of the repeated three-day observance to the prohibition in 137 is not explained here and is not silently reconciled.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Chapter 4 of the Second Part, concerning Kāmarūpa, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, traditionally designated twenty-four thousand [verses].
Coverage: all 138 numbered units. Some mantra and proper-name readings remain provisional; no fully reviewed critical translation is claimed.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 5
Maṇipīṭha and its surrounding sacred geography: English working translation
Source: Mishra edition (1984), printed pages 299–362; PDF pages 319–382. Sanskrit primary; accompanying Hindi consulted. Names, traditional distances and ritual promises are translated as the source's sacred geography. Unclear readings are marked rather than harmonized into a modern map. This is a provisional reading translation.

II.5.1 — The Blessed Lord said:
Beloved, next at clear dawn, the practitioner intent on accomplishment should go to Mānaśaila, northeast of Somaśaila within sight, and then to Vārāṇasī lake.

II.5.2
Northeast of Maṇīśvara, somewhat toward the east, seven bows away, is Vārāṇasī pool.

II.5.3
It is twenty-two bows long, joined by all gods. There are the Goddess Tripathagā, Gomatī, and Sarasvatī—

II.5.4
—Karatoyā, Divyanada, Lohitya, Ghargharā, Sarayū, Dhūtapāpā, and the great river Narmadā—

II.5.5
—Dṛṣadvatī, Devikā, the river Carmaṇvatī, holy Kṛṣṇaveṇī, Śoṇa, and the great river Śyena—

II.5.6
—Kāverī, Yamunā, and others not named. To please me they come to Vārāṇasī pool.

II.5.7
The deep ocean, the milk ocean, [the water ocean], the clarified-butter ocean, liquor ocean, curd ocean, and the sea—

II.5.8
—lakes, rivers, and the various pilgrimage places come on Caitra's fourteenth day, without doubt.

II.5.9
They also come on Vaiśākha's third day, slender-waisted one. Bathing there, one goes to heaven until the dissolution of beings.

II.5.10
“Māyā of the universe, lamp of the world, destroyer of the world's sin: Vārāṇasī pool, give me nectar; homage to you.”

II.5.11
With this mantra, or another [appropriate one], present arghya.

II.5.12
Five bows south of it is Maṇikarṇikā pool, measuring twenty-two bows. No ford equal to Maṇikarṇikā has existed or will exist.

II.5.13
Truly, truly, again truly; this is certainly true: nowhere in the cosmic sphere is there a ford equal to Maṇikarṇikā.
Note: The Sanskrit's extracted number repeats 12; the gloss and sequence give 13.

II.5.14
Great Lady, bathe at midday on beginnings of ages, solar transitions, or eclipses: it destroys great sins.

II.5.15
“Maṇikarṇi, foremost among gods, Maṇīśvarī, lover of jewels, established here, remove sin; Maṇikarṇi, homage to you.”

II.5.16
Bathe with this mantra, bow down, and worship.

II.5.17
Northeast of Maṇi mountain is the river named Maṅgalā: “You who carry milk-like water, purify me from the flood of sins.”

II.5.18
Queen of the gods, bathe with the mantra, bow, and propitiate [her]. Then go to Maṇīśvara; wash with the first mantra, touch with the second, and worship with the third.

II.5.19
[By the measure] of two kalā-hastas this is called the accomplishment-field. [Contemplate the Lord] mounted on a golden seat, joined with rays and bindu.
Note: Both the measurement and visualization are corrupt in the text layer and not adequately clarified by the gloss.

II.5.20
Garga is declared the seer of this mantra of the God of gods; anuṣṭubh its metre, Bhava its deity; apply it to the desired purpose.

II.5.21
[Contemplate] the supreme three-eyed god, the lunar digit rising in his crown, wearing tiger skin; [his four hands bear] trident, boon, fearlessness, and thunderbolt, with red eyes [and the deer: uncertain].

II.5.22
Worship Kṛttivāsas in the centre, the terrible god Hara before him; [then] Bhava, Sāmba, Śakra, and behind, Vāmana called Kāla [arrangement uncertain].
Note: The verse is heavily corrupted and the gloss only paraphrases part of the sequence. Names and directions here remain provisional.

II.5.23
Worship the Śaktis on the lotus petals, Goddess, Vārāṇasī and Kīrti before [them]; outside worship Śrīkaṇṭha and the others, then the planets and directional lords in front.

II.5.24
On the left worship Ananta and Pinākin; on the right Kamalā and Sarvajña [uncertain]. Worship those beginning with Siddheśa in front, each with its own mantra prescribed in its own ritual manual.
Note: The Sanskrit/gloss allocation of Pinākin and Kamalā differs; the arrangement is not securely established.

II.5.25
Maṇinātha, the primordial liṅga, is the imperishable Brahman-stone. Northeast is Maṅgalā, Goddess; beloved, [the field] lies between these.

II.5.26
Worshipped by gods, this Maṇipīṭha field extends three krośas: Kāmeśī on its southern face, Hayagrīva to the west—

II.5.27
—to the north the Kamala liṅga, arisen from Uttarā; Viraja on the eastern face, and Kīlakodbhava to the north.

II.5.28
Elsewhere are twenty million [pools], all arisen on the left; five hundred pools arisen from Ramaṇā.

II.5.29
There are fifteen million liṅgas; in Kali three hundred [are manifest]. A hundred thousand are underground and a hundred and fifty thousand in water, beloved—

II.5.30
—two hundred thousand on mountains, five hundred thousand in caves, seven hundred thousand in the earth-seat, and a hundred thousand at tree roots.

II.5.31
Fifty thousand liṅgas are within pools; at twilight and the twilight divisions, [there is] the purifying Lohitya pool.
Note: The number is extracted as 32 but the gloss gives 31.

II.5.32
In the nava-indu śaka [a coded date], Queen of the gods, all becomes known; in the twilight division of the thirtieth [śaka?], when a Śūdra becomes ruler—

II.5.33
—then Kāmeśvarī appears in the middle portion; at the end Śākeśvarī likewise appears in the middle portion.

II.5.34
At the final division of the śaka Urvaśī becomes manifest; in the earth-śaka [coded date], at Mādhava, Viraja appears in the nectar division, beloved.
Note on 32–34: These chronological codes are corrupt and inconsistent in the gloss. They are not converted into invented historical dates.

II.5.35
Goddess, by worshipping and saluting there one obtains the divine sovereignty gained through a rājasūya sacrifice.

II.5.36
Northwest of Mānaśaila is a mountain named Varāha.

II.5.37
To its southeast is Naranārāyaṇa lake. Bathing and drinking there, one is honoured in Viṣṇu's world.

II.5.38
On its western bank is Hara's Someśvara liṅga, and the ford named Prabhāsa, supremely liberating the dead.

II.5.39
[There the ancestors] sport and receive rice-balls, fulfilling people's desires. Northwest is Vaināyaka ford, eight bows in measure.

II.5.40
Excellent Prabhāsa ford is a hundred bows long. Queen of the gods, twelve bows to its northwest—

II.5.41
—is holy Bindusaras, whose bathing destroys sin; [the region] extends five thousand bows to Maṇi and Soma mountains.

II.5.42
At Bhūliṅga [there are] ten million [liṅgas / bows: uncertain], Yamunā and Sarasvatī; Dhānuṣkokilaka Śambhu and a hundred thousand Kālikās [names/counts uncertain].

II.5.43
East of Nāṭakācala is the mountain named Mataṅga. As far as the mountain [boundary], this is remembered as Śiva's inner sanctuary.

II.5.44
Those who die in the inner sanctuary reach eternal Brahman. Whatever is done there becomes imperishable.

II.5.45
Those dwelling on Maṇiśaila who die there are not born again. Giving there equals Kurukṣetra's [reward], without exception.
Note: The Sanskrit extraction appears positive punarbhavāḥ, conflicting with the gloss's no-rebirth reading; a lost negative may be involved. The running rendering follows the gloss provisionally.

II.5.46
Between Aśvatīrtha and Indra [mountain] the [Brahma] limit is declared. At Varāha's mouth behold the water as Matsyodarī, when in Āṣāḍha's rains Viṣṇu's fish-belly appears.
Note: This seasonal hydrological image is retained without a modern identification.

II.5.47
Then, my beloved, bathe with every effort. Bathing certainly destroys the sins of a hundred births.

II.5.48
In Bhādra or Śrāvaṇa [one gains] half that fruit; in Kārttika, when it appears, more than tenfold fruit.

II.5.49
East of Hasti mountain, somewhat northeast, remain still, behold Bhasmācala, and utter the Kāma [mantra].

II.5.50
At Kīlaka's extremity do not cast the measuring-thread [across] the traversed [place: uncertain].
Note: The clause is not securely recovered from Sanskrit or gloss.

II.5.51
Twelve bows north of that field is Urvaśī, destroyer of every sin.

II.5.52
Bathing there with concentration on Māgha's bright twelfth day or a solar transition yields horse-sacrifice merit.

II.5.53
Never bathe there at day's decline or an eclipse, for the eldest son and wealth are destroyed, Supreme Lady.

II.5.54
[Construct the mantra from] Tāra, śravaṇa, śūnya, Varāha accompanied by fire, full syllables, and fire's wife; it is proclaimed Ananta.
Note: These are encoded phonetic names, partly corrupt; a continuous mantra cannot securely be recovered.

II.5.55
“All pilgrimage places upon earth, oceans and lakes, ending with Urvaśī: remove sin; homage to you.”

II.5.56
Duly bathe with the mantra facing north; offer arghya with the Vāruṇa mantra for prosperity.

II.5.57
Bathing facing east frees one from great poverty; immersion facing Kubera's direction increases wealth, grain, and offspring.

II.5.58
Twelve bows east of it is renowned Sūryatīrtha, [ten thousand?] exceedingly difficult even for gods to obtain.
Note: The number ayuta is present but its syntactic referent is unclear.

II.5.59
Seers, Siddhas, Gandharvas, pilgrimage places and lakes [attend it]. Śaṅkarī, incomparable is Sūryakuṇḍa's greatness.

II.5.60
Goddess, [the formula with] Bhṛgu ending in sky, and Bhṛgu with the heaven-ending, is approved for bathing, worship, arghya, and praise.
Note: The gloss interprets this as praṇava enclosed by Bhṛgu syllables. The corrupt phonetic code is retained without invented expansion.

II.5.61
Bathing on a Sunday seventh day in Caitra or Māgha, one obtains everything and reaches the Sun's world.

II.5.62
“Red-rayed one, born of [Viṃśa: uncertain], destroyer of great sin, auspicious in Hara's mind, remove sin; homage to you.”

II.5.63
Worship with this mantra and bow. Five bows east is the lake named Kāma; bathing there on the thirteenth grants every desire.

II.5.64
A sonless person who bathes with an āmalaka fruit in the mouth obtains a son who becomes a king, lord of earth.

II.5.65
Bathing on Caitra's bright thirteenth grants a kingdom. Present arghya with Kāma [seed] and the mantra to be stated.

II.5.66
“Greatly fortunate Kāmakuṇḍa, consecrated by the goddesses themselves, grant all desires and protect me from sin on every side.”

II.5.67
Lovely-limbed one, whoever offers arghya at Sūryatīrtha one hundred and eight, a thousand, ten thousand, twelve, or eight times obtains the horse-sacrifice's reward.

II.5.68
On Māgha's or Phālguna's seventh day, bathing at sunrise and giving arghya, a person with leprosy is released from sin.

II.5.69
A woman whose menstruation does not occur becomes menstruant after worship and arghya there [within twenty days, according to the gloss].
Note: This is a historical ritual efficacy claim, not medical advice.

II.5.70
Śaṅkarī, whoever offers the Sun arghya in an earthen vessel [escapes] poverty through seven births [negative uncertain in extraction; gloss supports this sense].

II.5.71
A woman whose children have died, worshipping the Sun by day with oleander or arka and āmalaka fruit, and giving a hundred oleanders, never remains childless.

II.5.72
If oleander flowers are unavailable, offer their leaves; red rudrajaṭā and red oleander—

II.5.73
—and other red flowers are approved for worshipping the Sun, Goddess. Oleander is foremost among all flowers.

II.5.74
One oleander equals a thousand red lotuses; with each flower the horse-sacrifice's reward properly arises.

II.5.75
Therefore worship with oleander by every effort. If it is unavailable, silently remember it three times.

II.5.76
Uttering “oleander” [pleases the Sun more than] ten million repetitions. No other [flower] pleases Bhāskara as oleander does.

II.5.77
Performing the seventh-day vow at Sūryatīrtha once within a year purifies seven family lines.

II.5.78
Know afternoon as its best time. Queen of the gods, falling short or exceeding [the timing] brings no accomplishment on earth.

II.5.79
Avoid twice-cooked food, clarified butter, kalāya pulse, kaśeru, ginger, salt, and astringent [foods]—

II.5.80
—sour, bitter, and spoiled foods. Eat from a stone vessel, never silver or copper.

II.5.81
Ten bows south of Madana is the ford named Gaṅgāsarit. Bathing there brings great reward.

II.5.82
Bathing on the Gaṅgā's bank and satisfying ancestors and gods at a solar transition or eclipse, one attains Brahmā's world.

II.5.83
“Born from dust of Viṣṇu's feet, Gaṅgā, flowing in three paths, fluid of dharma, foremost river, protect me from all sins.”

II.5.84
In Libra or Capricorn and on the bright eighth day, radiant one, merely bathing makes a person honoured in Viṣṇu's world.

II.5.85
Eight bows south is supreme Agastya ford, giving enjoyment and liberation to the dead. A mortal who goes and immerses obtains omniscience.

II.5.86
Mahādeva himself and Viṣṇu stand there. For Kāmarūpā's sport [there is] excellent Agastya pool—

II.5.87
—pure, removing all sin, attended by Viṣṇu, Brahmā and the others, saluted by gods, Dānavas, and Vidyādharas, always granting desires—

II.5.88
—with charming steps adorned with many jewels. The Great Goddess's four pools remove piercing affliction.

II.5.89
Beautiful-faced one, merely bathing on the tenth of the bright fortnight of Māgha or Kārttika yields Puṣkara's reward.

II.5.90
Sin done through a hundred births vanishes instantly; at an equinox or day-decline, the sin of a thousand births [vanishes].

II.5.91
Great Lady, avoid bathing in Pauṣa, Cancer, or on the dark eighth day, for it causes loss of one's wife.

II.5.92
As Vārāṇasī is holy, so is this, without doubt. Goddess, this ford is exceedingly secret; do not doubt it.

II.5.93
This is the most secret field, supreme and more secret still. Going there, a person is immediately freed of every sin.

II.5.94
There are Mahādeva, the Goddess Sarasvatī, all rivers beginning with Gaṅgā, and the seven oceans—

II.5.95
—rivers beginning with Śoṇa, all fords and lakes. What more [can be said] of Parameṣṭhin's pool? Lotus-eyed one, I cannot describe it fully.

II.5.96
“As you swiftly protect the entire moving and unmoving three worlds, so protect me always. Foremost ford, homage to you.”

II.5.97
Southeast of that field, somewhat toward the west, is the ford named Vāsava, measuring twenty-one bows.

II.5.98
Bathe at supreme Vāsava ford and worship Vāsava with Śakra's seed, Queen of the gods; reach the desired heavenly abode. Offer arghya with the following mantra.

II.5.99
“Great ford named Vāsava, destroyer of every sin, immersed in worldly waters [uncertain construction], grant the promised fruit.”

II.5.100
Great Lady, a little distance west is Rambhātīrtha, measuring seven bows.

II.5.101
A man bathing at Rambhātīrtha becomes handsome. A faithful woman who knowingly dies there with her husband reaches Rambhā's world, then Hari's dwelling.
Note: This is the historical text's praise of co-death; it is not an instruction or endorsement.

II.5.102
There is no doubt. On Thursday [say], “Auspicious one arisen from Brahmā's act, giver of every desire—”

II.5.103
“—fluid of desire, homage to you; rescue me from worldly existence's ocean.” Bathe and offer arghya to Rambhā with this mantra.

II.5.104
In the field's western portion is Rukmiṇī pool, thirty bows in measure; bathing there, one goes to Brahmā's city.

II.5.105
A woman or man washing the face there becomes beautiful in the next world, without doubt.

II.5.106
