Bathe with Kandarpa's seed; hear the face-washing mantra: “Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Maheśa washed their faces in you. Truly grant me beauty like the Aśvins.”

II.5.107
Northwest of that field, eight bows in measure, is the ancestors' supreme ford; bathing brings the supreme state.

II.5.108
“Greatly fortunate ancestral ford, honoured by the eight gods themselves, cause of satisfaction, purify me from terrible sin.”

II.5.109
Bathing there with this mantra gives the pitṛmedha sacrifice's fruit. Go south of Agastya, bathe, and satisfy [the ancestors]—

II.5.110
—at the lake Gavākṣagati, four bows in measure. Going there on the seventh day, one is free of ancestral debt.

II.5.111
“Gayātīrtha, great ford, none equals you for the ancestors. Purifying among all fords, purify me from exceeding sin.”

II.5.112
Bathe with this [mantra], put on washed garments, apply the forehead mark, and circumambulate the field.

II.5.113
Go to Daśāśvakṣetra and offer rice-balls with concentration. Goddess, the discerning person should satisfy ancestors with sixteen services.

II.5.114
[Worship] with milk, honey, and water from the [divine] foot. There are sixteen goddesses, one hand apart; worship each, beginning from the south.

II.5.115
Bathing in Gayā well and beholding the god Umāpati, a person immediately delivers oneself and ten generations before and after.

II.5.116
Viṣṇu, Brahmā, Rudra, Agastya, Śatakratu, Krauñca, Gaṇeśa, Kumāra, Prajāpati—

II.5.117
—Cyavana, Kaśyapa, and Pulastya [dwell here] in order, having come [from] Aśvakrānta on the nectar day [uncertain]. Here [offer] the mother's rice-ball separately; elsewhere together with her husband.

II.5.118
Whoever's name receives a rice-ball at Daśāśvamedha is firmly established in heaven and attains liberation there.

II.5.119
“Those ancestors in our lineage whose rice-ball and water rites have lapsed; those without a first tonsure, and those lost from the womb—”

II.5.120
“—those never married, those burned by fire and the others: let them be satisfied by what is offered on earth; satisfied, let them reach the supreme state.

II.5.121
“Father, grandfather, great-grandfather; mother, grandmother, great-grandmother—

II.5.122
“—maternal grandfather and his father; those killed by lions, tigers, other attackers, fanged or horned creatures: I give them a rice-ball.

II.5.123
“Those burned by fire, those not burned, those killed by lightning or thieves: I give them a rice-ball.

II.5.124
“Those reborn as animals, birds, insects, reptiles, or trees: I give them a rice-ball.

II.5.125
“Those established among innumerable [beings or births], those taken under Yama's command: for their deliverance I give this rice-ball.

II.5.126
“Those who wander through thousands of different births by their own actions, those who have entered human birth: I give them a rice-ball.

II.5.127
“Others dwelling in places of torment and inhabitants of the departed spirits' world: for their deliverance I give this rice-ball.

II.5.128
“Relatives, non-relatives, and relatives from another birth: may they all obtain satisfaction forever through this rice-ball gift.

II.5.129
“Those born in the father's lineage or the mother's, among the guru's or father-in-law's relatives, and other remembered relatives—

II.5.130
“—those whose lineage has lost its rice-ball offerings, bereft of sons or spouses, deprived of rites, born blind or lame—

II.5.131
“—deformed or miscarried, born within my lineage: may the rice-ball I give them stand imperishably.

II.5.132
“Relatives born in my father's lineage or my mother's, those who became servants in both these lineages, dependants and attendants—

II.5.133
“—friends, companions, all well-wishers, those touched or seen, benefactors, and those associated with me in another birth: I give them this final rice-ball.”
Note: Some social terms in 132–133 are corrupt; the comprehensive intention is retained without inventing unprinted categories.

II.5.134
Northwest of Sūryakuṇḍa, a bow-staff away, stands the god Gadādhara. Bow there and give [the rice-balls].

II.5.135
“Let the gods headed by Brahmā and Īśāna be my witnesses: having reached Gayā, I have accomplished the ancestors' release.

II.5.136
“Gadādhara, God, I have come to Gayā for ancestral duty. Blessed Lord, you alone are witness: I am free of the three debts.”

II.5.137
Among the ancestral rice-balls give sixteen; make abundant rice-balls and pour streams of milk.

II.5.138
Śaṅkarī, not far to Gadādhara's left is Mātṛgayā; Goddess, to the south is Sutīrthaka.

II.5.139
Carefully bowing to the god Gadādhara, Keśava Puruṣottama, fear no longer arises for people.
Note: The gloss substitutes no further birth for freedom from fear; the apparent Sanskrit is followed.

II.5.140
Silently behold great-souled Maunāditya, particularly Kanakārka; the Brahmin seer follows the ancestors. Worshipping Brahmā, one attains Brahmā's world.

II.5.141
The lofty shining stone on Urvaśī's southern bank should be known as Gāyatrī; worship it with perfumes and sandalwood.

II.5.142
Rising in the morning, approach Gāyatrī by name and carefully perform twilight worship: one obtains all the Vedas' fruit.

II.5.143
Beholding Sāvitrī at midday gives sacrifice's reward. At Daśāśvamedha stands the wealth-giving God of gods Janārdana.

II.5.144
Rice-ball offerings there bring eternal satisfaction.

II.5.145
At Gayā Janārdana, God of gods, is in ancestral form. Seeing that lotus-eyed one releases one from the three debts.

II.5.146
Beholding the god Pitāmaha releases one from all sins.

II.5.147
Giving rice-balls at Agastya when [the Sun is] in Capricorn or at lunar and solar eclipses is a rare opportunity in the three worlds.

II.5.148
Whether a son or another, whoever casts a rice-ball by name in Gayā well at Aśvamedhika leads that [recipient] to eternal Brahman.

II.5.149
Brahmā established that place and the Brahmins; when they are worshipped, all gods together with ancestors are worshipped.

II.5.150
Satisfy Gayā's Brahmins according to the offerings for gods and ancestors. Giving and renunciation are prescribed at Gayā.

II.5.151
One who gives a great gift or releases a bull at Daśāśvamedhika has no rebirth.

II.5.152
The Agastya field measures sixty-four bows; fifty-five fords are described there, slender-waisted one.

II.5.153
Urvaśī, Sūrya, Kāma, Putra, Vāsava, Agastya, Aśvamedha, and Gayābila are the essence of pilgrimage places.

II.5.154
Those dying through hanging or a noose at the throat, and a woman who has entered the pyre, have no [ordinary] rites: no final sacrifice, cremation, or impurity [observance] is prescribed for them.
Note: The historical classification and its exclusion from ordinary funerary rites belong to this text.

II.5.155
At Agastya or Gayā perform their rites for three nights, beginning at new moon and continuing three nights.

II.5.156
Whoever foolishly performs their rites elsewhere has fruitless rites. Perform the cāndrāyaṇa vow; thereby gain purity, otherwise become an inhabitant of hell.

II.5.157
Sin committed at the Horse Ford vanishes at Gayā; sin committed at Gayā vanishes at Rāmakṣetra.

II.5.158
Sin committed at Rāmakṣetra vanishes at Maṇikūṭa; sin at Maṇikūṭa vanishes on Nīla mountain.

II.5.159
Sin committed on Nīla mountain vanishes in the inner sanctuary; sin in the inner sanctuary vanishes at Somatīrtha.

II.5.160
Sin committed at Somatīrtha is removed at Maṅgalā; sin at Maṅgalā vanishes at Agastya.

II.5.161
Sin committed at Agastya vanishes at Mandara; sin at Mandara vanishes at Vajralepa.

II.5.162
Sin from Vajralepa vanishes at Aśvakrānta; sin committed at Aśvakrānta is removed at Urvaśī.

II.5.163
Great Lady, spend the day hearing the greatness and the compendium; at night contemplate Viṣṇu. Staying there, do not eat at night.

II.5.164
Then on the next day, at dawn [kālya: uncertain], bathe at Agastya.

II.5.165
The stream touching Bhasmācala should be known as Sarasvatī. Going there gives the agniṣṭoma's reward, Great Lady.

II.5.166
“Auspicious one established on Viṣṇu's breast, lover of nectar, Supreme Lady, remove the sin I have accumulated birth after birth.”

II.5.167
Bathe with this mantra, especially at Kārttikī.

II.5.168
East of the god stands a beautiful tank. Drinking its clear water, one is not born again.

II.5.169
Southeast of Bhasmaśaila, eight bows in measure, is the supremely purifying ford named Piśācamocana.

II.5.170
At Piśācamocana worship the trident-bearer. This is the god's excellent liṅga named Kapardīśvara.

II.5.171
Northwest of Bhasmakūṭa, measuring five bows—

II.5.172
—is Kapālalocana, foremost among fords. Carefully worship and praise with diverse hymns.

II.5.173
Beautiful one, my skull fell at that place. Bathing there removes the sin of Brahmin-killing.

II.5.174
I am Kapāleśvara Īśāna, established at that ford. Five bows north is Kapilā; bathing there releases one from worldly bondage.

II.5.175
With restrained mind bathe at Kapilāhrada, behold bull-bannered Śiva, and obtain all sacrifices' fruit.

II.5.176
Facing east, ascend Bhasmakūṭa. Worship with the mantra to be stated and receive praise [or merit].

II.5.177
“Vṛṣācala, homage to you, heaven of dharma's path. I ascend the summit; Bhasmakūṭa, homage to you.”

II.5.178
If one ascends the mountain facing west, merit accumulated through ten births is instantly destroyed.

II.5.179
If one faces north or northeast while ascending, wealth, sons, and spouse all instantly perish.

II.5.180
I shall explain another auspicious doorway, more secret than the secret. Beautiful-faced Goddess, hear the greatness of Vṛṣadhvaja.

II.5.181
If new moon coincides with Monday, go to Bhasmācala and carefully worship the god; delivering twenty-one family lines, one reaches the supreme state.

II.5.182
Bathe the liṅga with perfumes and charming lotuses; anoint it with the five nectars, water, and sandalwood.

II.5.183
A great-souled person should do this at every festival at month's end. Worship with bilva leaves and bathe with jewel-water.

II.5.184
Worship with the Prāsāda mantra, rudra flowers, bandhūka, jayanta, and especially mālūra.
Note: The gloss identifies mālūra differently; its botanical identity is not asserted here.

II.5.185
Contemplate the yellow God of gods, Pinākin, shining with his three eyes, remover of every flood of sin, bearing five faces—

II.5.186
—wearing tiger skin, white with ashes, his body lovely as moonstone, [his or the Goddess's body] blue, graciously touching the Goddess with his hand.
Note: The colour and body references are grammatically uncertain; the English retains the unresolved contrast.

II.5.187
On the petals worship these deities of Parameṣṭhin: Dhāma, Dharma, Sūkṣma, Viṣṇu, Nārāyaṇa, Hara—

II.5.188
—Vijñeya, Viraja, and Viśva, beginning from the centre. Worship the Śaktis beginning with Rāmā, whose characteristics have been stated.

II.5.189
Outside worship devotedly Śrīkaṇṭha and the others; outside them [worship] the seat-lords, and worship the seat-lords in front.
Note: Repetition of pīṭheśa is present in the extracted text and not silently removed.

II.5.190
With the Tryambaka mantra, worship [all] except Kamalā; then Nandīśa, Mukuṭa, and the directional guardians in order.

II.5.191
Having devotedly worshipped the Lord of gods, bow to the Supreme Lord and utter this hymn:

II.5.192
“Oṃ, homage to you of every colour, golden, matted-haired; homage to you whose crest is made of gold, lord of gold.

II.5.193
“Īśāna, arisen from the thunderbolt, tawny-haired one, homage to you; homage to him whose ears are like the rising sun, bearer of a blazing form.

II.5.194
“Homage to the impure and pure, to good fortune and dissolution; homage to Bhavāṅga, of boundless hair and loose hair.

II.5.195
“Homage to him delighted in the six rites and devoted to the three rites; who duly sets in motion the distinct duties of social classes and stages of life.

II.5.196
“Homage to Śośīya and Śośa [uncertain epithets], homage to you as the cause; white and tawny-eyed, dark-faced and dark-eyed.

II.5.197
“To dharma, desire, prosperity, and liberation, remover of all sin; homage to the trident-holder, homage to Umā's beloved.

II.5.198
“Arisen from Īśāna's face, tawny-haired one, homage to you. Be gracious, Pārvatī's beloved; homage to Umānanda.”

II.5.199
Then receive permission with folded hands, bow and worship, and utter this mantra:

II.5.200
“Umānanda, homage to you, increaser of Pārvatī's delight. Let my accomplishment proceed unobstructed, for today I have performed your worship.

II.5.201
“Lord of the world, through your grace, Great Lord, Lord of gods, at your command I now worship glorious Kāmeśvara Śiva.”

II.5.202
Worship Viśvaksena Janārdana before him. West of the god is Mātaṅga field, twenty-two bows in measure; dwelling there one does not grieve.
Note: Sanskrit says west; the Hindi says south. The Sanskrit is followed provisionally.

II.5.203
Whatever sin is committed there equals Brahmin-killing; whatever small good is done bestows the agniṣṭoma's reward.

II.5.204
Devoted and concentrated, worship Mātaṅgī there with perfumes and the Māyā seed, Queen of the gods.

II.5.205
Whoever stands there facing south and sees Mandara delivers the whole lineage and is honoured in Brahmā's world.

II.5.206
“Homage to Mandara mountain, to the Creator in Viṣṇu's form.” Beholding it, one reaches heaven through removal of worldly sin.

II.5.207
Then behold Sandhyācala by day and utter: “Sin accumulated through thousands of millions of ages is destroyed at Gajavaktra. Elephant-faced one, be my witness.”

II.5.208
Then offer the Sun arghya with sesame, water, and kuśa. Rising and bowing down, present water for sipping.

II.5.209
In a vow for Āditya, especially while offering arghya, give it seated; elsewhere give arghya standing.

II.5.210
The discerning person should invert the arghya vessel after offering; bow to Caṇḍeśvara Sūrya there and dismiss [the deity].

II.5.211
Beautiful one, southwest of Daśāśvamedha and south of me, a sugarcane-throw away, stands Kali mountain—

II.5.212
—merely ascending it destroys merit; suffering stains the body, and Kali touches it, without exception.

II.5.213
The stream Kali touches is my channel. It is entirely stained with Kali's impurity; therefore avoid it.

II.5.214
Northeast of Mandara is the great Cakratīrtha, sixteen bows in measure, destroying every sin, planetary defect and much committed wrongdoing—

II.5.215
—and the fault of seeing the Moon in Leo on the fourth of either fortnight: all that fault and sin vanishes through bathing.
Note: The Sanskrit connects the Moon sighting to the fault removed; the Hindi paraphrase ambiguously makes it a bathing time. The Sanskrit sequence is followed.

II.5.216
My beloved, whoever deposits bones [within] seven nights becomes marked by the discus, Goddess, without doubt.

II.5.217
A being who undertakes a vow at Cakratīrtha obtains [its] fruit by wearing gopīcandana and goes to Hari's city at death.

II.5.218
“Arisen at Dvārakā, twice-born [uncertain epithet], king of fords, homage to you; rescue me from worldly bondage and existence's ocean.”

II.5.219
Bathe with this mantra and offer the Sun arghya. Bathing at Cakratīrtha and worshipping the Creator delivers oneself and ten generations before and after.

II.5.220
The mountain touching Cakratīrtha is named Nandana, sixty-two bows in measure, beautiful one; to its west—

II.5.221
—stands Janārdana, Lord of gods, in Buddha's form in Kali. Beholding him releases one from exceedingly terrible sins.

II.5.222
The rough stone raised [at the ford], like a pauṇḍra [uncertain], west of Mandara and [south] of the auspicious mountain, is declared Janārdana's mark and form.

II.5.223
North and northeast of that mountain is Viraja; south of Gajaśaila and to the west is Śaṅkaraliṅgaka.

II.5.224
The field between these I name Agastya. Thus a hundred-measured field is declared to arise from me.
Note: The unit and the compound matsambhava remain uncertain.

II.5.225
Know this without doubt; another [ford] is called Lohita. Offering rice-balls there gives ancestors the supreme state.

II.5.226
Present a svāhā rice-ball into Janārdana's hand: “Janārdana, this rice-ball I have given into your hand—”

II.5.227
“—you will give it to me when I have gone to the next world.” Eight bows east of Kaliśeṣa—

II.5.228
—is that stone, in departed-spirit form, for delivering ancestors. Offering rice-balls there, one never becomes a preta.

II.5.229
Two bows southeast of Cakratīrtha is supreme Liṅgalola ford; satisfy [the deity] with sesame and milk.

II.5.230
Then behold Janārdana and be freed of the three debts.

II.5.231
At the junction of Dvāpara and Kali, Śukra established the liṅga [measuring eight bows], remembered as Śukreśa—

II.5.232
—who, seeing Śukreśvara, is not freed from bondage? Then behold Goleśvara and be freed from Brahmin-killing.

II.5.233
Behold Aṅgāreśa, Siddheśa, Gayāditya, Gaja, and Mārkaṇḍeyeśvara and become free of ancestral debt.

II.5.234
Seeing Goleśvara and Janārdana at Gayā is more than a means of human purification: twenty-one generations go to Brahmā's world.

II.5.235
All fords, oceans, and lakes upon earth go to Cakratīrtha once every day.

II.5.236
On earth holy Gayā [is foremost]; within Gayā, Kūpagayā. Goddess, auspicious Mātṛgayā is eight times greater than the well.

II.5.237
A son going to Mātṛgayā is freed of debt instantly; offering rice-balls at Gayā releases ancestral debt.

II.5.238
Rice-ball offering ends at Gayā, pilgrimage ends at Gayā, Kāmarūpa ends at Pañcaka, and rivers at Picchilā, auspicious one. Offer one's own rice-ball into Janārdana's hand.
Note: “Ends” carries both a boundary and a culminating-completion sense; the gloss emphasizes completion of pilgrimage duty.

II.5.239
At Viraja, the Horse Ford, [Karṇivāma: uncertain], and Somaka, offering a living person's rice-ball makes the person short-lived.

II.5.240
Whoever gives one's own rice-ball at the great Agastya field gradually increases life's span by a year and two months.

II.5.241
One who personally performs bull-release and one's own post-mortem rite obtains its fruit in the next world, Great Lady, without doubt; whether with many sons, one son, or a son devoted to yoga—

II.5.242
—or a son afflicted by consumption or leprosy, or three sons, Great Lady: after forty, Goddess, one should personally perform one's own rite.
Note: The son's circumstances are syntactically uneven; the gloss supports this general sequence without resolving every alternative.

II.5.243
Perform gifts and bull-release, but not one's own tenth-day rite. While both spouses live, always perform two bull-releases.

II.5.244
In one diagram and one fire-pit, perform the bull-releases separately. For the dead [there is] onefold reward; for the living eightfold.

II.5.245
What is done for a different gotra obtains only scant fruit.

II.5.246
Preparing to go to Gayā, perform the ancestral rite with ghee, put on the pilgrim's guise, circumambulate the village, go to another village, eat the rite's remainder, and depart without accepting gifts.

II.5.247
When going to Agastya, from a mere cubit beyond one's house, every step is a staircase for the ancestors' ascent to heaven.

II.5.248
At Agastya one may always perform ancestral rites by day or night. Ancestral rites at Aśvatīrtha, Nīlakūṭa, Pañcaka, Rāmāśrama, and Somakūṭa lead ancestors to heaven.

II.5.249
After tonsure at Agastya, behold Janārdana and ascend Mandara mountain: seven family lines are purified.

II.5.250
If the annual Gayā rite, the festival ancestral rite at every festival, and the two equinox rites are performed without meat—

II.5.251
—the connected ancestors depart disappointed. At an āma ancestral rite give raw meat without hesitation.
Note: The text's historical meat prescription is retained; āma can also designate an uncooked offering rite, so no modern dietary rule is inferred.

II.5.252
My beloved, the ancestors remain downcast. Whoever performs an ancestral rite with meat but eats without meat—

II.5.253
—goes to Tāmisra hell with the ancestors, not otherwise. Śāṅkarī, [the concluding injunction is corrupt].

II.5.254
Perform this vow apart from the ancestral observance, my beloved. A vegetarian should perform a vegetarian ancestral rite—

II.5.255
—and, having eaten, should eat vegetarian food, never meat. Invite [the Brahmins] immediately and perform the rite discerningly.
Note: The tension between these verses and 250–253 is retained, not harmonized into one universal prescription.

II.5.256
Whoever feeds at ancestral rites or sacrifices a Brahmin censured by a Śūdra eats filth, Śāṅkarī.
Note: This is the historical text's polemical social rule, not an endorsement.

II.5.257
Buffalo, large goat, antelope, cāmara, monitor lizard, tortoise, śālva, hare, pig, boar, and sheep should all be given at an ancestral rite.
Note: Several animal names are uncertain and retained without an asserted modern species identification.

II.5.258
Not [in?] Kali, cow's flesh and dog's [flesh]; a goat with impaired organs, and what is not potent, should be avoided.
Textual caution: The era-related negative is grammatically uncertain; the Hindi reverses its scope. This verse is not offered as a settled dietary injunction.

II.5.259
Whoever satisfies ancestors with black goat's meat has those ancestors depart disappointed, uttering a terrible curse.

II.5.260
Satisfying ancestors with the flesh of a goat with defective organs is declared exceedingly dreadful; therefore avoid it.

II.5.261
Buffalo flesh satisfies for twelve years; goat for a year; mountain-goat for six years.

II.5.262
Cāmara [satisfies] a hundred years; monitor-lizard flesh [a thousand / six months: uncertain]; tortoise, a month, beautiful one.
Note: The numerical order is corrupt in Sanskrit/gloss; the disagreement is recorded rather than turned into a confident table.

II.5.263
Boar [satisfies] six months, hare nine, small boar twenty-two months, and cāmara a hundred years. Avoid gross meat.

II.5.264
What four or six people reject is called excessively gross by the earlier sages.
Note: The Hindi combines four and six into ten; the apparent Sanskrit alternatives are retained.

II.5.265
My beloved, buffalo, cow/bovine, boar, deer, and large-goat meat may be given even when gross.

II.5.266
Rhinoceros and five-nailed [animals] are edible; [the next horn/hoof clause is corrupt]. Avoid four-nailed aquatic [animals], my beloved.

II.5.267
The learned person should avoid monitor lizard, golden rhinoceros, cāmara, kṛṣṇa, and tortoise if marked with the discus.
Note: The species list and “discus-marked” condition are retained without a speculative zoological reconstruction.

II.5.268
Siṃha, śubha, rohita, rājīva, citraka, mahāśalka, proṣṭhika, and mountain fish—

II.5.269
—large rohita, large proṣṭhika, large śalka, and citra should carefully be served at the ancestral rite.

II.5.270
Avoid scaleless fish and serpent-shaped fish. Among the scaleless, the two kavacas are to be given.

II.5.271
Avoid pratodhāna and the others, malformed [fish], snake-mouthed, pīvara, and rajanī fish.

II.5.272
Carefully avoid jīvāśa, seluka, padmaka, kuṅkuma, svarṇaka, and granthivarṇa at ancestral rites.
Note on 268–272: These are vernacular or traditional fish-names. Modern species are not confidently assigned from the corrupt text layer.

II.5.273
Avoid dhūmra, pañcakadala, [sugar or insect-infested food: uncertain], buffalo ghee, milk, and buttermilk at an ancestral rite.

II.5.274
Coconut, palm, date, pīnasa, and buttermilk [are excluded]; except ghee and milk, carefully avoid [the remaining items].
Note: The punctuation and scope of exceptions in 273–274 are uncertain; the conflict is left visible.

II.5.275
Avoid a defective lamp, [directly pressed] oil, safflower, nālikā greens, and jasmine flowers—

II.5.276
—lotus-born [substances] and oleander at the ancestral rite of increase. Do not give gāṅgeya [uncertain] or a red water-born lotus.

II.5.277
An ancestral rite without cloth, sacred threads, sesame, or cow-products is fruitless, Queen of the gods.

II.5.278
If cloth is unavailable, offer a kuśa garland; if sacred thread is unavailable, place two threads.

II.5.279
Omit the sacred thread at a Śūdra's or woman's ancestral rite. Give betel without lime, Śāṅkarī; if unavailable give jīvaka, and milk-rice with honey.

II.5.280
Offer food attentively in vessels of one kind. First give the divine portion, then offer in the ancestral vessel.

II.5.281
Never offer ancestral leftovers again to the gods. At an uncooked ancestral rite without fire, never wash the grain.

II.5.282
At increase-rites, solar transitions, and eclipses, wash the grain. Give each Brahmin eight handfuls in sequence, neither more nor less in the ancestral rite.

II.5.283
Whoever performs an ancestral rite on a beautiful lotus leaf satisfies [the ancestors] for over a hundred years.

II.5.284
On an aśvattha leaf or Brahma vessel, Goddess, Śāṅkarī, satisfaction lasts six months; on a banyan leaf, infinitely.

II.5.285
In copper, a month; in gold, a year; in silver, tenfold is declared; in rhinoceros-horn, over a hundredfold.

II.5.286
Use vessels of one kind on the death anniversary and ancestral rite; [different kinds may be used] at the festival and increase rites.

II.5.287
Use rice [stored] for a year; avoid rice older than a year at a preta ancestral rite.
Note: The extracted Sanskrit and gloss are uneven about exactly one year versus older rice; this is provisional.

II.5.288
Avoid rain-grown grain, chickpeas, sesame, and barley cooked twice at sacrifices and ancestral rites.

II.5.289
Yaṣṭidhānya, rājadhānya, bṛhaddhānya, somadhānya, snigdhadhānya, vāṅga, and red śāli—

II.5.290
—ketakī, kalaviṅka, nārāyaṇa, mādhava, pradīpa, and beloved viṣṇudhānya—

II.5.291
—bhogya, aśoka, nāgākṣa, and pañcaka: these grains are stated in the Vedas as fit for ancestral rites.

II.5.292
Great Lady, [use] wheat, barley, cakes, nīvāra, and devadhānya for ancestral rites. Carefully avoid grain planted in spring.

II.5.293
Eating that food causes sin to enter people; eating śravaṇa grain makes one poor.

II.5.294
For eating somadhānya, perform the cāndrāyaṇa vow. Eating aged grain produces prosperity.
Note: This apparently conflicts with somadhānya's inclusion in 289; the source is not silently reconciled.

II.5.295
Eating rājadhānya and snigdhadhānya gives Viṣṇu's world; eating red śāli rice brings victory and fortune.

II.5.296
My beautiful one, eating nārāyaṇa, mādhava, bhoga, and yādava grains brings renown.

II.5.297
Whoever excludes a Brahmin already invited to an ancestral rite goes to terrible hell until dissolution.

II.5.298
An invited Brahmin who somehow eats at his own house goes to Kālasūtra and enters a sow's womb.
Note: The Hindi assigns the punishment to the inviter; the Sanskrit's apparent subject is the invited Brahmin. The disagreement remains marked.

II.5.299
Do not pass over an invited Brahmin who observes a vow or celibacy at an ancestral rite. There is no fault in eating honey.

II.5.300
Goddess, there is no fault when a vow-observer eats freshly prepared grain, meat, and oil in the ancestral sacrifice.

II.5.301
Beloved, do not smell the rice-balls at Gayā's rite, in the ancestors' fortnight, after a subsequent death, at a pilgrimage rite, or an eclipse.

II.5.302
A Brahmin woman should not follow her husband [in death] except at a liberation-ford; [the verse specifies the same rather than a separate pyre].
Note: The historical co-death prescription is recorded non-instructionally, not endorsed.

II.5.303
A ten-day rite is to be performed [there]; elsewhere it is excluded. For one dead by hanging or a corpse recovered from water—

II.5.304
—or an old corpse or one affected by consumption or leprosy, a Brahmin woman should not follow [in death], beloved, except at a liberation-ford.

II.5.305
[The historical co-death rule excludes] a woman with many sons, one who is pregnant or menstruating, one who has fallen [from prescribed conduct], one given to quarrels, or one regarded as unfaithful: she is never to follow [her husband in death].

II.5.306
For the purpose of that following, the text permits keeping the corpse for one day and says that following on the next day entails no fault.
Note: This and the following verses record a historical co-death prescription, not a recommendation or instruction for present conduct.

II.5.307
If the husband died abroad and an article of his is available, [the rule for] Kṣatriyas and the other [classes] describes following him with that article held at the breast.

II.5.308
If a Śūdra woman is devoted in feeling and regarded as faithful, the text allows her to follow in death; the same rule is remembered for a Vaiśya woman.

II.5.309
If the husband died on the third lunar day, she may follow even on the fourth. The discerning person should perform her annual rite on the husband's death-tithi.

II.5.310
Goddess, when death occurs together, offer the rice-balls together and perform the ancestral rite simultaneously; completing it thus brings no fault.

II.5.311
For husband and wife, make the rice-ball round, cover it with a garment, and pour honey and milk upon it.

II.5.312
Never [offer] milk with the rice-ball, dried mango, ghee, buffalo [ghee], dhātrī, or lakuca—

II.5.313
—pomegranate, citron, urvāruka fruit, jambū fruit, padmākṣa, plantain, or rāmaka: leave them aside.
Note on312–313: The extracted sāhiṣyādyaṃ is understood as buffalo ghee by the Hindi. The padmākṣa reading is also supported by the gloss. Milk is prescribed in311 yet prohibited in312; the apparent conflict is preserved rather than silently harmonized. Several plant names are retained without speculative identifications.

II.5.314
Carefully give kaśeru, yugāna, milk of a tawny cow, and ripe jambū fruit at the ancestral rite.

II.5.315
For the Brahmins [offer] areca, honey, milk, root [vegetables], karamardaka, bilva, tinduka, madhuka, madhurī, jambū fruit, padmākṣa, and jīvantī.
Note: The opening words are poorly recognized; “areca” follows the Hindi gloss. The apparent overlap with the preceding prohibited list remains unresolved in this edition.

II.5.316
The ancestral rite is proclaimed for Brahmins, Kṣatriyas, and Vaiśyas [with an unclear qualifying expression]. According to Kula dharma, [the offering] should be made with mantra.
Note: The term extracted as sucavasodita is uncertain. The Hindi adds permission from guru and Brahmins, which is not inserted as an assured Sanskrit reading.

II.5.317
For Śūdras, according to the Brahmins' instruction, [the rite is performed] duly without mantra and without cooking by fire—

II.5.318
—at pilgrimage places beginning with Puṣkara, holy sanctuaries, the peaks of great mountains, and holy regions, Śaṅkarī—

II.5.319
—rivers with sacred water, lakes and great streams, river-confluences, and the seven oceans—

II.5.320
—at a deity's sanctuary, a cattle enclosure, the foot of a dhātrī tree, the roots of divine trees, and within a tulasī grove—

II.5.321
—in Daśārṇa, Kumārī, Magadha, and Kuśa; on Viraja's northern bank and Lauhitya's southern [bank]—

II.5.322
—south of the Narmadā and south of Agastya, and east of the Karatoyā: the ancestral offering is said not to be given.
Textual caution on317–322: The negative injunction concluding this long list is explicit in the extracted Sanskrit and Hindi. Its scope is difficult, especially because the text elsewhere commends ancestral rites at several of these locations. It may qualify a particular class or form of offering; this draft does not silently remove the negative or claim a settled reconciliation. The geographical names follow this edition.

II.5.323
They say that the ancestral rite should be given at the waning end of each month. On the full moon also, perform it within [the appropriate] stellar occasion.
Note: The final akṣagocara expression is uncertain; no particular constellation is supplied conjecturally.

II.5.324
The daily ancestral rite is described as accompanied by the divine [offering] and human [recipients]; so too the occasional and the daily-occasional ancestral rites.
Textual caution: The arrangement of nitya, naimittika, sura, and manuṣya is compressed and partly corrupt. This is a provisional construction, not a definite classification reconstructed from later ritual manuals.

II.5.325
A Brahmin should perform ancestral rites for desired results every year, and the prosperity ancestral rite on the occasions of the stated ceremonies.

II.5.326
Śaṅkarī, know the bathing [or rite] there as beginning with the mother. When the sun enters Virgo, the discerning person should perform ancestral rites for fifteen days according to the pārvaṇa procedure.
Note: The opening phrase is imperfectly recovered. “Beginning with the mother” is retained rather than replaced with an inferred sequence of ancestors.

II.5.327
Whoever gives sesame mixed with jaggery at the ancestral rite, with mantra and mixed with honey, [makes an offering] called imperishable.
Note: The source's numerical reading is misrecognized as227/397; the sequence establishes327.

II.5.328
Worshipping the ancestors under Kṛttikā, a person attains liberation. One desiring offspring [acts] under Rohiṇī; under Saumya, one obtains brilliance—

II.5.329
—under Maghā, progeny and flourishing; under Phālgunī, good fortune. Under other lunar mansions too one may act according to one's desire.
Note: The division of flourishing and good fortune between Maghā and Phālgunī is compressed; the Hindi partly distributes these differently. Saumya conventionally names Mṛgaśīrṣa, but the source's designation is retained.

II.5.330
Whoever's ancestors died by weapons in battle should perform [their rite] on the fourteenth lunar day, wishing for their sustenance.

II.5.331
When fifteen ancestral rites are performed with a desired purpose, together with the fourteenth-day [rite] sixteen ancestral rites are prescribed.
Textual caution: The exact counting rule is not fully clear in this printing; it is not expanded into a modern ritual calendar.

II.5.332
Beginning with the tenth [day] and the fifth [day] and the others, one should then exclude [the stated occasion]; on the fourteenth tithi perform [the rite for] the gods.
Textual caution: The syntax relating the starting days, exclusion, and fourteenth day is damaged in the extracted text and not resolved by the Hindi. This is a record of the recoverable terms and general sense, not a settled instruction.

II.5.333
The person who performs the ancestral rite on the new moon each month obtains every desire and reaches heaven.

II.5.334
No ritual fee is prescribed for the daily ancestral rite, libation, daily worship of deities, or feeding Brahmins.

II.5.335
Beloved, one unable to perform the ancestral rite in the ancestors' fortnight should feed Brahmins. Give food in water to the deities, and present the other [offerings] in this manner.
Note: The relationship of “in water” to the deities' food and the final clause is uncertain; the Hindi instead understands giving food and water.

II.5.336
Goddess, when the parents are living, on the day of a sacrifice and on an ancestral-rite day, feed [the recipients] various foods to be chewed and eaten, and fruits.
Note: The Hindi interprets the occasion as another relative's ancestral rite coinciding with the living parents' sacrificial day. That explanation is possible but not explicitly developed in this Sanskrit verse.

II.5.337
Without feeding [them], the sacrifice is ruined and the ancestral rite is ruined. At the prosperity rite, the pārvaṇa rite, and the daily ancestral rite, Great Lady, avoid feeding many Brahmins.
Note: The qualification may restrict numbers or a particular overlapping observance; the apparent negative is preserved despite the preceding praise of feeding.

II.5.338
If one desires the difficult-to-obtain station [or fruit] of the Rājasūya, horse sacrifice, and the others—

II.5.339
—one should go to Gayā and the Gaṅgā and duly perform the ancestral rite, even with water, greens, roots, grain, meal, and barley preparations.

II.5.340
The city of the ancestors remains empty until Viṣṇu awakens. When that awakening has passed, the ancestors together with the deities sigh and return, having pronounced a terrible curse.
Note: The Hindi relates Viṣṇu's awakening to Kārttika's bright eleventh day. That calendrical identification is explanatory rather than a separate Sanskrit clause here.

II.5.341
Queen of the gods, a person whose father is living should avoid the Gayā ancestral rite, bathing at Gayā, and sesame libation from a khaḍga vessel.
Note: Khaḍga can denote a rhinoceros; in this ritual context its horn may be the vessel material. The precise material is not elaborated in the verse.

II.5.342
Avoid silent bathing on Monday and on the new moon.

II.5.343
Goddess, beloved, one whose mother is dead [has occasion for] Mātṛgayā. If the father has died, or a paternal uncle dies, [one goes]. Even when mother and father are living, one should go to Gayā without deliberation.
Textual caution: The last Sanskrit clause appears positive, whereas the Hindi paraphrase reads a prohibition on going while both parents live. This draft records the apparent Sanskrit reading and the disagreement; it is not a settled reconciliation with341.

II.5.344
Give the father's rice-ball, [the offering for] the grandfather, and the great-grandfather's rice-ball: this is established in scripture.
Note: The verb recognized as bhojet may literally mean “feed” and the final sequence is unclear. The list follows the Hindi's explanation provisionally.

II.5.345
For the deceased, rice-balls should be given, and Brahmins should also be fed.

II.5.346
A proper, secluded place, facing south, auspicious in its characteristics and clean, should be smeared with cow dung.

II.5.347
[Make the offering] in the fire and on the ground; [the following negative concerning the ancestors' placement is uncertain]. Goddess, avoid a sleeping-room or [ordinary] house.
Textual caution: The extracted naiva nivapa conflicts with the Hindi's positive instruction to establish the ancestors within a fire-enclosed space. A definite construction cannot be recovered without further collation.

II.5.348
When a mendicant or celibate student arrives for food while [the recipients] are seated at the ancestral rite, honour [that person] according to one's means and wish.

II.5.349
The rite, place, time, materials, and suitability of the Brahmins: these five [when defective] injure the ancestors; the causes extend through the rite's constituent parts.
Note: The bracketed condition is supplied from the ritual context; the literal extracted text says that the five “injure the ancestors,” while the relationship of the concluding compound remains uncertain.

II.5.350
Or appoint a Brahmin thoroughly versed in the Veda. Goddess, the person who [thinks], “There are many undertakings [for me to do]”—
Note: The printed number is misrecognized as250; sequence establishes350. The last clause continues into351 and is elucidated by the Hindi gloss.

II.5.351
—should not perform the ancestral rite merely at will; it should be completed even through the prescribed comprehensive procedure. Avoid that ancestral rite at the beginning of the Vaiśvadeva [offering].
Textual caution: The connection to the distracted person in350 is explained by the Hindi as failure to complete the rite through preoccupation. Tantra here may mean a coordinated or comprehensive ritual procedure rather than the title of a scripture; the exact syntax remains provisional.

II.5.352
Building a temple, a journey, and household ceremonies [are not prescribed in] the dark fortnight; avoid the tantric bathing [rite then].
Note: The qualifying syntax is compressed and not fully secure. This follows the Hindi's general restriction while marking that uncertainty.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Second Part, Chapter5, with the vernacular commentary, in the Kāmarūpa section of the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand [verses].

EDITORIAL STATUS
All352 numbered units have English draft entries. Obvious OCR numeral errors are normalized only where the surrounding sequence establishes the number. Damaged Sanskrit, uncertain ritual syntax, and disagreements with the Hindi gloss are explicitly marked. This is a provisional reading translation and has not undergone independent scholarly review or complete critical collation.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 6
English working translation
Source: Mishra edition, 1984; printed pages 362–396, PDF pages 382–416. Sanskrit primary; Hindi gloss consulted. Verse numbering follows the edition, with obvious OCR numbering errors normalized. Uncertain readings are marked. Religious results are claims of the source.

II.6.1 — The Lord said:
Goddess Pārvatī, hear what should be done on the second auspicious day. Bathing at Cakratīrtha releases a person from every sin.

II.6.2
Go south of the Lauhitya to Kola Mountain in the northwest. In its western region is the powerful [Pāṇḍu]nātha.

II.6.3
Northwest of him is the lake called Brahmakuṇḍa, measuring twelve bow-lengths and destroying all sins.

II.6.4
What need of repetition, austerity, gifts, or sons? Bathing at Brahmakuṇḍa brings accomplishment immediately.

II.6.5
Formerly Brahmā dwelt there by Īśvara's permission. Gods and Dānavas hasten to that sacred ford for bathing—

II.6.6
—as do Ṛṣis, Siddhas, Gandharvas, sacred fords, and lakes. Beautiful one, Brahmakuṇḍa's greatness is excellent.

II.6.7
Bathe duly with the Tārā [or praṇava] mantra, then give jewels, gold, and precious stones according to one's means.

II.6.8
A deluded, lowly person who can bathe there but does not is tormented in dreadful hell for the reigns of fourteen Indras.

II.6.9
Five bow-lengths to its south is the sacred Lauhitya, where bathing destroys sin.

II.6.10
Bathing in the king of fords destroys every sin. Its holy lake bestows the fruit of every pilgrimage place.

II.6.11
All the earth's sacred fords, rivers, and lakes enter there.

II.6.12
As the ocean rules rivers, this is king of all fords, foremost among them and granting every desire.

II.6.13
Beloved, as darkness disappears when the sun rises, bathing in Tīrtharāja destroys all sins.

II.6.14
No ford equal to Tīrtharāja has existed or will exist. Lord Nārāyaṇa always dwells there. Who can describe its qualities, beloved?

II.6.15
Ninety-three myriads of fords are there. Thus bathing, giving, fire-offering, repetition, worship, and every meritorious act performed there become imperishable.
Note: The Sanskrit numeral appears to be trinavatyayutāni, 93 ten-thousands; the Hindi says 93 thousand. The numerical reading requires collation.

II.6.16
“Homage to Brahmaputra; homage to Śantanu's son. Of blood-red nature, remove the sin accumulated through my three births.”

II.6.17
Bathe with this mantra and present the arghya offering. Radiant one, worship with supreme devotion using this mantra:

II.6.18
“[Construct it with] the sixth of Tīrtharāja's letters, ha joined with the left eye, Śamana's heart, the beloved of fire, the steadfast form, and lake [sara].”
Note: Encoded syllable construction is corrupt or obscure here; the complete mantra cannot be securely reconstructed.

II.6.19
Not far to its south is the renowned Viṣṇukuṇḍa, [within a bow-length: measurement uncertain].

II.6.20
Bathing at Viṣṇukuṇḍa and seeing Pāṇḍuśaunaka, in the form of a large beautiful stone [further epithet unclear], gives the fruit of five horse-sacrifices.

II.6.21
“Viṣṇukuṇḍa, life of all beings, source of birth and lord of rivers, homage to you. Protect me from every stain.”

II.6.22
Beautiful one, bathing with this [mantra] on the eleventh lunar day in Phālguna releases one from every sin and suffering—

II.6.23
—glorious like a god, endowed with beauty and youth, radiant like the pure sun and served by divine Gandharvas, one rescues twenty-one lineages and goes to Viṣṇu's world.

II.6.24
A little southwest, toward the southern quarter, is the renowned Śivakuṇḍa, measuring eleven bow-lengths.

II.6.25
A mere ablution there takes one to Rudra's world. Beloved, bathing at Śivakuṇḍa at dawn on each month's fourteenth day prevents rebirth as a departed spirit on earth.

II.6.26
“Supreme ford among fords, born from Viṣṇu's bathing, lord of rivers, beloved of Śiva, homage to you; protect me.”

II.6.27
Bathe with this mantra and offer arghya with haṃsa. Then go to Pāṇḍuśaila, bathe [the deity] with fragrant water, and worship with white lotuses and white oleander.

II.6.28
Pronounce “Viṣṇave,” then “Pāṇḍunāthāya,” followed by repetition and salutation; [this rescues] the father and other ancestors.

II.6.29
This fourteen-syllable mantra ends in śikhā [svāhā]. Nārada is its seer, Gāyatrī its metre, Hari its deity; its application is every purpose, especially desired results.

II.6.30
The discerning practitioner contemplates white, two-armed Viṣṇu, conch and discus gleaming in his hands, lord of all worlds, served by gods and Gandharvas, and worships with the offerings in order.

II.6.31
Worship Lakṣmī, Sarasvatī, Gaṅgā, Yamunā, Narmadā, Śivā, Bālā, Kamalā, Saṃkarṣaṇa and the others, the guardians of directions, planets, and Viṣvaksena.

II.6.32
Bathing duly in the Lohita and worshipping Pāṇḍunātha releases every sin; one is honoured in Viṣṇu's world—

II.6.33
—for a full manvantara, free from old age and death. When merit is exhausted, one returns and enters a family endowed with every virtue—

II.6.34
—and becomes a Vaiṣṇava [the phrase concerning death is unclear]. Repeat the mantra and worship the deity with one's chosen mantra.

II.6.35
“Pāṇḍunātha, homage to you; homage, maker of liberation. Lord of all worlds, protect me; form of Viṣṇu, homage to you.

II.6.36
“Resembling pure bliss, supreme Person, homage to you. Lotus-eyed Pāṇḍunātha, homage to you.

II.6.37
“Golden [or Hema-garbha: uncertain epithet], Garuḍa-bannered one, homage. Form of Brahman, Nārāyaṇa, homage to you.

II.6.38
“Lotus-like, affectionate toward devotees, homage. Pāṇḍunātha, homage; protect, protect; homage to you.

II.6.39
“Abode of gods, beloved of gods, homage. Nārāyaṇa, homage; protect me who have taken refuge.

II.6.40
“Foremost of gods, lotus-born, homage. Four-faced abode of the universe, Pāṇḍu-formed one, homage.

II.6.41
“Dark-cloud-like, worshipped by the thirty gods, homage. Viṣṇu, lord of the universe, protect me; Pāṇḍu-formed one, homage.

II.6.42
“Narasiṃha of great power and blazing eyes, protect me. Form of Viṣṇu, homage; Pāṇḍunātha, homage to you.”

II.6.43
Five bow-lengths southwest of the deity is a field marked by an aśvattha tree, proclaimed Dharmakṣetra.

II.6.44
Recite the Saṃhitā and the teaching of song [Gītāśāstra] there. By eight repetitions, [one gains] the fruit of the mantra [syntax uncertain]—

II.6.45
—without doubt; a single recitation [has] a thousandfold [fruit]. Goddess, ascending that field gives Kurukṣetra's fruit.

II.6.46
One bow-length east of the deity is a clear, beautiful stone, proclaimed Lakṣmī.

II.6.47
Worship her with the Śrī seed and mālatī flowers.

II.6.48
Bathe at Viṣṇukuṇḍa and worship Lakṣmī according to rule. On the full moon with the sun in Libra, [such worship makes] one's fortune steadfast.

II.6.49
Śāṅkarī, a little south is Kolakṣetra, measuring eight bow-lengths.

II.6.50
Seeing and devotedly worshipping the divine lord as a beautiful dark stone at the aśvattha's root takes one to Viṣṇu's world.

II.6.51
North of Brahmakūṭa is Śrīkuṇḍa Lake, measuring two bow-lengths; bathing there brings fortune.

II.6.52
Bathe with the mantra at Śrītīrtha on Caitra's bright tenth day and the dark eleventh day; one attains the unsurpassed destination.

II.6.53
“May fortune be [mine], foremost blessed one, giver of health and victory. Give fortune, give fame, remove sin; homage to you.”

II.6.54
Twenty-two bow-lengths east is the ford Kanakhala, destroyer of great sins.

II.6.55
Especially on Vaiśākha's bright third day, bathing with the Dakṣiṇāmūrti mantra brings honour in heaven.

II.6.56
“Foremost river, greatly fortunate one, served by gods and Gandharvas, sacred ford, remove the sin of ten births; homage to you.”

II.6.57
On the beautiful mountain to its south, four bow-lengths away, worship Campakeśa.

II.6.58
Purified and intent, performing ablution at Kanakhala releases every sin and leads to Brahmā's world.

II.6.59
Goddess, seven bow-lengths east is the three-world-renowned ford of Brahmā Parameṣṭhin—

II.6.60
—Puṣkara, destroyer of all sins, giver of Brahmā's world to the dead. Merely remembering it releases all sins and brings delight with Indra.

II.6.61
Gods, Gandharvas, Yakṣas, serpents, Rākṣasas, and companies of Siddhas worship lotus-born Brahmā there.

II.6.62
Bathing there brings liberation. Worshipping Brahmā Parameṣṭhin, one beholds the boon-giving Brahmā.

II.6.63
Approaching the faultless lord of gods, Puruhūta, a mortal becomes beautiful and obtains every desire. “Puṣkara, greatly fortunate one, homage; triple Puṣkara—

II.6.64
“—hūṃ hūṃ hauṃ, lord of rivers, remove my sin, Puṣkara.” Bathe with this and then present arghya.

II.6.65
Beloved, slightly leftward to Puṣkara's southwest is Badarikāśrama, measuring twenty [Hindi: twenty-eight] bow-lengths.

II.6.66
Go there and worship flawless Nārāyaṇa. Bathing and worshipping on Hari's day gives the fruit of a thousand cow-gifts.

II.6.67
Performing the Rohiṇī vow at Nārāyaṇa's hermitage gives, through that one vow, the fruit of a hundred ten-million vows.

II.6.68
Maheśa's liṅga there is renowned as Vibhāṇḍaka. Worshipping with the Prāsāda mantra brings Rudra's state.

II.6.69
The supreme Pañcagodāvara ford is served by Brahmā and others. Worship the gracious Supreme Lord Rudra there—

II.6.70
—and propitiate Hara with the five-syllabled [mantra]. Worshipping and saluting gives the fruit of a hundred cow-gifts.

II.6.71
East of Puṣkara is the lake Kumāra. Bathing at Kumāratīrtha brings lordship among the Gaṇas.

II.6.72
Goddess, fifty bow-lengths southeast of Kumāratīrtha is Naranāraṇyaka, surrounded by all the gods.

II.6.73
“Kumāreśa, [purifier?], beloved of Viṣṇu [wording unclear], oṃ oṃ īṃ, renowned throughout the world, remove sin, Kumāra.” Bathe with this and offer arghya to the lord of gods.

II.6.74
Mahādeva is called Sthāṇu there. Merely seeing him immediately releases a person from every sin.

II.6.75
Sixty-two bow-lengths north of Campakeśa is Campaka Forest, honoured by Siddhas and Brahmarṣis—

II.6.76
—a holy abode of Viṣṇu, where Puruṣottama resides. North of Brahmakūṭa, among five stones—

II.6.77
—is the great Durgākūpa well, with openings on every side. Bathing with the ten-syllabled mantra fulfils desires.

II.6.78
On the eighth day, bathe at Durgākūpa, repeat the Kāma mantra, and worship three or five times with kṛṣṇavijaya flowers; one becomes [pīlaśrutidhara: unclear].

II.6.79
A woman who has borne only one child, or whose children have died, obtains offspring, particularly in autumn.

II.6.80
Worshipping Kāmeśvarī there with bandhūka flowers and bilva leaves, Queen of the gods, brings lasting accomplishment.

II.6.81
One fulfils desired wishes and finds accomplishment there.

II.6.82
Supreme Lady, the northern mountains are Kola, Viṣṇuśaila, Īśa, Pārijāta, Kumāra, Gaṇeśvara, Nīla, and Śvetasunnīta [last name uncertain].

II.6.83
In the middle are Viṣṇu, Sthāṇu, Bala, Kamala, Śikhā, Kapota, and Marutācala.

II.6.84
East are Pātukūpa and the other mountains; southeast, Hastikarṇa and Vikarṇaka; south, Amācala, Maruvaka, and Prajeśvara.

II.6.85
[Also south:] Sumanta and Kanaka. Northwest are Nīlalohita, Kāmāhva, Mānaśaila, Vahni, Indra, and Śatakratu—

II.6.86
—and Lohita and Kamala. Southwest are Nirṛti, Gandha [Hindi: Gandharva], Lākṣaṇa, Piśāca, and Vihagācala.

II.6.87
West are Brahmayūpa, Hayamedha, and Girīśvara; north, Uttara and Uttarapāṇḍaka—

II.6.88
—and Āditya. Northwest are Vāyu, Bhallātaka, Dhanada, Mahīdhra, Janaka, and Nala.

II.6.89
Northeast are Maṇḍala, Aśvakrānta, Candraka, Yama, Citravaha, and Graha in order.
Note on 82–89: Several names and directional transitions are uncertain in the OCR. Names are retained as recovered, not equated with modern sites.

II.6.90
Then, Supreme Lady, go to Nīla Mountain at midday, on the eighth, thirteenth, or fourteenth lunar day—

II.6.91
—at an equinox, solstice, or the sun's passage between signs. Entering by the east gate brings abundant wealth.

II.6.92
The liberation-seeker [goes] north; the kingdom-seeker, west. Ascending Nīlakūṭa by the southern path, a king loses his kingdom, and others suffer decline.
Note: The Hindi appears to reverse the negative result of the southern route; the apparent Sanskrit is retained.

II.6.93
Going northeast brings abundant fortune. Northwest, southeast, and southwest bring great danger.

II.6.94
Contemplate Nīla, ten-armed, peaceful, adorned with jewelled earrings, wearing a serpent necklace and upper garment, mounted on a bull.

II.6.95
Worship with the fire seed and bow according to rule. Ascending the mountain with the mantra gives a horse-sacrifice's fruit. A householder should ascend by the east gate.

II.6.96
“Nīlācala of mighty arms, giver of dharma, wealth, desire, and liberation: I ascend your summit. Be gracious; quickly remove my sin.”

II.6.97
Three bow-lengths east of Durgākūpa, Goddess, worship the god Āmrātakeśvara with Keśava and others.

II.6.98
Beloved, eight bow-lengths south of that god, worship dark-coloured, elephant-faced Gaṇanāyaka.

II.6.99
One bow-length east is Trivikrama. A person bowing devotedly obtains every desire.

II.6.100
[At a fifth division of its distance: uncertain] is Saubhāgya Lake, forty bow-lengths in extent—

II.6.101
—Kāmākhyā's pleasure pond, Queen of the gods. Formerly Indra and the gods worshipped Prajāpati there.

II.6.102
Bathe on its western bank; duly form the circle [Hindi: circumambulate] and observe a fast.

II.6.103
At dawn, bathe duly in the five [waters: uncertain], go to the pleasure pond, and worship Kāmeśī. One swiftly rescues ancestors and rejoices in the Goddess's world.

II.6.104
“Whirlpool of the Saubhāgya stream, pure one, dear to the mind, namaḥ oṃ vaṣaṭ svāhā; remove sin, homage to you.” Bathe and offer arghya with Kāma.

II.6.105
Northeast of that well is Lauhitya Lake. Bathing with Dhruva releases worldly bondage, Queen of the gods.

II.6.106
[There are] Agnikuṇḍa, Kālahasta, and Yāmala lakes. Bathing with Pārśva [mantra] brings beauty in a birth on earth.

II.6.107
Supreme Lady, five hand-lengths southwest of Saubhāgya is Gaṅgāsara, whose water originates in all the sacred fords.

II.6.108
The pool within Kola is called Saubhāgya. The three and a half ten-million [fords] in heaven, earth, and midspace—

II.6.109
—are all in Saubhāgya when the sun becomes mild. Therefore bathe when the sun is in Capricorn.

II.6.110
Bathing at Libra's equinox and the solar transitions gives a wife to the wifeless; one rejoices in the Goddess's world.

II.6.111
Beloved, the partly manifest, partly unmanifest stone shaped like a monitor lizard is called Ananta; the pool is above it.

II.6.112
To Ananta's western side [the following directional word is unclear] is a dark stone: know it as Varāha, with water arising from all fords.

II.6.113
Bathing in Libra or Virgo, especially on the bright eighth day, then beholding the Goddess gives Agniṣṭoma's fruit.

II.6.114
Satisfy ancestors, gods, and other desired recipients. Queen of the gods, do not perform desire-directed libation at every ford.

II.6.115
[It is prescribed] at this pool, Aśvakrānta, Agastya, Prayāga, Vārāṇasī Lake, Bhārgava, Meru, Puṣkara, Gaṅgā Lake, Vartala [uncertain], and Durgākūpa.

II.6.116
Circumambulating this pool gives the fruit that the great sages proclaim for circumambulating the earth.

II.6.117
Southeast of the pool, at a tulā's distance, seeing Śiva called Kambala releases worldly bondage.

II.6.118
Worship with feeling, with the vowel [mantra] ending in salutation. “Homage, homage, lord of gods, well worshipped by knowers of mantra—”

II.6.119
“—husband of Lakṣmī, endless supreme Person, homage. Gods, Dānavas, and Gandharvas always worship your lotus-feet. Homage to the lotus-navelled one; homage to Kamalā's husband.”

II.6.120
Beloved, bowing to that dark form of Viṣṇu, praising and circumambulating, go to the Goddess's house.

II.6.121
Śāṅkarī, establish the corpse-seat, repeat [the mantra], look with Tārā, touch with Madana [mantra], and bow with Kāma.

II.6.122
Bathe [her] with the five nectars, water, and pure water; offer sipping with the root mantra and wipe with the [appropriate] mantra.

II.6.123
Auspicious one, write the Kāma [formula/diagram] on the right with kuśīta [Hindi: red sandalwood]; write Kāma on the left and worship there.

II.6.124
Omit the maṇḍala on the Goddess's body, paintings, puṣṭimaṇi, sword, cremation ground, great liṅga, images, water, Śālagrāma, yantra, and tantra.

II.6.125
Making a maṇḍala at the great homa [Hindi: great delusion] incurs grave sin. [The deity] does not accept the worship and departs from the site.

II.6.126
Nor [make one] within another yoni. Worship in the Goddess's great maṇḍala situated east of the cremation ground.

II.6.127
[There is] a red-stone area of eighty-seven bow-lengths, with an eight-hand liṅga [and obscure additional measurements]—

II.6.128
—and a four-hand field westward, the yonimaṇḍala: one arm-length [long], twelve finger-breadths across, established in water reaching the underworld.
Note: The measurement compounds are corrupt in extraction; no exact physical plan is asserted.

II.6.129
The Urvaśī, Yamunā stream, Kāverī, and Sarasvatī arise at Brahmakuṇḍa and [pass] pure Maṇikūṭa—

II.6.130
—without doubt; reaching Vārāṇasī Lake and washing the Goddess's maṇḍala, they appear in Brahmasara, beloved.

II.6.131
[The flow] becomes dry after sixty years and three months; [for] two months and three days it remains without hindrance. Remaining [dry] six months, Goddess, is considered disastrous.
Note: The verse's subject and temporal relations are uncertain. The Hindi reads dryness of menstrual potency, but the next verses concern the sanctuary's water flow.

II.6.132
When the channel dries, discard excrement and urine outside. When the stream becomes dry year after year, Śaṅkarī—

II.6.133
—famine and disease certainly arise in the outer country. Dryness in the inner cavity destroys the kingdom. Hear the result of total dryness:

II.6.134
The king loses his kingdom and another state invades. Beautiful-faced one, many such faults occur.

II.6.135
Perform pacification to quell these faults: two hundred thousand fire-offerings with ghee-soaked oleander, milk-rice, red lotuses, or śrīphala fruit.

II.6.136
Or [offer] two hundred thousand with the three sweets or monitor-lizard flesh. Sugar, honey, and ghee combined are called the three sweets.

II.6.137
Offer reduced milk combined with ghee.

II.6.138
West of Ananta is the Asī River; its western stream is Varuṇā [Sanskrit extraction: Aruṇā]. Drinking its clear water prevents rebirth on earth.

II.6.139
Siddheśvara, Koṭiliṅga, Heruka, Muktimaṇḍala, and the Vārāṇasī field are remembered as the Goddess's inner house.

II.6.140
In a book, an image, a prepared altar, sandals, a painting, a sword, fire, or water—

II.6.141
—in Lauhitya, Gaṅgā, the ocean, sacred confluences, each pīṭha, or a bilva's root, worship the Goddess dwelling in the liṅga.

II.6.142
What is called Kālaśilā is the Maṇipūraka pīṭha; in the inner house's great pīṭha, it is Maṇipīṭha.

II.6.143
A devoted person should worship the Goddess daily on a stone, mountain peak, or mountain cave. Vārāṇasī gives full fruit, Puruṣottama twice that.

II.6.144
At one's field and ford, the fruit equals Kālagiri's; at Kaumāra, eightfold; at Cauhāra, the same.

II.6.145
In Āryāvarta, Madhyadeśa, Brahmāvarta, and Śrīhaṭṭa, Goddess, worship gives fruit equal to Maṇipūra.

II.6.146
Agastya and Aśvamedhika give fourfold fruit; Jalpeśvara certainly gives fourfold that.

II.6.147
Where the yoni shines, the fruit is tenfold; at Ekāmra, Supreme Lady, fourfold that.

II.6.148
Maṇikūṭa gives a hundredfold, Maṇiśaila a thousandfold; in water or on land at Aśvatīrtha, tenfold fruit is proclaimed.

II.6.149
Worship on land or water in Kāmarūpa gives equal fruit. As Viṣṇu is foremost, Great Lady, Goddess-worship in Kāmarūpa is supreme.

II.6.150
Kāmarūpa is the Goddess's field, unequalled anywhere. Elsewhere the Goddess is rare; in Kāmarūpa she is in every house.

II.6.151
A person worshipping Mahāmāyā at Kāmākhyā obtains all desires here and becomes Śiva in the next world.

II.6.152
No comparable undertaking exists elsewhere on earth. Gaining the desired object, one certainly becomes long-lived.

II.6.153
Do not approach or touch Mahāmāyā during bathing, at midnight, or at the conclusion of the great worship.

II.6.154
Do not see the Goddess during kumāraṇa [uncertain], Mahāṣṭamī, at night or day's end, at the beginning of an age, or in Kārttika.

II.6.155
A Śūdra seeing the Goddess's lamp-waving obtains beauty and a good destination. A widowed Brahmin woman may always see Mahāmāyā.

II.6.156
Women, especially young women, should not see the Goddess during bathing, at midday, or while used offerings are removed.

II.6.157
Do not enter Pārvatī's house on Pauṣa's eighth, ninth, or thirteenth day, or the first three days of Cancer. Touching then brings a curse and loss of lifespan.

II.6.158
Worship is approved for the initiated, not the uninitiated. Therefore the initiation-seeker should wear red garments—

II.6.159
—adorn himself with red sandalwood and a nāgaja [nāgakeśara] forehead mark, sit upon a soft skin-seat, and receive initiation devotedly.

II.6.160
Because it gives supreme knowledge and destroys bondage through sin, contemplators of reality call it dīkṣā.

II.6.161
It entirely destroys sin acquired by mind, act, and speech, and gives supreme knowledge; thus scriptural experts call it dīkṣā in this world.

II.6.162
First it gives realization's fruit; second it causes dissolution; third it grants liberation. Therefore it is praised as dīkṣā.

II.6.163
Initiation is twofold, supported and unsupported. That which confers eligibility for daily, occasional, and desire-directed rites—

II.6.164
—is called supported. Unsupported grants liberation and is known as stainless, say contemplators of reality.

II.6.165
Constructing a fire-pit and maṇḍala, give it to worthy recipients; then initiation bears fruit, otherwise it is fruitless.

II.6.166
Great Lady, initiation given to the unworthy certainly becomes powerless through mere mental activity [precise sense uncertain].

II.6.167
Never [receive initiation] from a teacher who has no son, whose son has died, who is a kuṇḍa, dwarf, has defective nails, dark teeth, extra limbs, or is dominated by a woman—

II.6.168
—but a discerning, handsome, well-born, virtuous teacher, possessing knowledge and conduct and knowing the stipulated observances, should give the mantra.
Note: These exclusionary criteria are the historical source's prescriptions, not current judgments about disability, family status, or gender.

II.6.169
Goddess, do not take [such] initiation: truly I tell you. Do not receive a mantra from one's father or maternal grandfather. A dream-received or woman-given mantra is purified by consecration.

II.6.170
For accomplishment of a dream-received mantra, install the guru's vital power [in a vessel, supplied by the Hindi], write it in kuṅkuma on a banyan leaf, and receive it. Then it is purified; otherwise fruitless.

II.6.171
At a solstice, equinox, lunar or solar eclipse, the sun's transition, or the beginning of an age, Queen of the gods—

II.6.172
—on manvantara commemorations, fourteenth or eighth lunar days, the great worship day, or days of the disciple's purification, receive [initiation] attentively, with devotion and faith.

II.6.173
The person intent on devotion in Goddess-worship, engaged in the meaning of all scriptures, [is eligible for] initiation, not another.

II.6.174
Initiation in Caitra brings sorrow; Vaiśākha, all accomplishments; Jyeṣṭha, death; Āṣāḍha, many offspring.

II.6.175
Śrāvaṇa brings great loss; Bhādra, sorrow; Āśvina, all accomplishments; Kārttika, increased knowledge.

II.6.176
Mārgaśīrṣa is auspicious; Pauṣa destroys knowledge; Māgha increases intelligence; Phālguna produces every crop.

II.6.177
At an eclipse or great pilgrimage place, timing is not restricted. At Gayā, Bhāskarākṣetra, Viraja, Candraparvata—

II.6.178
—Koṅkaṇa, Mataṅga, and Kanyāśrama, do not receive initiation, Pārvatī. Initiated disciples should follow the guru's excellent command.

II.6.179
[The disciple] should hold the deity in the heart, devote himself to guru-worship, practise preliminary observance, be pure in self and master the senses—

II.6.180
—and always, by the guru's permission, study mantras, yantras, Purāṇas, and the Bhārata, and go to Gayā and other places according to rule.

II.6.181
Great Lady, do not commit violence against women or engage in [sexual] association [context of the restriction uncertain].

II.6.182
Barley, cakravāka birds, cranes, pigeons, and Nīla and the other mountains should always be dear to that person.

II.6.183
Thus the initiated should perform daily duties; at night, after carefully contemplating and worshipping, eat happily.

II.6.184
On the preceding day, eat sacrificial or meatless food; on the next day observe a diet of sacrificial food.

II.6.185
Cook caru and offer half to the deity; give half [of the remainder] to the guru, and [eat] the rest oneself—

II.6.186
—together with the guru. This is the rule for every initiation. If the guru fasts after imparting the mantra—

II.6.187
—he becomes a worm in the hell of delusion's darkness. If the practitioner fasts after receiving initiation, the deity, angry, curses him and returns to its abode.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Second Part, Chapter 6, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand verses.

STATUS
All 187 verse units have entries. Corrupt readings, encoded syllables, disputed numbers, and differences from the Hindi are explicitly marked. This working translation has not been independently reviewed.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 7
English working translation
Source: Mishra edition, 1984, PDF pages 416–459. Sanskrit primary; Hindi consulted. Uncertain readings are flagged. SOURCE SUPPLEMENT: the supplied PDF jumps from printed page399 to402 and omits verses19–28. Those ten entries are translated from the same printed pages recovered in an alternate Mishra scan: https://archive.org/details/YoginiTantraKanhaiyyaLalMishra (PDF pages419–420). These supplements are individually labelled; they are not presented as text visible in the supplied scan.

II.7.1 — The Lord said:
In a beautiful, secluded, hidden place, in an auspicious pavilion strewn with flowers and scented with fragrance and blossoms—
II.7.2
—with no third person and outside paśus' sight, on a board or the like, the mantra practitioner should properly extract the mantra.
II.7.3
A yantra without extraction gives very little fruit. Extracting the mantra in the yantra gives complete fruit.
II.7.4
Do not write letters on the ground; write them in a book. Do not put a book on the ground, lest a Ḍākinī carry it away.
II.7.5
Putting letters or a book on the ground during an earthquake or eclipse makes one foolish birth after birth, Queen of the gods; avoid it.
II.7.6
Do not write with bamboo, for it certainly brings loss. Writing with a copper stylus brings imperishable wealth.
II.7.7
A gold stylus grants great fortune; a bṛhannala [reed] needle increases intelligence.
II.7.8
A fire-made [metal] stylus brings sons, grandsons, and wealth; brass, great fortune; bronze, death.
II.7.9
Writing a book with a stylus eight, ten, or four finger-breadths long diminishes lifespan by the number of its letters [precise construction uncertain].
II.7.10
Goddess, hear briefly the book's measurements. Correct measure brings fruit; incorrect measure destroys fortune.
II.7.11
A hand, fist, or forearm measure; twelve, ten, or eight finger-breadths: do not make it smaller.
II.7.12
Two holes [are prescribed] in fist-, hand-, or arm-sized [books]; place the binding equally, Great Lady—
II.7.13
—leaving eight finger-breadths between and piercing the middle; or [at] two finger-breadths [the placement is unclear].
Note: The manuscript dimensions and hole-spacing cannot be securely reconstructed from the OCR and Hindi.
II.7.14
Failure to provide holes at the beginning and end of the book causes swift loss of wife or wealth.
II.7.15
Beloved Queen of the gods, make a book on birch bark, tejapatra, or palm leaf, not excessively heavy.
II.7.16
Where possible, on gold or copper sheets, Śāṅkarī; [the sentence continues with other materials]—
II.7.17
—other tree bark, ketakī leaves, clay, copper or silver vessels, banyan leaves, or other many-layered leaves, beautiful one: whoever writes and practises [on these]—
II.7.18
—attains a bad destination and certainly loses wealth. Writing the deity and reading [it improperly?] makes one a Brahmin-slayer.
Note: The approved/prohibited material lists and final clause conflict or are corrupt in this printing; the uncertainty is preserved.
II.7.19 — Supplement from alternate Mishra scan
Keeping that book in the house brings a thunderbolt strike, certainly. A hole made by burning causes affliction; a circular hole brings auspiciousness. A four-cornered hole brings upheaval; a triangular one, death.
Note: These are the text's prescriptions about manuscript construction, not modern binding advice. This verse and19–28 below are absent from the user-supplied scan and restored from printed pages400–401 of the alternate Mishra scan.
II.7.20 — Supplement
In the true/eternal letter stands Śambhu, trident-bearing and three-eyed. In Dvāpara [stands] Prajāpati, in Tretā the sun, in the Kṛta age Pinākin, and in Kali Hari in the written letter.
Note: The opening satye 'kṣare could also be read “in Satya [age], in the letter.” That creates an apparent repetition with the Kṛta age later in the verse; the ambiguity is retained rather than silently removed.
II.7.21 — Supplement
At the beginning and completion, worship what has been written, and Hari, with fragrance, flowers, and beautiful garments.
II.7.22 — Supplement
Śaṅkarī, for as many letters as there are on each leaf, [the writer] dwells for so many thousands of ages in heaven, for a long time.
II.7.23 — Supplement
Whoever accepts wages after writing a book dwells in hell for as many [periods] as the number of letters.
Note: The length of each period is not explicit in this verse. The alternate Shastri edition has a differently phrased negative injunction; this entry follows the recovered Mishra text.
II.7.24 — Supplement
Know as the great seed the consonant mounted upon earth and joined with the left eye and lunar dot; through its repetition one attains liberation.
Note: This is an encoded seed-syllable construction. The exact consonant and its expansion are not established by the OCR, so a guessed mantra is not supplied.
II.7.25 — Supplement
After praṇava I shall state praṇava; at the end of vaṣaṭ, [place] ṭha twice. Pronounce [it] oneself at the end of the vowel, and the salutation having the nature of the heart.
Textual note: The wording and segmentation of this encoded mantra rule remain provisional, though both scans preserve the general sequence. It should not be treated as a verified continuous mantra.
II.7.26 — Supplement
For the householder, praṇava comes at the beginning of every mantra. Praṇava at both beginning and end increases knowledge of the Self.
II.7.27 — Supplement
Beloved, the division into mantra and Vidyā is twofold: mantras are declared to have male deities, and Vidyās are remembered as having female deities.
II.7.28 — Supplement
Male mantras end in hūṃ phaṭ; female [mantras] are understood to end in ṭha twice. Neuter ones end in namaḥ. Thus mantras are regarded as threefold.
Note: The paired ṭha is a traditional encoded expression, often associated with svāhā; the text's own designation is retained. This is a classification of ritual formulas, not a statement about human gender.
II.7.29
The Vidyā devoid of these [endings] is designated by the word Mahā. Parameṣṭhin is the seer, Gāyatrī the metre, Tripurā the deity; employ it for every purpose.
II.7.30
Beloved, establish the Goddess according to rule; first wash her face with the hand, then perform bathing.
II.7.31
Every two days, and after eight days, Goddess, anoint with oil and dry thoroughly with astringent [liquid].
II.7.32
At the fortnight's and month's ends perform the great bath, using the root seed or the substance's mantra—
II.7.33
—or a Vedic mantra or Māyā. Bathe with jars, then perform the arghya bath.
II.7.34
Bathing again after the arghya bath causes a fall from the Goddess's world and loss of wealth.
II.7.35
First bathe with water, then milk, curds, two lumps of ghee, sugar, jaggery, honey, sesame, milk, curds with sesame, and honey-milk [sequence imperfect].
II.7.36
Then warm water, fruit [water], kuśa water, fragrant water, jewel water, flower water, bilva water, seven leaves, and red-flower water—
II.7.37
—gold-and-conch water, then a copper vessel, jar water and kuśa: perform the arghya bath.
II.7.38
Bathing the Goddess with the five cow products, milk and kuśa water, with various mantras, is called the Brahman bath.
II.7.39
Bathing with the five products of a tawny cow, together with milk, or with sugarcane juice gives a hundredfold fruit.
II.7.40
Devoutly and faithfully bathing Kāmākhyā with milk brings honour in the gods' world.
II.7.41
Beloved, duly anointing the deity's body with ghee rescues oneself and ten preceding and ten succeeding generations from worldly existence; one is honoured in Durgā's world.
II.7.42
Duly bathing with curds, dūrvā, and unbroken rice brings honour in Śiva's world in a silver vehicle.
II.7.43
Bathing Kāmākhyā with fresh sugarcane juice gives delight with Viṣṇu in a Garuḍa vehicle.
II.7.44
Bathing Durgā with fragrant sandalwood water makes one pure as moonbeams and honoured in the moon's world.
II.7.45
Bathing with fragrant flower water brings sport with serpents in the Nāgas' world.
II.7.46
Bathing Kāmeśī with purified gold water brings delight with the Vasus in a golden vehicle.
II.7.47
Duly bathing with jewel water brings delight with Hari in a divine vehicle.
II.7.48
Droṇa leaves, bilva leaves, oleander, and lotuses should accompany the bath; they delight the Goddess.
II.7.49
Faithfully and devotedly giving any one of these baths brings honour in Viṣṇu's world.
II.7.50
Bathing the Goddess with camphor water leads to the supreme abode where Kāmeśvarī resides.
II.7.51
Hear the merit of one who duly and devotedly bathes the Goddess with milk or honey for the ancestors.
II.7.52
The ancestors are satisfied for two hundred years. For the great bath use a hundred of each of the five nectars, a hundred fruits, and a hundred water jars.
II.7.53
A hundred jars of water and three palas of oil [with māñjiṣṭha: reading uncertain] constitute the great bath, say the wise.
II.7.54
Half that is the middle bath; half again the lesser bath. Do not use less.
II.7.55
Whoever performs such a bath is immediately freed from sins committed in seven births.
II.7.56
Bathing Kāmākhyā grants life, strength, fame, brilliance, good fortune, and nourishment, without doubt.
II.7.57
A devoted person performing the great bath obtains bodily health, long life, and excellent fortune.
II.7.58
The lesser bath uses ten tolas of each substance separately, or four tolas [the alternative's relation is unclear].
II.7.59
A vessel with an opening eight finger-breadths, belly twenty-one, and height one forearm is called a maṇikuṇḍa.
II.7.60
A ray passing through a window is [the measure] lekhikā; eight lekhikās make a dust particle, eight dust particles a mustard seed.
II.7.61
Four mustard seeds are called a raktikā; twenty raktikās, a pādaka.
II.7.62
Four pādakas make one tolikā; four tolikās, a prasṛti; two prasṛtis, a karṣaka; two karṣas, a pala.
II.7.63
Half a pala is a mukti [śukti?]; two make a guḍaka [uncertain]. Having performed the bath, the discerning person wipes the body.
II.7.64
Apply a fragrant sandalwood forehead mark; offer a waist-cord, garment, and sacred thread.
II.7.65
Contemplate the Goddess, radiant like peacock feathers, with smooth lovely hair, long lips, red eyes, and fine garments.
II.7.66
The reddish face, clearly visible, with collyrium-dark radiance, is called [Tripurā's], supreme abode of the three worlds—
II.7.67
—joined with Narakeśa; contemplate that excellent [face] as before in clay, Great Lady, when seeking fortune.
II.7.68
The dreadful dark southern face, with divine fangs, is renowned as Kāmākhyā—
II.7.69
—giver of all accomplishments and fulfiller of every purpose. To the deity's south contemplate the yellow face [directional transition uncertain].
II.7.70
The dark, auspicious face in Kubera's quarter, wondrous and dear to Śatavītāṅga [uncertain epithet], Bhuvaneśvarī—
II.7.71
—is the unmanifest, lovely, divine, excellent face of Kubjikā, united with Narakeśa; seekers of victory should contemplate it.
II.7.72
The face of Vāgīśvarī is like pure crystal, lovely with betel [wording partly corrupt], embodying all knowledge.
II.7.73
The Goddess's Īśāna face, whose honey the bull-marked bee has drunk, should be contemplated by seekers of omniscience.
II.7.74
Beloved, the upward face shines with countless sunbeams; know it as Kāmeśvara's supreme great pīṭha.
II.7.75
Auspicious one, contemplate that supreme radiance, kissed by Narakeśa, for destruction of the ocean of worldly existence.
II.7.76
Mother of the hosts, Tripurā is its deity, Kāmākhyā [syntax uncertain]. These Goddesses, endowed with powers, dwell within the maṇḍala.
II.7.77
Kāmākhyā wears a lion skin above, has a great belly, and a tiger-skin garment; she consumes all [harodarī: uncertain]—
II.7.78
—born of supreme bliss, laughing loudly in great festivity, delighted by the sight of Sunandā, with eighteen manifest eyes—
II.7.79
—adorned with two earrings filled with beautiful rubies, fierce and terrible, wearing a garland of skulls [the compound is uncertain]—
II.7.80
—her crest crowned by the white moon, shining with lotus radiance, bearing a necklace studded with varied jewels—
II.7.81
—with twelve arms soft and smooth as lotus stalks, [adorned] with divine bones and jewels and lotus garlands—
II.7.82
—with goad, thunderbolt, cord, discus, [ray/sun], and blade in the right arms [list partly corrupt]—
II.7.83
—with bow, severed head, skull-staff, lotus stalk and lotus, skull bowl, and book; fierce, wearing a severed-head garland—
II.7.84
—her lotus-feet [adorned: compound unclear], mounted upon a lion throne, resting upon a corpse-seat—
II.7.85
—embraced by supreme Śiva, Narakeśa, radiant with jewels: [thus contemplate] Supreme Lady Kāmākhyā.
II.7.86
Contemplate the supreme Mother of letters thus; perform Śrīkaṇṭha placement with names, planets, and lunar mansions, then pīṭha, digit, and mantra placement.
II.7.87
Install the yantra, consecrate it duly in ten ways, scatter the protective substances, and worship the pīṭha.
II.7.88
Beginning eastward, [worship] Gaṇeśa, Gaṇādhipa, Gaṇanātha, and Gaṇakrīḍa, with the [specified] mantra [closing phrase obscure].
II.7.89
Worship Śrī to the east, then Govaṭa; worship with the long alternate mantra joined with Tārā.
II.7.90
