South, [the mantra includes] krīḍāsara, mandra, the left eye, rays and bindu; west, Lohajaṅgha. Worship the field's lord with the Narasiṃha seed.
Note: This verse encodes syllables; the extraction and Hindi misleadingly resemble a list of places. A continuous mantra is not reconstructed.
II.7.91
North [worship] Bhūtanātha with the mantra, Gaurīputra, Baṭuka, and Samayaputraka—
II.7.92
—Jñānaputra and Samayaputra, beginning eastward in order. Contemplate with “hala” [uncertain] and worship with the red [mantra/offering].
II.7.93
[Worship] the guardians of the Śānti [powers], supreme Bindukalā, then Nivṛttikalā and Pratiṣṭhākalā—
II.7.94
—with Māyā seed, eastward in order; then Hetuka, Tripuraghna, and Agnivetālaka—
II.7.95
—northwestward in order, Kāla, Karālaka, Ekapāda, and Bhīma, the four, with gagana [seed].
II.7.96
Asitāṅga and the others with Brāhmī and the accomplishments, the ten Carcikās: worship them in the six points [remaining positional phrase obscure].
II.7.97
At the six points [worship] Madana and Rati's daughter, at both sides the five arrows and bow, the planets and directional guardians.
II.7.98
Worship the seat, contemplate [her] and place the Goddess upon it with the appropriate conception, pronouncing this mantra:
II.7.99
“Come, come, Supreme Lady; make your presence in this maṇḍala, Mother of the world, deliverer across worldly existence's ocean.
II.7.100
“Dwelling in the great lotus forest, embodying causal bliss, made of sound-Brahman, pure Kāmeśvarī, be gracious to me.”
II.7.101
Śāṅkarī, invoke Kāmeśa with “I invoke.” “Homage to Bhava, Śarva, Rudra, and the boon-giver—
II.7.102
“—lord of creatures, self of all bliss, three-locked, three-headed, bearer of the excellent trident—
II.7.103
“—three-eyed, three-timed, destroyer of Tripura; homage to Caṇḍa, Muṇḍa, bearer of the universe's staff—
II.7.104
“—red, smoky, blue-throated; homage to the triple-city form, the various-formed; homage to the sun, sun's lord, and Siddhanātha.”
II.7.105
Not far north of that forest is a low monitor-lizard-shaped stone, white and dark. Śiva is to its west; understand the earlier [arrangement] accordingly.
II.7.106
Gayātīrtha is proclaimed at its belly, northward; the head, [of fourfold measure], is Gayāśiras—
II.7.107
—beside the head, Rāmagayā; south, Rāmapiṇḍa; at the tail, Mānasa Ford; south, the great river.
II.7.108
Bathe there according to prescribed ritual. Beloved, north of it, two arrow-shots away—
II.7.109
—is Pretaśilā Ford; ancestral offerings lead the ancestors to heaven. Offerings at the great river lead ancestors to heaven—
II.7.110
—and offerings at Akṣayavaṭa lead them to Brahmā's world. Bathing at Gayātīrtha releases every sin.
II.7.111
Great Lady, persons [related to oneself] at Gayā should make the remaining rice-ball offerings without sesame [wording uncertain].
II.7.112
Eighteen bow-lengths west of Vāsudeva is the elongated, five-angled Northern [Mānasa], consecrated by sages.
II.7.113
Making ancestral offerings at Northern Mānasa prevents rebirth. The four-angled Śiva south of Koṭiliṅga—
II.7.114
—is Southern Mānasa, destroyer of every sin. Ancestral offerings there lead the ancestors to Brahmā's world.
II.7.115
Ancestral offerings at the great river and Rāma Lake lead ancestors to Brahmā's world, Goddess.
II.7.116
Give rice balls for three days at Gayāśiras, northern Gayā, and Gayā's tail; one rescues the lineage.
II.7.117
Ancestors, fearful of hell, long for sons: “Someone may go to Gayā and deliver us.”
II.7.118
Seeing wish-fulfilling Dīrgheśvarī seven bow-lengths west of Kāmanātha brings sixty thousand years of godlike delight on earth.
II.7.119
Worshipping the Queen with Māyā seed each eighth day grants every Vidyā and preeminence among [vaiśyas/people: reading uncertain].
II.7.120
Go to the wish-fulfilling tree called Tintiḍī, circle it three times, and worship with this mantra:
II.7.121
“Oṃ, homage to the manifest form, praised by all gods, the form of Śiva's dwelling, embodied as the Tintiḍī tree.”
II.7.122
[This is] approved in all mantras, for desired results, liberation, and the right [hand]. Touching or taking the rosary with the left hand—
II.7.123
—or dropping it, another's touching it, breakage, or a year's passage requires reconsecration. Goddess, place it in a copper vessel.
II.7.124
First sprinkle with Gāyatrī, then the five cow products, then with that mantra, “Hūṃ, homage to accomplishment.”
II.7.125
Then with fragrant water and diverse fragrant flowers, place it upon three jars and worship with red flowers.
II.7.126
Give a barley-alkali bali and install the deity's vital powers. Bring the rosary, touch it, and suspend it in space.
II.7.127
At night place the rosary on the pīṭha; give scent, sandalwood, dūrvā, and rice, and install it again on the deity's pīṭha.
II.7.128
A hundred, thousand, ten thousand, niyuta, hundred thousand, and ten million are the counts for repetition and fire-offering.
II.7.129
At each measure [add] eight [the OCR gives “hands”]; repeat at every joint, or combine eight or three seeds [construction uncertain].
II.7.130
“Rosary, rosary, great rosary, embodiment of all: the four aims are placed in you. Rosary, grant accomplishment.”
II.7.131
[Form the seed with] Puṣkarī's friend, the subtle [letter] with the subtle, space and moon, and the heart called accomplishment [encoded wording uncertain].
II.7.132
This is proclaimed the rosary's five-syllabled mantra. Employ it in receiving, installation, and worship.
II.7.133
Place Śiva at repetition's beginning; at its end recite praise, then give bali and gifts according to means. Worship with the root mantra in forward and reverse order.
II.7.134
[Worship] with “Ambe Ambike” and the Purāṇic [formula]. “Victory, Kāmeśī, Cāmuṇḍā; victory, remover of spirits.
II.7.135
“Victory, all-pervading Goddess; Kāmeśvarī, homage. Universal form, auspicious, pure, various-eyed, three-eyed—
II.7.136
“—terrible-formed, auspicious Vidyā, Kāmeśvarī, homage. Conqueror of Māyā, Jaya, Jambhā, spirit-eyed, agitated, imperishable, Mahāmāyā, Great Lady, Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.137
“Terrible-eyed, giver of fear, Goddess destroying all beings, Karālī and Vikarālī, Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.138
“Kālī, terribly valiant, Kāmeśvarī, beloved of Hara, ocean of all scriptures, Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.139
“Lamp of Kāmarūpa, dweller on Nīlakūṭa, crusher of Niśumbha and Śumbha, Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.140
“Kāmākhyā dwelling in Kāmarūpa, Kāmeśvarī beloved of Hara, always grant my desires; Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.141
“Your face delights in drinking blood-wine, ruler of the three worlds; destroyer of Mahiṣāsura, Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.142
“Pleased by goats, greatly terrible Kāmākhyā honoured by gods, victory; pleased granter of desires, Kāmeśvarī, homage.”
II.7.143
A king who has lost his kingdom should be disciplined and pure, fasting on the ninth, eighth, and fourteenth days—
II.7.144
—and within a year regain an untroubled kingdom. Hearing this account of you devotedly, Goddess, releases all sin and leads to supreme nirvāṇa.
II.7.145
“Kāmeśvarī Goddess, beloved of gods and antigods, [lotus-face epithet unclear], homage. Delighted by destroying the gods' enemies' power, embodiment of the three Vedas, I bow.
II.7.146
“White and dark, red and tawny-bodied, your forms shine everywhere; in hand and skull [you hold] the imaginings of auspicious and inauspicious [syntax uncertain]; I bow.
II.7.147
“Arisen in Kāmarūpa, ornament of Kāmapīṭha, support of the universe, Mahāmāyā, Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.148
“Unmanifest-bodied, peaceful, continuous, fulfilled with desire, accessible to Time, supreme, tranquil Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.149
“You dwell within Suṣumnā, contemplated as light, stainless one. I bow to the supreme heroine; Kāmeśvarī, homage.
II.7.150
“Fanged and terrible-faced, adorned with a head-garland, all-pervading Goddess everywhere, Kāmeśvarī, homage.”
II.7.151
At dawn, bathing, eating, cleaning the teeth, and without clothing, do not touch the teeth [or deity's teeth: reference uncertain].
II.7.152
Women should not touch the Goddess on Caitra's thirteenth or Taurus's fourteenth day; touching and sight should be avoided.
II.7.153
Sight brings fear, and a curse falls on the head. Likewise boys should not see her on Jyeṣṭha's bright eighth day.
II.7.154
Nor beautiful unmarried girls; the practitioner should not touch a woman at night, nor [let her touch] bali; never approach the Great Goddess dwelling in a liṅga [text uncertain].
II.7.155
For Brahmins [offer] milk preparations, śāli rice or milk-rice, ghee-soaked edible fruit and flowers with ghee—
II.7.156
—milk, milk-rice or cooked rice, śāli rice with honey, kṛsara, and sugar-sweetmeats.
II.7.157
Animals are approved for kings; rice for Vaiśyas; honey for Śūdras, or animals for all.
II.7.158
Offer a red-mouthed [monkey], a human, buffalo, boar, goat, cāmara, varuṇa [species uncertain], ram, boar, and monitor lizard.
II.7.159
A goat surpasses ten cāmaras; one tortoise is praised above ten goats.
II.7.160
One hare surpasses a hundred tortoises; a boar surpasses a thousand hares.
II.7.161
One buffalo is called superior to two thousand boars; one rhinoceros surpasses two thousand buffaloes.
II.7.162
A human gives incomparable fruit above a thousand rhinoceroses; a red-mouthed [monkey] is praised above two thousand humans.
II.7.163
A white-necked [animal] is praised above two hundred red-mouthed ones; a monitor lizard above two hundred white-necked ones [Hindi: thousand].
II.7.164
Goddess, a human boy [is ranked] above a hundred monitor lizards. Animals become eligible as offerings beyond six months.
Note: The sacrificial ranking, including humans, is the historical text's claim; the verse is reproduced as textual evidence.
II.7.165
A white or black goat beyond two years, [a guggulu-related qualification is obscure], a red-named jackal—
II.7.166
—a fragrant deer, mountain goat, mouse, karāla, and small cat [the boundary between prescribed and prohibited list is uncertain]—
II.7.167
—a kākola, kalaviṅka, royal goose, mynah, cuckoo, parrot, vulture, peacock, and citraka—
II.7.168
—horse, veṇupṛṣṭha, black pigeon, great pigeon, and wagtail—
II.7.169
—heron and crane: carefully avoid these. In destructive sacrifice do not consider a female [eligible: negative force uncertain].
II.7.170
Beloved, giving a pair causes no fault. Offer a buffalo in the cremation ground, near the deity.
II.7.171
One goes to Brahmā's world and enjoys earth like Śiva. Killing [the sacrificial animal] in the inner house leads to eternal Brahman.
II.7.172
Beloved, even now Brahman is seen southward; knowledge-giving Sadāśiva is there, without doubt [syntax uncertain].
II.7.173
Therefore a person falls to earth on the right ear, whether burdened with great sins or lesser sins—
II.7.174
—and through sacrifice in the cremation ground goes liberated to Śiva's abode. Dying there, with or without desire, one becomes lord of the Gaṇas.
II.7.175
A king [may offer] one captured from another country; carefully exclude a great sinner, thief, fool, family's sole hero, Brahman-hater, or man dominated by a woman [syntax difficult].
II.7.176
Jewels, pearls, gold, and copper vessels given to the deity are not treated as spent offerings for twelve days.
II.7.177
Paṭṭī and śāṭī cloth [remain] six months; food upon offering; sweetmeats and kṛsara for half a watch, Great Lady.
II.7.178
Silk [remains] three months; the sacred thread one day; rice and milk-rice as long as they remain hot.
II.7.179
Pārvatī, a head and blood for half a night; curds and milk one muhūrta; ghee one watch, Śāṅkarī.
II.7.180
Oleander and bilva leaves [remain] a day and night; hibiscus and bandhūka garlands a month and a half.
II.7.181
Oleander and lotus garlands [remain] [a month: supplied by gloss]; bilva [garlands] half a month; betel only upon presentation.
Note: Durations in 180–181 are inconsistent in the source and are not silently harmonized.
II.7.182
Carefully offer dates, jackfruit, grapes, citron, śāvaka, banana, orange, jambū, lotus-root, and madhūka.
II.7.183
Bilva fruit, pomegranate, jayantī, cucumber, tripura, and eggplant delight the Goddess.
II.7.184
Beloved, pomegranate and bilva fruit do not become spent offerings. Carefully employ saugandhika leaves.
II.7.185
Offer banana, citron, and cooked milk; oven-cooked kaśeru and jambavāla are dear [to her].
II.7.186
Ginger, salt, cumin, long pepper, nutmeg [jātīkoṣa], and tinduka are especially dear to the Goddess.
II.7.187
Do not offer rāmarambhā fruit or flowers, smoke-heated banana, or lotus seeds. Avoid śrāvaṇaka grain, fish [reading uncertain], and twice-cooked food.
II.7.188
Offer golden-coloured coconut and the leftward [variety], especially its water, to the Great Goddess.
II.7.189
Avoid coconut and bhāṇḍīra in offerings to gods and ancestors. Ripe bakula fruit and lotus fruit—
II.7.190
—offered to the Great Goddess give a cāndrāyaṇa vow's fruit. Goddess, hear briefly the chapter on flowers.
II.7.191
Seasonal flowers, mallikā and jāti, white and red blossoms, blue and pale lotuses—
II.7.192
—kiṃśuka, tagara, hibiscus, kanaka, campaka, bakula, mandāra, kunda, and kuraṇṭaka—
II.7.193
—datura and others, bandhūka, agastya, madana, sindhuvāra, tender dūrvā shoots and leaves—
II.7.194
—tulasī and tender bilva leaves: whoever gives thousands of oleander and mādhya [flowers] obtains desired wishes and honour in the Goddess's world.
II.7.195
One oleander equals two thousand lotuses. Never give spent offerings or forest flowers without [the appropriate dedication].
II.7.196
The Goddesses, though striving, cannot collect [them], for ten Yakṣas guard each blossom—
II.7.197
—and five Yakṣa women surround every flower. Therefore gather and offer flowers to gods and ancestors.
II.7.198
Śāṅkarī, build a flower-house over Kāmākhyā; obtain desires here and honour in Durgā's world.
II.7.199
Worship with oleander and related flowers gives Agniṣṭoma's fruit and honour in the sun's world.
II.7.200
Devoted worship of Caṇḍikā with a lotus garland gives Jyotiṣṭoma's fruit and honour in the sun's world.
II.7.201
Baka flowers and related blossoms, and rudrajaṭā, give Vājapeya sacrifice's fruit, without doubt.
II.7.202
Blue lotus is foremost among flowers. Hear the merit of duly offering Durgā a garland of a thousand blue lotuses:
II.7.203
One becomes the Goddess's attendant and is honoured in Rudra's world for thousands and hundreds of ten-million years.
II.7.204
[Unbroken rice] arising from vakra, sūkṣma, rāja, and red varieties of grain—
II.7.205
—seven, eight, or nine unbroken grains with dūrvā shoots, offered on the eighth or ninth day, give a horse-sacrifice's fruit.
II.7.206
Giving fragrant unguent gives Jyotiṣṭoma's fruit.
II.7.207
Kuṅkuma-anointed arghya gives a thousand cow-gifts' fruit. Worshipping Durgā with sandalwood, aloe, camphor, and kuṅkuma-anointed white flowers gives Agniṣṭoma's fruit.
II.7.208
Neem leaves, kunda, tamāla and āmalakī leaves, kahlāra, tulasī, lotus, and [ahlāni: unclear] flowers—
II.7.209
—these and other buds do not become stale. Śāṅkarī, [the rule concerning] torn jāti flowers [continues].
II.7.210
Lotus, dūrvā shoots, and tulasī leaves do not quickly become stale; nor [the specified] flowers, Śāṅkarī.
II.7.211
Do not worship with jhiṇṭī, yellow tagara, white [oḍhra], or black vijaya.
Note: The Sanskrit has prohibitions; the Hindi omits the negative in its paraphrase.
II.7.212
For accomplishment of preliminary practice, repeat three hundred thousand times, perform placement, libation, fire-offering, and one hundred eight [acts]. The self-controlled practitioner gains Agniṣṭoma's fruit.
II.7.213
Beloved, whoever worships the Supreme Lady three times daily on the bright eighth, ninth, and fourteenth days—
II.7.214
—goes to her supreme abode, sports there long, then becomes a king on earth.
II.7.215
Whoever worships Kāmeśī always, with bathing, fasting, discipline, worship, vigil, and cleaning—
II.7.216
—mounts an excellent vehicle crowded with banners and garlands and goes to Brahmā's world, delighting for eternal years.
II.7.217
Therefore, always devoted, worship Durgā according to one's ample means, desiring great meritorious fruit.
II.7.218
The merit of duly worshipping Caṇḍikā at solstices, equinoxes, ṣaḍaśītimukha [solar occasions], and for four months is obtained on Kārttika's ninth day.
II.7.219
Hear the merit of worshipping the Great Goddess on Āśvayuja's bright ninth day:
II.7.220
One obtains the fruit of a thousand horse-sacrifices and a hundred Vājapeyas, without any doubt.
II.7.221
Twenty-one bow-lengths north of Hanūmat is the exceedingly rare place called Muktimaṇḍapikā.
II.7.222
Remaining there and repeating [the mantra] brings the highest destination. Join the hands in the añjali gesture and propitiate the Goddess:
II.7.223
“Queen of all gods, remover of devotees' fear, homage. I am sunk in worldly existence's ocean: protect me, Supreme Lady.”
II.7.224
Propitiate the Goddess thus and bow full-length. Then worship the guru devotedly with flowers, fragrance, and unguents.
II.7.225
Feed unmarried girls there, keep a night vigil, and arrange recitation of the Great Goddess's glory and songs.
II.7.226
The discerning should pass the night contemplating and praising the supreme Goddess. Each month's eighth, especially fourteenth day—
II.7.227
—at cow-dust time [dusk], take her by chariot or palanquin to Kāmeśvara's abode. Seeing her there removes every sin and brings honour in her world.
II.7.228
Śāṅkarī, taking her to Muktimaṇḍapikā and worshipping gives the merit of ten horse-sacrifices, without doubt.
II.7.229
Do not conduct the procession by day, at night, or deep midnight. At evening on autumn's seventh day go south of the city; one gains all sacrifices' fruit.
Note: The general prohibition and prescribed evening exception are retained as printed.
II.7.230
Worship on the eighth day; on the ninth bathe the Goddess in flowing water. Performing the bath by day causes no fault.
II.7.231
Touching her in the chariot prevents sorrow on earth. Carrying her on one's shoulders gives a horse-sacrifice at every step; therefore carefully make a palanquin.
II.7.232
Thirty bow-lengths away is the mountain for the Goddess's bath. See Kāmeśvara established on the ground-pīṭha there.
II.7.233
North is Kāmasara, twelve hand-lengths in extent. Bathe and repeat on the desire-seat; wishes are fulfilled.
II.7.234
Bathing at Kāmakuṇḍa and seeing Kāmeśvara prevents return [to rebirth] and brings honour in Rudra's world.
II.7.235
Worship the four-armed, lotus-seated god, holding trident and skull-staff and granting boons and fearlessness, destroyer of all sins; propitiate him with Dakṣiṇāmūrti's mantra.
II.7.236
“Oṃ, homage to Śiva; homage to the night-formed one, Śiva worshipped by Śiva, compassionate one, refuge within Māyā's wilderness, Śeṣa, great darkness, refuge, Gaṇa and companion of terrible hosts, maker of many worlds.”
Note: Several epithets in this long mantra are imperfectly recovered; this translates readable lexical material, not a verified liturgical transcription.
II.7.237
Bathe with ghee and honey; worship with fragrance, lamps, kanaka, bilva leaves, red rudrajaṭā, victory cries, praises, and various food offerings.
II.7.238
Seeing foremost Kāmeśvara on Caitra's bright thirteenth day leads to the highest state.
II.7.239
On the eighth night's portion, take him to Kāmeśvarī's house and worship especially with the Goddess; one obtains Agniṣṭoma's fruit.
II.7.240
Seeing Kāmeśvarī there gives whatever fruit one asks, without doubt.
II.7.241
Auspicious one, eight bow-lengths to Kambala's left [Hindi: south], worship supreme Koṭīśvarī joined with the god.
II.7.242
A seeker of sons should see but never touch the Goddess. Released from every sin, one is honoured in Rudra's world.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Second Part, Chapter7, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand verses.

STATUS
Entries1–242 are recorded. Verses19–28 have been restored and translated from the alternate Mishra scan, as labelled; no source-gap placeholders remain. Other uncertain readings are marked. This is a provisional reading translation, not an independently reviewed critical edition.

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, CHAPTER 8
English working translation
Source: Mishra edition, 1984, printed pages441–465/PDF459–483. Sanskrit primary; Hindi consulted. Place names are retained rather than assigned speculative modern locations. Uncertain words and ritual claims are marked.

II.8.1 — The Lord said:
That same day, Goddess, [go to] auspicious, sin-destroying, sky-flowing Māyā, renowned as Lokacakṣus—
II.8.2
—two arrow-shots south of the Goddess. Three streams appear there; the middle stream is Sarasvatī.
II.8.3
The southern stream is Vāruṇā; the northern, Yāmunā. Bathing in Yāmunā releases dreadful sins.
II.8.4
Bathing in Sarasvatī brings honour in Viṣṇu's world; bathing in Vāruṇā grants unsurpassed liberation.
II.8.5
Where the three streams meet, Ananta appears; she is the Goddess Ākāśagaṅgā, destroyer of great sins.
II.8.6
“Thousand-eyed Goddess, destroyer of worldly bondage, auspicious one on Nīla Mountain, Jāhnavī, remove my sin; homage.” Bathe with this and worship with the sound seed.
II.8.7
Not far north is Vāsudeva, of white and beautiful form.
II.8.8
Worship the liṅga with Smara, fragrance, and milk-rice. Seeing it on Kārttika's twelfth day grants liberation; night vigil releases every sin.
II.8.9
Four bow-lengths south of Gaṅgā, the Lord sports with groups of women in the great cremation ground.
II.8.10
Gods, Gandharvas, Ṛṣis, and Cāraṇas always come respectfully to propitiate the god.
II.8.11
Worshipping Mahādeva, lord of the hosts, with devotional feeling grants Gaṇa-lordship, rare even for gods.
II.8.12
Even today a great marvel appears daily: human bones deposited there become ashes [the clause concerning Prajāpati is unclear].
II.8.13
Worshipping Mahādeva there leads in another body to a divine world, without doubt.
II.8.14
Worship the all-beloved ancestral lord on Kārttika's bright fourteenth day; through his favour become Rudra's attendant.
II.8.15
Three aspects appear there: north, Hara; west, Heruka, the imperishable form of Viṣṇu—
II.8.16
—south, Bhairavī called Tripurā. Worshipping the Supreme Lord with the Pradāna [Prāsāda?] mantra grants a horse-sacrifice's excellent fruit.
II.8.17
Devoted worship with the kula-initial trikuṇḍa mantra grants Mahāvidyā, without doubt.
II.8.18
[Worship] Heruka as Vāsudeva with the ten-syllable [Hindi: twelve-syllable] mantra; one becomes lord of worlds and foremost of one's birth-group.
II.8.19
Worship the Supreme Lady with blood, meat, and liquor; never offer tortoise flesh at night.
II.8.20
Carefully avoid cāmara and magura fish. Eating ciñcā, nīlaśaiva, or viśāka [plants uncertain] through negligence brings great danger; touching brings a curse.
II.8.21
Do not touch [these] greens for seven nights, beloved Kāmeśvarī. Performing three cāndrāyaṇas then brings purification.
II.8.22
Having inadvertently eaten rājīva, rajanī, or cāmara-produced greens, Goddess, perform that same vow.
II.8.23
Avoid karkeṇḍhu with sugar, dambha greens, kūṣmāṇḍa, and pārvatī [plant names uncertain]—
II.8.24
—especially on the eighth, ninth, and thirteenth days. [Make] twenty-one threads, tripled—
II.8.25
—then tripled together [called triśaka: unclear]. Use silk from a cocoon; if unavailable, red thread.
II.8.26
Do not show the rosary to another or touch it with the left hand. Forest-dweller, renunciant, and student, beloved, in repeating Viṣṇu's mantra or another good mantra—
II.8.27
—hold it in the left hand, repeat the seven seed syllables, then each seed with the right [hand], beautiful one.
Note: The apparent exception to the left-hand prohibition is preserved, not silently harmonized.
II.8.28
“Homage to the boon-giving Tintiḍī at the house-door.” [Repeat] a thousand times; one sheds sin like a serpent its old skin.
II.8.29
The tract behind Kāmeśvara extending to Siddheśvara is proclaimed Chāyārudra.
II.8.30
Shade is always present there. Releasing a bull in that field, beautiful one, gives a hundred Agniṣṭomas' merit.
II.8.31
Great Lady, making rice-ball offerings without sesame at Chāyārudra in Māgha discharges one's debt to ancestors.
II.8.32
Go to Vindhyācala; its dark-red stone is called Vindhyeśī. Worship with lotuses and other offerings.
II.8.33
Worship her with Kāmeśvarī's mantra. Pārvatī, seeing Vindhyeśī gives the fruit of ten thousand cow-gifts, truly.
II.8.34
Northeast, more than a hundred arrow-shots away, is the stone Suradīrghikā marked by Ākāśagaṅgā—
II.8.35
—and slightly raised toward its southern front, lovely Lalitā, remover of Brahmin-killing's sin.
II.8.36
The aśvattha is Nandin's form; at its root is a tortoise-shaped stone. Seeing that god frees a person from sin.
II.8.37
There is a yoni-seated liṅga measuring four hands and a lamp-shaped pool destroying all sin. Seeing Vyāseśvara there removes sin.
II.8.38
Bathing at Vyāsatīrtha and worshipping Lalitā gives the great fruit of a thousand horse-sacrifices.
II.8.39
Twenty bow-lengths east is a pomegranate tree called Vanadā, its leaves lotus-shaped and marked by resin—
II.8.40
—at its root dwells Uccāvaraṇa [Hindi: Uccāraṇa]. Worshipping her prevents affliction by planetary faults.
II.8.41
A devoted childless woman touching the tree becomes pregnant; one whose hand is lost regains it, and in time other limbs return.
Note: These are the text's miraculous claims, not medically verified effects.
II.8.42
A little east of the pomegranate is a nine-hand opening, Bhuvaneśvara. Worshipping with the Kāma seed grants victory.
II.8.43
Northwest, in Agastya's auspicious hermitage, worship Gadādhara with flowers and other offerings.
II.8.44
Not far from the deity is a brilliant white stone, the great liṅga Jalpeśa. Worship while uttering [the mantra].
II.8.45
Beloved, duly bathing at Saubhāgya and worshipping Jalpeśa grants Agniṣṭoma's fruit.
II.8.46
West, in a tract of twenty-one [units], dwells Sadāśiva marked as lord of the underworld. Bowing devotedly prevents rebirth.
II.8.47
Śāṅkarī, in that deity's Kālahastaka tract, on lovely Govinda Mountain is a white stone—
II.8.48
—[understand as Govinda and worship on Hari's day, supplied by Hindi]. Nine bow-lengths east is the red stone Śaraṇeśī, destroyer of great sins.
II.8.49
On high Śivācala is supreme Śivā called Prakaṭā; worshipping carefully brings great fortune.
II.8.50
Nine arrow-shots north of Vindhyācala dwells Mahālakṣmī; worship her with white flowers.
II.8.51
Bathe at Śrīkuṇḍa on Śrīparvata; on Āśvina's full moon bathe at Dhruvakuṇḍa, see and worship devotedly; one becomes lord of the earth.
II.8.52
Go to Gautama's hermitage and worship the bull-bannered one; a person does not go to hell, and sins disappear.
II.8.53
Śāṅkarī, within the tract extending west-northward to Southern Mānasa, worship [the deity]; one obtains Brahmā's world.
II.8.54
On that lake's bank is unsurpassed Haṃsatīrtha. Worshipping the twelve Ādityas grants excellent brilliance.
II.8.55
Worshipping Revanta grants the highest destination; worshipping Indra brings great power and good fortune, Gaurī.
II.8.56
Worshipping Puruṣottama fulfils all wishes; worshipping Nārāyaṇa makes one lord of men.
II.8.57
Seeing and bowing to Narasiṃha grants victory in battle; worshipping Varāha grants earthly sovereignty.
II.8.58
Worshipping Somanātha brings honour in Rudra's world. Pāṇḍukūṭa's stream is Narmadā; bathing on the fourteenth day gives a horse-sacrifice.
II.8.59
The yoni emerging between Śiva and Viṣṇu is the great river, destroyer of all sin.
II.8.60
The stream between Nitamba and Dhana [names uncertain], Supreme Lady, is Maṅgalā, producer of every auspiciousness.
II.8.61
The stream between [Viśva] and Śrīparvata is Sarasvatī. Drinking its clear water makes one foremost of poets.
II.8.62
Mataṅga's stream is Narmadā, without doubt; Kāmakuṇḍa's stream is called Kāmagaṅgā.
II.8.63
Kāmākhyā's stream is proclaimed Gaṅgā; Nandikuṇḍa's is Madhuśravā.
II.8.64
Kāmadhenu's stream is Sudharmiṇī; Padmaśaila's is remembered as Urvaśī Gaṅgā.
II.8.65
Nīlakuṇḍa's stream is Subhadrā; Vyāsakuṇḍa's is Candrabhāgā.
II.8.66
Śakraśaila's stream is Urvaśī; Somakuṇḍa's river is Vaitaraṇī.
II.8.67
Yamaśaila's stream is Godāvarī. Bathing, drinking, and bowing at Bhaṇḍīśvara's stream grants a hundred Agniṣṭomas' excellent fruit.
II.8.68
Beloved, go to Dharmāraṇya, bathe at Rāma Lake, and see Koṭiliṅga; one obtains a divine body.
II.8.69
Seeing Koṭiliṅga thirty bow-lengths north of Kāma gives a Vājapeya's fruit.
II.8.70
“Life's rod, eternal and red one, homage; thousand-headed Koṭiliṅga, homage.
II.8.71
“Lord of mountains, lover of mountain trees, lord of sacrifice, Koṭiliṅga, homage.”
II.8.72
Worship with this and hibiscus flowers; give milk-rice bali, praise, repeat, and dismiss.
II.8.73
The stone adjoining its southern side is Vetāla Mahādeva; leftward, Viṣṇu Dvikarṇaka [Hindi differs]. Take flowers in joined hands and recite single-mindedly:
II.8.74
“Dharma, desire, wealth, and liberation; fierce, afflicting one; Sāṃkhya, foremost of Sāṃkhya, Vetāla, homage, homage.
II.8.75
“Wearing black-antelope skin above and a yellow cord, you alone are Brahmā, lord of the universe, form of Brahman; homage.”
II.8.76
Before it, go to Brahmayoni, uttering its mantra. Knowing [or entering] Brahmayoni prevents entry into another womb.
II.8.77
One does not enter animal birth, but enters Brahmā's state. In Śiva's beloved region, revealing liberation's path—
II.8.78
—“Be gracious, lord of gods; save me from the danger of birth.” Emerging from Brahmayoni, worship Gaṇeśa at the doorway—
II.8.79
—the great-bodied one upon the stone, with this mantra, practitioner:
II.8.80
“Foremost Lambodara, giver of the gods' desired objects, lord of all, master, homage [the closing epithet involving birth's danger is unclear].”
II.8.81
Go toward liberation's path facing Śakra. Left and right are the two ages [the repeated Satya designation is unclear]—
II.8.82
—above, Kṛta; beside, Tretā and Dvāpara. Devotedly bow to Bhuvaneśvara's liṅga situated in Balivaktra and attain the lordly state.
II.8.83
Bowing to the four ages and touching Kapardin prevents birth in a womb and affliction by the age's defects.
II.8.84
“He is lord of the ages, bearing the form of the world's births. Supreme Lord, [remove] the sin committed in that age—
II.8.85
“—lord of men in Tretā, sustaining [beings] from birth [phrase uncertain], remove the earthly sin committed in that age.
II.8.86
“True means and truth, form of Nara-Nārāyaṇa, cause of Kṛta and the other ages, true dharma, homage.
II.8.87
“Worshipped by kings at victory and other occasions, lord of the wheel of ages, I bow continually with devotion; remove sin, homage.”
II.8.88
Worshipping Kapardin with the five-syllable mantra grants Rājasūya's and horse-sacrifice's fruit.
II.8.89
Seeing Kāmadhenu fulfils every wish. Worship, bow, then feed Brahmins.
II.8.90
“Daughter of Surabhi, homage; wish-roaming one [additional epithet uncertain]. May that cow-formed Goddess remove my sin.”
II.8.91
Seeing Kāmadhenu gives the fruit of a weighed-person gift at Kurukṣetra during a solar eclipse.
II.8.92
Give the guru's fee, offer arghya to the sun, perform pacification, and arrange the Goddess's seat.
II.8.93
Beloved, rise, behold the sun, and recite the two mantras: “Homage to Kālī, Girijā, Kāmeśvarī. Homage to the mountain-born Goddess, Gaurī, destroyer of sin. Homage to those establishing fords, Vyāsa and the others; to hosts, protectors, and field-lords; to [Pauṇḍravighna: uncertain], Kālabhairava, Dakṣiṇāmūrti, and Daṇḍapāṇi.”
Note: The extended prayer after verse93 is unnumbered in the supplied printing and included here rather than dropped.
II.8.94
Confidence that one will go to the ford, fast, make ancestral offerings, and repeat [the mantra]: these are signs of accomplishment.
II.8.95 — The Goddess said:
Will a sinner continually dwelling in this field obtain liberation from dreadful sins such as liquor-drinking?
II.8.96 — Īśvara said:
One living in the holy field while continually devoted to sin enters animal birth for ten thousand years—
II.8.97
—then, in that holy city, gains knowledge and liberation. Beloved, this is my secret.
II.8.98
[Residence] is easy elsewhere, but rare at five places: Agastyatīrtha, Lauhitya, Maṇikarṇa Lake, Apunarbhava, and Candrakuṇḍa.
II.8.99
[A hundred thousand paths and the field's boundaries] are guarded by hosts stationed outside and in the empty ways [text corrupt].
II.8.100
Kāla, Raṇabhadra, mighty Saurabha, Vetāla, and Vikaṇṭa are the hosts stationed eastward.
II.8.101
Ekajaṅgha, Anala, the mud-smeared one, Ghaṇṭākarṇa, and [Uddha?] stand in the south [and above: syntax unclear].
II.8.102
Balanāśa and Bhīṣaṇa are westward; Pañcama, Lohitākṣa, and Nandana [the directional continuation is unclear]—
II.8.103
—Keśava and Cakrapāṇi are northern hosts of Dhanada; Madhuna, Madhuka, Jayanta, and Madhuśrī—
II.8.104
—[these] always guard Kāmeśvarī. Gaṇeśas Kāladanta, Vikarṇa, and Kapidhvaja guard the doorway; Hara himself guards the pavilion.
II.8.105
Kanda, Makaranda, Prabala, Anudodhara, Soma, and Vipula are hosts stationed at Aśvatīrtha.
II.8.106
A hundred thousand Yakṣiṇīs guard liberation's door; ten thousand guard the inner house.
II.8.107
At Kubjatīrtha [eight with ten thousands: numerical construction uncertain]. Obstacles dwell particularly at the beginning of shrines, religious works, vows, and sacrifices.
II.8.108
First worship them with bali, sweetmeats, and the rest; otherwise obstacles arise, beloved.
II.8.109
Other obstacles dwell in the body, knowledge-destroying and situated in the mind. Hear them, beloved.
II.8.110
Some turn one back; some drive one onward, Goddess.
II.8.111
Closeness, distance [further terms corrupt], remembering sins, laziness, and faultfinding—
II.8.112
—grief, delusion, old age, illness, youth, lost wealth, quarrels with one's wife, famine, and domestic trouble—
II.8.113
—many vows and the thought “I am righteous,” grief, and considering wrongdoing free of sin—
II.8.114
—considering tulasī mere tree-leaves, dhātrī mere tree-fruit, Śālagrāma a stone fragment, an image merely wood—
II.8.115
—a Brahmin merely human, self-born Śiva a rounded stone, a conch a kind of snail, and a rhinoceros [ordinary] flesh—
II.8.116
—considering sacred ford-water merely water, Gaṅgā merely a river, and holy ground merely earth—
II.8.117
—these obstacles return repeatedly. Mind is the cause here; mind alone causes human bondage and liberation.
II.8.118
Attachment and devotion to [evil thoughts] in the mind cause suffering; the inwardly corrupted mind prevents bathing at holy fords.
II.8.119
Whoever reads or hears [this] obtains enjoyment and liberation; the seeker of sons gains a son, the seeker of fame gains fame.
II.8.120
The student gains knowledge; the victory-seeker victory; sins beginning with Brahmin-killing are destroyed at their root.
II.8.121
The childless woman gains a son; a maiden a good husband; the liberation-seeker liberation, and the enjoyment-seeker enjoyment.
II.8.122
The seeker of poetry becomes a poet and finds essence in the inessential; the seeker of knowledge gains knowledge [that rescues] all worldly existence.
II.8.123
This blessed Yoginī Tantra brings well-being. Reading one verse prevents untimely death; reading half a verse destroys planetary afflictions.

COLOPHON
Thus ends Second Part, Chapter8, with the vernacular commentary, in the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand verses.

STATUS
All123 verse units have draft entries, with the unnumbered prayer preserved after93. Uncertain readings are flagged; no modern geographical identifications are invented.

YOGINĪ TANTRA — SECOND PART, FINAL CHAPTER (9 IN SEQUENCE)
English working translation
Source: Mishra edition1984; printed pages465–515/PDF483–533. The final colophon repeats “eighth chapter,” although it follows a completed eighth chapter and restarts at verse1; this file numbers it9 in sequence and preserves that discrepancy. Sanskrit primary, Hindi consulted. Uncertain readings are explicitly marked; devotional and social claims are those of the historical text.

II.9.1 — The Lord said:
Goddess, hear of a pure place, rare on earth, auspicious like Hari's field, furnished with eight hundred liṅgas—
II.9.2
—the Viṣṇupuṣkara field, containing eight hundred sacred fords, crowded with flourishing people, men and women—
II.9.3
—with many learned people, endowed with wealth and grain—
II.9.4
—with houses and gateways, rampart-adorned on earth, strewn with varied gems and splendid jewels—
II.9.5
—crowded with upper chambers, adorned with streets and white palaces like royal geese.
II.9.6
Yet one [impure in heart] remains unclean like a liquor vessel, even washed in water. Dwelling in caves or mountain peaks, bearing three staffs, ashes or garments, reading Purāṇas and Vedic doctrinal truth: all is contrary [or futile] if the heart is impure.
II.9.7
Neither pilgrimage, gifts, vows, nor stages of life [purify] the evil-minded, evil-intentioned person, like one destroyed by illness.
II.9.8
Mastering the senses, wherever one dwells, there are one's Kurukṣetra, Prayāga, Puṣkara, and Gayā.
II.9.9
Do not transgress drinking/customary observance or local dharma. Whatever conduct belongs to a pīṭha is the prescribed rule there.
II.9.10
Faults of touch and non-touch, yoni, and drinking are not reckoned [there]. Reversal of marriage order and a younger brother's marrying first cause no fault—
II.9.11
—nor sleeping and the rest through association with women [scope and wording uncertain].
II.9.12
Great Lady, at Jālandhara fish and meat are condemned; purity [in footwear?] and chewing [are discussed: the damaged wording is uncertain].
II.9.13
There is no [requirement of] full twilight observance or time-rule. The religion of the Yoginī pīṭha is regarded as Kirāta-born.
II.9.14
In Kāmarūpa, renunciation and long [observance: unclear] [are restricted]; do not abandon meat, Goddess; thus celibacy is not [the rule].
II.9.15
Association brings no sin; resort to women's dharma. [A phrase concerning women and semen is obscure.] They should always partake of betel.
II.9.16
Goose, pigeon, tortoise, and boar are edible; abandoning them in Kāmarūpa leads to a bad destination.
II.9.17
In Saumāra there is inferior conduct, eating and selling everything; women are always constrained there, and the king is meritorious.
II.9.18
In Kolvapīṭha, follow the rules of one's birth-group; determine oneself through consideration of right and wrong.
II.9.19
At Mahendra [dwell] wise yogins knowing Brahman; at Śrīhaṭṭa there is abundant drinking and no [restriction/care] concerning food.
II.9.20
Thus I have explained what you asked. Do not give this to a non-disciple or one who reviles gods and Brahmins—
II.9.21
—nor to a slanderer lacking Vedic devotion; give it to one devoted and intent on one's own dharma.
II.9.22
[The place is] adorned with blue, red, and white palaces, protected by weapons and ornamented by moats—
II.9.23
—with white, red, yellow, black, smoky, and other-coloured banners waving in the wind—
II.9.24
—delighted by constant festivals, resounding with instruments, adorned with vīṇā, flute, drum, and kṣepaṇī—
II.9.25
—with divine temples and excellent gardens, adorned everywhere with varied worship—
II.9.26
—where delighted slender-waisted women appear, their necks adorned with heavy necklaces and eyes long as lotus petals—
II.9.27
—with full raised breasts, full-moon faces, smooth curls, lovely cheeks, and sounding girdles and anklets—
II.9.28
—with beautiful hips and eyes reaching toward the ears. [The place has] varied reservoirs adorned with hundreds of lotuses—
II.9.29
—and charming, clear-water lakes with water-lilies, white and blue lotuses—
II.9.30
—with kadamba birds, cakravākas, waterfowl, groups of kāraṇḍavas and geese, and land-going creatures—
II.9.31
—varied trees, sacred sounds, and other waters adorn it everywhere.
II.9.32
Lord Hayagrīva Janārdana himself dwells there. All the earth's fords, rivers, lakes, lotus ponds, tanks, wells, pools, and oceans—
II.9.33
—Rudra with Umā [OCR: Soma] and his hosts formerly gathered their waters separately for the welfare of all worlds.
II.9.34
Beautiful one, in that field is the ford Apunarbhava; even enjoying desired objects there [one avoids rebirth], hence its name.
II.9.35
Beloved, whoever journeys to that great field in Māgha, self-controlled, bathes duly in the lake with faith—
II.9.36
—and satisfies gods, sages, people, and ancestors with sesame-water according to name and lineage, obtains a horse-sacrifice's fruit.
II.9.37
At eclipses, equinoxes, transitions, solstices, beginnings of ages, ṣaḍaśīti occasions, and other auspicious days—
II.9.38
—a person giving wealth and other gifts to Brahmins there obtains a hundredfold the fruit of other fords.
II.9.39
Giving rice balls to one's ancestors on that lake's bank gives them imperishable satisfaction, without doubt.
II.9.40
The pool is proclaimed eight bow-lengths in size, between Varāha and Kāma, granting every desire.
II.9.41
Because there is no coming into birth again, it is named Apunarbhava. Bathing there leads to the sun's abode.
II.9.42
“Because a creature immersed in you is not born again, I bathe at this great ford. Remove sin; homage.” Bathe thus and see three-eyed God.
II.9.43
[See] Gokarṇa, Vikarṇa, and wish-granting Yogīśa; Gokarṇa is bull-shaped, Vikarṇa human-shaped.
II.9.44
[Seeing Yogīśa brings] supreme knowledge of yoga. On the northern lake-bank, on Mount Bhadrakāśa—
II.9.45
—is the stone Potravittā, with red radiance in the middle, beloved. Five bow-lengths away is the field Haravīthika.
II.9.46
Worship the three deities in their prescribed portions of the Śaiva stone with varied fragrant flowers. Dying on the fourteenth day in Gemini brings liberation [Hindi differs in construction].
II.9.47
“Trident and fearlessness in hand, bearer of matted locks, bull-bannered God Gokarṇa, homage, homage.
II.9.48
“Age-formed god, moon-handed Viṣṇu, holder of mace and Śārṅga, Vikarṇa, homage, homage.
II.9.49
“Great Lord, bull-mounted, knowledge-formed knower, knower of dharma, beautiful lord of yogins, homage.”
II.9.50
Nine bow-lengths east of Apunarbhava is Vārāṇasīkuṇḍa, seven bow-lengths wide. Bathing and dying there brings liberation.
II.9.51
Carefully bathing there on Caitra's Kāmatrayodaśī releases every sin and leads to Brahmā's state.
II.9.52
A single bath in Vārāṇasīkuṇḍa immediately gives the fruit of bathing at every ford for a hundred years.
II.9.53
Śāṅkarī, five bow-lengths farther east is Mārkaṇḍeya Lake; bathing there leads to Śiva.
II.9.54
On the lake's northern bank is Maheśvara called Mārkaṇḍeśvara. Bathe, then behold him—
II.9.55
—worship three-eyed God of gods, honoured by Āditya [Hindi also treats Āditya as worshipped]. Released from all sins, mount an excellent vehicle—
II.9.56
—and, praised by Gandharvas, go to Śiva's world, delighting there for a kalpa, beautiful one.
II.9.57
Wise, foremost sage Mārkaṇḍeya performed austerity [here], hence Mārkaṇḍeya Lake: “Lake, remove my sin.”
II.9.58
Bathe with this, shave the head, carefully perform ancestral offerings, and fast.
II.9.59
At clear dawn complete daily observances. Great Lady, not far from Gokarṇa and Vikarṇa—
II.9.60
—is Brahmasara, measuring twenty-one [bow-lengths]. Bathing at that sin-destroying ford prevents rebirth.
II.9.61
“Formerly made by Brahmā for release from all sins, greatly fortunate Brahmakuṇḍa, rescue me from worldly existence's ocean.”
II.9.62
Bathe with this. West of the lake is the dark mountain-formed deity named Varāha.
II.9.63
Devotedly bowing to him gives delight in Viṣṇu's world.
II.9.64
“Great Varāha, tawny-haired [additional bodily epithet uncertain], bearing earth upon your tusk-tip, your body shining like Nīlācala, homage, homage.”
II.9.65
Three arrow-shots northeast of Gokarṇa, go to lovely Madana Mountain and behold Śaṅkara—
II.9.66
—Mahādeva named Kedāra, saluted by every god. See that imperishable liṅga with faith and concentration—
II.9.67
—and worship devotedly with charming fragrance, flowers, incense, lamps, foods, salutations, and praise—
II.9.68
—with full-length prostrations, dance, song, and other services according to rule; one goes to Śiva's world.
II.9.69
“Madana, foremost [mountain/ocean: uncertain], giver of happiness and fortune, I ascend your summit. Remove sin; homage.”
II.9.70
Face east, proceed and circumambulate; rescuing [oneself/ancestors: object omitted] at once, go to Śiva's world.
II.9.71
Contemplate white Śiva, upon a white bull and lotus-seat, wearing a white-serpent sacred thread, granting boons and fearlessness; moon, sun, and fire his eyes, matted crown crescent-bearing, smeared with white ash, half-woman Lord, five-faced and three-eyed, called Sadyojāta, Vāmadeva, Tatpuruṣa, Aghora, and Īśāna. West [he bears] drum, sword, and other things; north Vāmadeva, milk-white, conch and discus; east Tatpuruṣa, molten-gold-coloured, mace and lotus; south Aghora, vermilion-bright, trident-bearing, tawny terrible fangs, like a dark cloud or collyrium [the enumeration is internally uneven].
II.9.72
Contemplate Kedāra Śiva thus; take flowers in joined hands and worship with his mantra according to Śiva Tantra.
II.9.73
“Homage to the crescent-crested, the skull-staff-bearer, trident-handed Kedāra; homage, homage.”
II.9.74
“I bow to the lord of all worlds, the imperishable cause of liberation, partless, supreme, ancient God.”
II.9.75
“Homage to imperishable Śambhu, protector of all; beyond sound and qualities, imperishable Śambhu, homage.”
II.9.76
Propitiate Kedāra thus, then go west to the Brahmavaṭa tree and ascertain ritual completeness.
II.9.77
Bowing to Kedāra and the wish-fulfilling tree immediately destroys sins accumulated over ten births.
II.9.78
“Unmanifest-formed, dwelling upon great Malaya, standing above Mahendra, banyan tree, homage, homage.”
II.9.79
Three arrow-shots north of Kedāra, worship lotus-eyed Hara in the field of Pauṣpada city.
II.9.80
“Sunk in worldly existence's ocean, seized by sin, unconscious: protect me, destroyer of Bhaga's eyes, enemy of Tripura; homage.
II.9.81
“Homage to peaceful Śiva, remover of every sin; enemy of Tripura, homage; Kamaleśa, homage.”
II.9.82
Beloved, one arrow-shot south of the wish-tree is the umbrella-shaped Pāriyātraka Mountain. Merely ascending prevents rebirth.
II.9.83
It is highest among sixty-eight mountains. Go next with concentrated mind to Mandara Mountain—
II.9.84
—on its east dwells Hari, enemy of Madhu. Seeing him rescues a hundred lineages.
II.9.85
Drinking Kedāra's water, touching Kāmadhenu, and devotedly worshipping Keśava prevents every future birth.
II.9.86
Beloved, Mandara Mountain is an excellent field; seeing Kakudeśvara Hara there leads to the highest destination.
II.9.87
Brahmeśvara is established amid the fire-offering there. Bowing to him grants knowledge of Brahman.
II.9.88
Seeing and circumambulating Bhāvabhūteśvara releases masses of sins and brings honour in Śiva's world.
II.9.89
Worshipping Śiva on Mandara's holy mountain destroys birth-acquired sins through mere sight.
II.9.90
One gains the fruit of bathing at Prayāga's confluence, indeed a thousandfold foremost fruit—
II.9.91
—at the well before Dharmeśvara. Goddess, bathing and giving rice balls grants fully a thousand cow-gifts' fruit.
II.9.92
Beloved, twenty bow-lengths north of Pāriyātra, behold Kapileśvara in lovely Kapila's hermitage—
II.9.93
—and worship devotedly: one is honoured in Viṣṇu's world. Seeing the sin-and-fear-destroying deity at Pāriyātra removes fear even in dreadful worldly existence.
II.9.94
Eleven bow-lengths east is Piśācamocana Ford, where Kālabhairava dwells.
II.9.95
Worship the trident-bearing Lord there, dark and fair, bull-shaped, situated eastward, beloved.
II.9.96
His excellent liṅga Kapardīśvara should be carefully worshipped and praised with varied hymns.
II.9.97
Beautiful one, south of Vyāghreśvara is an east-facing self-born liṅga, rare even for gods, the excellent place Kṛttivāseśvara; reaching it removes worldly fever.
II.9.98
Released from worldly fear and all sins, people happily attain liberation according to their worth.
II.9.99
Ten bow-lengths northeast of Vyāghreśvara is Kṛttivāseśvara's liṅga and yoni. Repeatedly behold him—
II.9.100
—if one desires saving knowledge, the eternal immortal state. All this should be done when one desires the supreme Self.
II.9.101
Three arrow-shots northeast of Madalācala is renowned Bāṇeśvara, penetrating the seven underworlds.
II.9.102
The Vatsara liṅga is best among all liṅgas; bowing devotedly, a person is liberated within a year [reading provisional].
II.9.103
Northwest of that god is a multicoloured stone, the great liṅga called Garuḍa; worship Garuḍa.
II.9.104
Seeing that deity gives the fruit of a hundred cow-gifts.
II.9.105
“Foremost king of birds, devoted to Vāsudeva's welfare, homage. Bird-lord, give me permission to behold this [deity].”
II.9.106
Bow and recite the mantra; reach Viṣṇu's great sanctuary westward, bathe in auspicious Śiva-water, shave the head, and spend the night contemplating Viṣṇu.
II.9.107
Then at dawn, Queen of the gods, north of Maṇikūṭa is the holy river called Vallabhā, releasing every sin.
II.9.108
Bathing in Vallabhā on the fourteenth lunar day in Māgha or Phālguna destroys great sins, Queen of the gods.
II.9.109
After bathing in Vallabhā and seeing Nīlakaṇṭha, a person is untouched even by sins committed in seven births.
II.9.110
Bathing at that ford and respectfully seeing Mādhava, wherever one lives, one does not re-enter worldly existence.
II.9.111
Every place in the world becomes for that person an abode of the Gaṅgā.
II.9.112
Having seen Varāha's cave, [go to] the great river Naravraja [name uncertain], arising from Aśokakamala and issuing from the boar's staff [Koladaṇḍa].
Note: The river-name and source-name are corrupt or compressed in the OCR; the Hindi repeats their uncertain forms.
II.9.113
Then [there are] Nandinī, Paṅkajā, and excellent Madhumatī rivers, coming from Maṇikūṭa. The fruit there is greater.
II.9.114
“Homage to you of holy water; homage, ocean-going one. Homage, pure one who removes sin; homage, Goddess, bestower of auspiciousness.”
II.9.115
Bathing in Apunarbhava water, enter [the sanctuary of] Lord Gokarṇa. There too is heaven's gate: pronounce this mantra.
II.9.116
Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Rudra, and all the gods have established it. Ascend that exceedingly holy and difficult-to-obtain gate of heaven.
II.9.117
“I give this arghya to Dharma, endowed with the ten characteristics, four-footed and beautiful, dwelling at heaven's gate.”
II.9.118
Having gone there, bow attentively with joined hands. Bathe in Vārāṇasī's pool and Mārkaṇḍeya Lake, behold Kāmeśvara, then bow to the wish-fulfilling tree.
II.9.119
After bathing in Durlabhā, go to Hari's house. Whoever performs worship at this ford according to rule, with restrained senses, rescues twenty-one lineages and goes to Viṣṇu's world.
II.9.120
Great Lady, not far east of Maṇikūṭa is water named Viṣṇupuṣkara, arising from all pilgrimage places.
II.9.121
Beautiful one, bathing there brings abundant fortune. It stands upon the earth in the form of a lotus.
II.9.122
“For the welfare of the mortal world, Puṣkara, remove my sin.” Having bathed with this mantra, repeat the Vāruṇa [mantra] there.
II.9.123
Offer arghya according to rule and ascend the peak. On Maṇikūṭa mountain Viṣṇu bears Hayagrīva's form; [the mountain or image measures] a hundred arm-lengths, and eight thousand [peaks are present: construction uncertain]. Ascend with the mantra, Goddess, and worship with yellow flowers.
II.9.124
Then propitiate the doorkeeper of Viṣṇu's body [or sanctuary].
II.9.125
“Staff-bearing, great-armed destroyer of the Daitya Kāla, doorkeeper, homage to you. Always grant me entrance.”
II.9.126
Contemplate [the guardian/mountain deity] yellow, two-armed, peaceful, adorned with jewelled earrings, bearing discus and arrow, radiant and surrounded on every side—
II.9.127
—endowed with every mark, three-eyed Maṇiśaila. Then, reciting the mantra at his seat, ascend the summit.
Note: The distribution of the epithets between guardian and mountain is compressed; the Sanskrit's sequence is retained.
II.9.128
“Maṇikūṭa, foremost mountain, yellow-coloured, three-eyed: ascending you today, I shall behold the sacred dwelling.”
II.9.129
Ascend Maṇiśaila facing east or north; avoid facing the other directions.
II.9.130
Then go to Vindhyācala and circumambulate it three times. Enter in clean garments, self-controlled and with subdued senses; bathe the god with the root mantra and fragrant waters—
II.9.131
—pleasant waters scented with camphor and fragrant flowers; [use] sandalwood, aguru, lotuses, and whatever desirable offerings there are. Having drawn a basin, perform bathing with its water [the last construction is uncertain].
Note: This unit is misnumbered132 in the Sanskrit OCR, but the Hindi and sequence give131.
II.9.132
[Use] pure, clear, cold water, lukewarm water, hot water, and then the five nectars, in order.
II.9.133
My beloved, bathe for that deity's pleasure with trisita, trigandha, and trijala.
II.9.134
Trisita is proclaimed sandalwood, lotus, and uśīra. [Nitya scent], Malaya sandalwood, and flower-derived scent [form] trigandha.
Note: The ingredients of trigandha are imperfectly recovered; the Hindi also gives an unclear third term. The mixture-names are retained rather than supplied with an unsupported standard recipe.
II.9.135
Water from pilgrimage places, Gaṅgā water, and camphor water [constitute] trijala. Great Lady, bathe with them one after another.
II.9.136
Having bathed as prescribed, perform the bathing observance; then the knower of the rule should perform the arghya bath, Supreme Lady.
II.9.137
Perform bathing with one thousand and eight gold-made jars, with rice and camphor and the other substances mixed in.
Note: The Sanskrit gives aṣṭottarasahasra,1008; the Hindi appears to reduce it to108. The Sanskrit is followed.
II.9.138
If unable, perform a hundred or ten [ablutions], beautiful one. If gold is unavailable, use copper or silver jars, or clay jars according to one's means.
Note: The Sanskrit count-phrase and Hindi differ; both the hundred and ten alternatives visible in the Sanskrit are retained provisionally.
II.9.139
Before bathing, Great Lady, go to a sacred ford, bring its water, and place it in a jar, knowing the procedure.
II.9.140
Give fragrance and flowers, repeat the mantra, transform [the water] into nectar, and display the ritual gesture there.
II.9.141
Place the six limbs [by nyāsa], veil and worship [the vessel]; hold a great festival, place [it] south of the god for bathing, and at bathing's end sprinkle the body, regarding [it] as divine.
Note: The object of placement is not explicit throughout; this is a provisional connected rendering.
II.9.142
Perform cleansing massage daily, on alternate days, or every three days, especially at the prescribed times.
II.9.143
My beloved, carefully use fragrant sesame oil, one pala, half a pala, or half of that; [use] an unguent mixed with turmeric, rice cleansing-paste, and the like—
II.9.144
—having established the Lord of gods, Śaṅkarī, rub the body with bilva leaves, or with apāmārga leaves and roots. Make a brush from a cluster of nala flowers, Great Lady.
II.9.145
Giving a kuśa, chowrie, cow-tail-hair, or especially uśīra brush frees one from every sin.
II.9.146
Giving a cow-hair brush removes every sin; giving a chowrie brush brings unsurpassed fortune.
II.9.147
Never [use] boar bristles or bamboo; avoid the hair of the gavaya and horse.
II.9.148
Do not brush a liṅga, image, or Śālagrāma daily; [do so] after five or seven days—
II.9.149
—or at the month's or fortnight's end, my beautiful one. At a solstice, equinox, on Tuesday, or at a day's diminution—
II.9.150
—on the twelfth lunar day, or during an eclipse, do not perform an oil bath.
II.9.151
Goddess, do not brush the head, face, end of the nose, private part, liṅga, or protuberances [pulaka: technical referent uncertain].
II.9.152
Wipe with cotton cloth, Goddess, or [sandalwood: text uncertain]. With red cloth one becomes leprous or develops pallor disease.
II.9.153
With silk one obtains good sight; with blue or red [cloth] one declines. Then clothe [the god] and [give] the two golden threads—
II.9.154
—a waist-binding and ornaments of many jewels. Whoever, having arranged the clothing, then performs bathing—
II.9.155
—at worship, eating, or especially bathing time, suffers destruction and loss of wealth.
Note: The subject and prohibition in154–155 are not entirely clear; the contradictory sequence is not silently harmonized.
II.9.156
With Malaya sandalwood paste, gopīcandana, paste from bilva wood, or from tulasī wood—
II.9.157
—or padmaka, tamāla, or rocanā, Goddess, make a tilaka: the earlier items are superior in this order.
II.9.158
Queen of the gods, anoint the deity with catuḥsama or dvisama [compounded perfumes]; omit this anointing at a solstice.
Note: These are names of mixtures, not necessarily counts of repeated application as the Hindi gloss suggests.
II.9.159
On a cold night, after bathing, do not anoint again with fragrances, especially on the forehead, and never on the head [or upper body].
II.9.160
Do not place catuḥsama, trisama, or dvisama ointment on the feet, back, or eyes, Queen of the gods.
II.9.161
Anointing the eyes destroys fortune; anointing the face destroys splendour; anointing the hands brings poverty, and anointing the feet, loss of wealth.
II.9.162
Beloved, do not put sandalwood on the liṅga's terminal protuberance. Where possible, give sandalwood on a lotus leaf, bilva leaf, or oleander leaf on the liṅga.
II.9.163
Do not anoint the eyes, Goddess, especially with collyrium. [The substance] prepared from mālatī leaves, heated with sesame oil in an iron vessel and deposited [as soot], is called collyrium.
II.9.164
Beautiful one, whoever worships with the waving of light obtains immortality in this world and the next.
II.9.165
You who are worshipped by gods, whoever does not purify the water becomes foolish, diseased, or quickly comes to destruction.
II.9.166
For a liṅga or image, my beloved, first cleanse with water [and cleanse] the implements; then circumambulate.
II.9.167
Auspicious one, touch the image and me in the form of the liṅga.
II.9.168
What need have the wise who say “Homage to Nārāyaṇa” of many mantras that bring confusion?
II.9.169
“Homage to Nārāyaṇa” is a mantra accomplishing every purpose. Worship with that mantra or with the Puruṣa Sūkta.
II.9.170
Worship with the twelve-syllable seed and Kṛṣṇa's seed, separately and together, forward and backward, and with the many duly prescribed mantras of Viṣṇu. Contemplate the circles of sun, moon, and fire there.
II.9.171
Then contemplate the heart and oṃ in the form of light, seated in the lotus's centre, whose nature is light. Then pronounce the eight-syllable mantra in order.
II.9.172
Placing Keśava and the others first, perform nyāsa of the twelve-syllable [mantra]. [Contemplate] the four-armed, greatly powerful one, radiant as ten million suns—
II.9.173
—then contemplate the eternal yoga, whose form is light. With the mind purified in sequence, invoke [the deity] using the mantras.
II.9.174
“May the boon-giving God come in the forms of Fish, Boar, and Man-lion [and complete my worship: closing words damaged].”
II.9.175
“On Sumeru's footstool is a lotus-conceived seat. Madhusūdana, remain here for the welfare of all beings.”
II.9.176 — Arghya
“To the lord of the lords of the three worlds, to the God of gods, this arghya; homage to Hṛṣīkeśa, Viṣṇu.”
II.9.177 — Water for the feet
“Eternal lotus-navelled God, Viṣṇu, lotus-leaf-eyed Madhusūdana, receive the water [arising from] your own feet.”
II.9.178 — Madhuparka
“Mahādeva, this madhuparka was devised for you by Brahmā and the others. Offered devotedly by me, receive it, supreme Person.”
II.9.179 — Water for sipping
“Drink this auspicious water of Mandākinī and remove inauspiciousness. Receive the sipping-water offered devotedly by me.”
II.9.180 — Bathing
“You are water and earth; you are light and fire. Merely for the world's observance, I bathe you with water.”
II.9.181 — Clothing
“Keśava, these two garments are decorated with many designs, adorned with the sacrificial thread, and woven with golden threads.”
II.9.182 — Anointing
“Keśava, I anoint your body [and limbs] with fragrances. Be pleased to accept the perfumes offered by me.”
II.9.183 — Sacred thread and lamp
“Purified by the lotus-born one with mantras beginning with the Ṛgveda, joined with Sāvitrī's knot, this unbroken sacred thread [is offered]. You are sun, moon, lightning, and fire; you alone are the light of lights. Receive this lamp.”
Note: The printed unit joins a sacred-thread formula and a lamp formula. The Hindi heading treats the whole as lamp-giving, but the Sanskrit thread wording is retained.
II.9.184 — Food offering
“Keśava, food of five kinds endowed with the six tastes is offered by me devotedly as your food offering.”
II.9.185
To the east [place] the God; to the south Saṅkarṣaṇa; to the west [Pradyumna, supplied by Hindi]; and to the northeast Trivikrama.
II.9.186
Place Garuḍa before Vāsudeva and the great mace to the god's right.
II.9.187
Then place the Veda and bow to the god's left; to the right [place] Vasudhā, and [the word/divine teaching: vāṅmaya, uncertain] there.
II.9.188
Establish Śrī to the right and Puṣṭi to the north; before [the god] the forest garland, then Śrīvatsa and Kaustubha. Place the heart and other [mantra limbs] in the four directions.
II.9.189
Then make the placements at the Lord of gods' corners. Worship Īśāna on the seat and his powers outside.
II.9.190
Give three flower-bali offerings to the planets and directional guardians. Thus worship the Lord, Janārdana, in the pavilion.
II.9.191
Whoever sees pavilion-seated Hayānana worshipped in this manner [encounters] no obstacle: [he is] imperishable Viṣṇu.
II.9.192
Whoever worships Keśava even once by this procedure, freed from fear of birth, death, and old age, reaches Viṣṇu's state.
II.9.193
For whoever devotedly and attentively remembers Hayagrīva continually at the two daily twilights, Śvetadvīpa is prepared.
II.9.194
What begins with oṃ, is illuminated by salutation, and contains the essence of all principles is called a mantra.
II.9.195
By this same procedure, Queen of the gods, worship Hayānana in the pavilion and offer fragrance and flowers.
II.9.196
Act in the prescribed order and display the gestures according to their stated sequence.
II.9.197
The knower of mantra should repeat the root mantra twenty-eight, eight, or one hundred and eight times.
II.9.198
For a desired object, do more, beloved: one hundred thousand, ten million, or more. [The gestures are] Śrīvatsa, lotus, conch, mace, and Garuḍa—
II.9.199
—discus, moon, and Śārṅga [bow]: these are proclaimed the eight gestures. Whoever does not know the prescribed mantras for Hari's worship—
II.9.200
—should worship the Supreme Lord with the Hiraṇyagarbha mantra, or worship him with the following mantra.
II.9.201
“Homage to the endless one, to the pure-minded one; homage to [his own form], the thousand-armed Creator, [inclined toward/possessing] a thousand rays. Homage, homage to the horse-faced form.”
Note: Several compounds are imperfect in the OCR. The broad epithets are translated without claiming a verified liturgical transcription.
II.9.202
“Homage to the vast-bodied, pure in action, remover of the whole world's distress, auspicious one; homage to him whose radiance is sharp as sun and fire, the horse-faced form: homage, homage.”
II.9.203
“Beginningless God, mountain-crested Lord, all-pervading lord of beings, Great Lord, accompanied by the winds, lord of all, lord of the world: homage, homage, blessed Great Lord.”
II.9.204
“Lord of waters, Nārāyaṇa, benefactor of the universe, lord of earth, universal sovereign, eye of the universe; whose form is joined with moon and sun, the horse-faced one: homage, homage.”
II.9.205
“Homage to the white one, giver of fearlessness to devotees, [vidyākṣiṇa: epithet uncertain], four-armed, of blood-red limbs, lord of the gods, horse-necked one: homage, homage.”
II.9.206
“[Homage] to Śakra, to him whose sanctuary is on Maṇiparvata, accompanied by the yellow-eyed bird, giver of liberation. I am your devotee, book-holder; uplift the ancestors, God of gods.”
Note: The relation of the opening Śakra epithet to Hayagrīva is compressed; it is retained without harmonization.
II.9.207
“Pure one, adorned with pearls and the like, long-armed, lotus-seated, destroyer of [sin/darkness], destroyer of cities, horse-faced form: homage, homage.”
II.9.208
“Victorious is the horse-faced enemy of Mura, whose hand grants boons and holds a book and white lotus [the full hand-list is imperfectly recovered], of endless forms, auspicious as the moon, giving enjoyment and liberation to gods and humans who bow.”
II.9.209
“[Iḍāsura: uncertain], Hayagrīva, enemy of Mura, Madhusūdana, dweller on Maṇikūṭa, Hayagrīva, homage to you.”
II.9.210
“[Freed from] sins committed through ten million births and ten million ages merely by seeing the horse-faced one, I do not fear the son of the sun.”
Note: The sun's son can denote Yama; the Hindi identifies Śani here. Neither identification is forced into the running translation.
II.9.211
Recite the praise seven times devotedly there, circumambulate three times, then bow to the lotus-formed [deity].
II.9.212
Great Lady, proceed from the south to the north of the god, join the hands, turn around, and bow.
II.9.213
Goddess, make each salutation as a full prostration. Failing to bow after circling is an offence.
II.9.214
Whoever salutes without joining the hands is tormented in the hell of delusion's darkness: there is no doubt.
II.9.215
Whoever bows with an intervening covering, without touching the ground with the head, is cursed by the deities; [the act] is proclaimed fruitless.
II.9.216
Beloved, Great Lady, hear the meritorious fruit of prostrating to the Lord of gods, [according to] the dust upon the body—
II.9.217
—for as many particles of dust [adhere], and as long as they remain, one is honoured in Brahmā's world for so many thousands of years.
Note: The preceding verse's mṛttikā is earth/dust, not the Hindi's apparent mūrti, image. The relation between particle-count and duration is compressed.
II.9.218 — The Goddess said:
“Lord of gods, my beloved, lord of the world: tell me who formerly established Viṣṇu himself on Maṇikūṭa.”
II.9.219 — The Blessed Lord said:
“Greatly fortunate Goddess, hear the Āgama resembling the Veda. I shall tell the ancient account and the origin of the image.”
II.9.220
When the great sacrifice began in the god-built palace, the king was troubled day and night about the image.
II.9.221
“By what means shall I see the God of gods, lord of all, purifier of the world, maker of creation, preservation, and destruction, the supreme Person?”
II.9.222
The king, filled with anxiety and sorrow, did not sleep day or night, nor enjoy pleasures, bathing, or adornment.
II.9.223
“Whether a mountain peak or a tree, whatever upon earth is approved and bears every characteristic suitable for Viṣṇu's image—
II.9.224
“—through these measures alone worship of the gods is cherished. How shall I accomplish it? Lord, command me.”
II.9.225
Having spread kuśa grass, mighty Indradyumna lay down, intent on Hari's meditation, and slept with restrained senses.
II.9.226
As the king slept, Vāsudeva, the world's teacher, discus-bearing, revealed himself to him.
II.9.227
The king saw the God of gods, teacher of the world, bearing conch, discus, mace, and lotus in his hands—
II.9.228
—radiant like the sun at the age's end, resembling blue beryl, seated upon Garuḍa's back, auspicious, with eight arms.
Note: The Sanskrit encodes the arm-count as half of sixteen; the Hindi confirms eight.
II.9.229
“King, ruler of the earth, I am pleased with your sacrifice, gifts, understanding, and devotion. Why do you grieve?
II.9.230
“Steadfast king, I shall tell you the means by which you will establish here the eternal image worshipped by the world.
II.9.231
“At the edge of the ocean's water, adorned with many trees, [there is] a tree that does not tremble though struck by the waves.
II.9.232
“Go alone, king, axe in hand, to the wave-filled [shore]. Truly you will see the tree.
II.9.233
“Reflecting upon this, cut it down without hesitation: the wondrous tree lying westward. Cut it at dawn; give oil and sap, then bring it, king.
Note: The oil-and-sap phrase is uncertain; the Hindi interprets it as an offering or treatment before bringing the wood.
II.9.234
“Make its divine image and abandon bewildering anxiety.” Having spoken thus, great-armed Hari disappeared.
II.9.235
Reflecting upon his dream, the king became greatly astonished. Looking toward that direction, he stood with his mind intent upon it—
II.9.236
—uttering the Vaiṣṇava mantra, identified with that [deity]. When night gave way to dawn, he remained single-minded.
II.9.237
The greatly renowned king bathed duly in the beautiful sea and saw that great tree, exceedingly radiant—
II.9.238
—ending delusion, difficult to climb, holy, fruitless, very tall and massive, lying by the water.
II.9.239
Seeing Viṣṇu's tree, radiant like a blue jewel, without [ordinary] name or species, King Indradyumna rejoiced.
II.9.240
He cut that king of trees with a sharpened axe, dividing it sevenfold and bringing it down upon the ground.
II.9.241
The Lord fashioned the root portion in Oḍra country. Its upper portion, trunk-shaped, [was established] in Kashmir—
II.9.242
—know it as Āditya, formerly established by Rāma. Beloved, the next higher portion was established by Śukra as Śoṇāditya.
II.9.243
Great Lady, the next was established by Guru in stone form; two portions [were established] in Kāmarūpa, and one portion on Malaya mountain.
II.9.244
The upper portion was established by Varuṇa on Maṇikūṭa. To the east [is] Nandīśa; to the northeast is Mādhava called Matsyākṣa—
II.9.245
—made of stone and wood, established by Kubera; and the one named Mahāvarāha, furnished with eighteen arms.
II.9.246
Hayāsya and Mādhava are established on Maṇikūṭa. Goddess, the origin has been told; Pārvatī, now hear the offerings.
Note on241–246: The distribution of the seven wooden portions and the named deities is not perfectly coherent in this printing; the names and directions are retained rather than reassembled into a conjectural list.
II.9.247
[Offer] iṅgudī fruit, bilva, jujube, āmalaka, dates, jackfruit, and palmyra fruit—
II.9.248
—and carefully provide pomegranate and banana, lakuca, madhuka, areca nuts, sweet citron, and karkandhū.
II.9.249
[Offer] radish greens and rājaka greens, [greens] whose fruit is large, and tender shoots.
Note: The last botanical expression is imperfect; the Hindi reads a bud or young shoot.
II.9.250
My beloved, [offer] vāstuka greens, pālaṅga greens, other pleasant bilva [or plant-products: reading uncertain], and tamarind fruit.
II.9.251
Avoid mountain-grown kūṣmāṇḍa, that arising from cāraṇa [uncertain], kadala, citron, rāmaka, pautraka, unseasonal jackfruit, and the other [prohibited produce].
Note: Citron and jackfruit appear both in approved and prohibited lists. Distinctions of season or variety may be intended, but the text does not establish them clearly enough to harmonize silently. The uncertain names are retained.
II.9.252
Śaṅkarī, I shall explain the uses of grains. Pārvatī, listen to the offerings with concentrated mind.
II.9.253
[Use] soma grain, bṛhad grain, red śāli rice, royal grain, ṣaṣṭika, and devavallabha.
II.9.254
Avoid chickpeas and kodrava, my beautiful one; [also] alkali, black milk [uncertain ingredient], and salt arising from earth—
II.9.255
—and salt originating in the east or north. I shall explain wild and village-dwelling animals.
Note: The salt classification is retained without a speculative modern mineral identification.
II.9.256
Those suitable for use [include] the cow's nectar-like milk, Goddess; deer, fish, goat, śālana, and hare—
II.9.257
—give offerings with these to please Viṣṇu. Avoid buffalo flesh, milk, curds, and ghee.
Note: The last three dairy products are understood as buffalo-derived, following the Hindi gloss and contrast with cow's milk.
II.9.258
My beloved, I shall explain the birds that may be used: hārīta, peacock, nāyaka, vartaka—
II.9.259
—kapila, cāṣa, crow, domestic fowl, śira [name uncertain], wild fowl, śarāru, and pigeon—
II.9.260
—bilvaka, kulika, red-tailed [bird], ṭiṭṭibha, and black fish-eater: these are distinguished among birds.
Note: The source's bird-names are retained where identification is uncertain; this is a historical offering-list, not contemporary wildlife guidance.
II.9.261
Avoid inedible flesh, flesh of five-nailed animals, citra fish, and rohita flesh.
II.9.262
Great Lady, mahāśālya, rājīva, and siṃhatuka: these fish are to be given; avoid vimālī.
Note: The Hindi offers conflicting identifications, including amphibians or birds. The Sanskrit category and uncertain names are preserved provisionally.
II.9.263
[Also avoid] web-footed [creatures], saṅkula, vārāhaka, gemunta greens, oil-cake, red-footed [creatures/plants], and kesara—
II.9.264
—śobhāñjana, red śelu, kaumuka, tinduka, pūtika, and kānaka: the excellent practitioner should avoid them.
Note: The long botanical and zoological list is damaged in places. Uncertain names are retained rather than given confident identifications or medicinal substitutes.
II.9.265
A yogin devoted to meditation should practise the mantra as prescribed, avoiding all prohibited food in the meditation and practice of every deity.
II.9.266
Eating sacrificial food, pure and expert in mantra and tantra, repeat the Vidyā day and night with the inner self absorbed in it.
II.9.267
Such a person becomes Kālikā's child, fearless everywhere. Goddess, this supreme secret has been revealed out of affection for you.

COLOPHON
Thus ends the final chapter of the Second Part of the illustrious Yoginī Tantra, foremost among Tantras, in the dialogue of the Goddess and Īśvara, traditionally described as comprising twenty-four thousand verses, with the Hindi commentary by Paṇḍit Kanhaiyalal Mishra of Moradabad, of the Kānyakubja lineage and son of the renowned learned Miśra Sukhānanda [the ornamental genealogy is imperfectly recovered].
Note: The printed concluding formula calls this “the eighth chapter,” although it follows the preceding eighth chapter and occupies the ninth position in the supplied Second Part. This file uses Chapter9 to preserve the sequential arrangement and records the printing discrepancy rather than silently concealing it.

EDITORIAL STATUS
All267 numbered units have English entries. Many botanical names, some ritual formulas, and several damaged compounds remain provisional as flagged. The text's instructions and supernatural consequences are translated as historical claims, not verified effects. This is a reading translation, not an independently reviewed critical edition.

============================================================================

YOGINĪ TANTRA — TRANSLATOR'S CLOSING VERSES
Source: Mishra edition, printed pages515–516, PDF533–534. These verses belong to the Hindi translator's end matter, not to the dialogue of the main Tantra.

Closing1
In the bright fortnight of Kārttika, on the thirteenth lunar day, a Thursday, in the year of King Vikrama numbered1959—

Closing2
—I, Kanhaiyalal Mishra, composed a vernacular commentary on the Tantra named Yoginī, for the delight of tantric practitioners.

Closing3
Human understanding is plainly limited in its scope. Therefore any error occurring here should be forgiven by sympathetic readers.

Closing4
This exceedingly rare Tantra, endowed with profound meanings, [has been entrusted] to the illustrious benefactor Kṣemarāja—

Closing5
—for printing and sale at a price. May the Lord's favour always attend him; may there always be auspiciousness.

Dedication
May this be offered to the illustrious Mother of the universe.
This book is concluded.

Editorial note: The Vikrama1959 date is the translator's original colophon date, not the1984 reprint date. The supplied text's ornamental titles and punctuation are imperfectly recovered. The following publisher addresses and advertisements are Hindi publishing matter and are not part of the Sanskrit main work.
